Uniform in time weak propagation of chaos on the torus
[

Abstract
We address the long time behaviour of weakly interacting diffusive particle systems on the d d -dimensional torus. Our main result is to show that, under certain mild regularity conditions, the weak error between the empirical distribution of the particle system and the limiting theoretical law (governed by a Fokker-Planck equation) is of the order 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / N ) {\mathcal{O}}(1/N) , uniform in time on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) , where N N is the number of particles in the interacting diffusion. This comprises Fokker-Planck equations with a globally attracting invariant measure for which the linearisation at the invariant measure enjoys appropriate ergodic properties. Our approach relies on a systematic analysis of the long-time behaviour of the derivatives of the semigroup generated by the Fokker-Planck equation. This strategy is flexible enough to cover a wider broad of situations, including the super-critical Kuramoto model, for which the corresponding Fokker-Planck equation has several invariant measures.

, 60K35
; secondary 35Q84
, 82C31

keywords

Uniform in time propagation of chaos
, Weakly interacting particle system
, Weak error
, McKean Vlasov equation
, Fokker-Planck equation

and B]Alvin Tse

1
Introduction

In this paper, we are concerned with
the large size and the large time behaviour of
a weakly interacting particle system
with toroidal data. Denoting by
N
the number of particles, the system has the following generic form

(1.1)

\begin{cases}Y^{i,N}_{t}=\eta^{i}+\int_{0}^{t}b\big(Y^{i,N}_{s},\mu^{N}_{s}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s+W^{i}_{t},\quad i\in\{1,\cdots,N\},\quad t\geq 0,\\
\mu^{N}_{s}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{Y^{i,N}_{s}},\end{cases}

where
b
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued function defined on
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

\mathbb{T}^{d}:={\mathbb{R}}^{d}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
denoting the
d
-dimensional torus and
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
the space of probability measures on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, which we equip (unless specified differently) with the
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
-Wasserstein distance

(1.2)

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(\mu,\nu)=\inf_{\pi}\biggl\{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}d_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(x,y)\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)\biggr\},

the infimum being over all the probability measures
\pi
on the product space

\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
that have
\mu
and
\nu
as respective marginal measures.
In (
1.1
),
W^{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,N
, are independent
d
-dimensional Brownian motions and
\eta^{i},

i=1,\cdots,N
, are
N

\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variables, with the two tuples

(\eta^{1},\cdots,\eta^{N})
and
(W^{1},\cdots,W^{N})
being independent.
Most of the time, the random variables
\eta^{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,N
,
are also assumed to be independent and identically distributed (I.I.D.) with a common law
{\mu_{\text{init}}}
, but this might not be the case in some of our results (in those cases we emphasise it very clearly). In physical applications, this type of processes arises when we consider interacting particle systems with periodic boundary conditions. (See, for example,
[
74
,
29
,
52
,
66
]
.)

1.1
State of the art

It is well-known that as the population size
N
grows to infinity, (
1.1
), when subjected to I.I.D. initial conditions
\eta^{1},\cdots,\eta^{N}
, behaves like the following McKean-Vlasov SDE

(1.3)

X_{t}=\eta+\int_{0}^{t}b\bigl(X_{s},{\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{s})}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s+W_{t},\quad t\geq 0\ ;\quad{\mathcal{L}}{{(\eta)}}:=\text{Law}(\eta)={\mu_{\text{init}}},

where
(\eta,W)
is a copy of
(\eta^{1},W^{1})
. Existence and uniqueness of a solution to
both (
1.1
) and (
1.3
)
is known if
b
is globally bounded and merely Lipschitz continuous in the measure argument with respect to the total variation distance (see
[
49
,
53
,
62
]
and the references therein).
Moreover, the flow of marginal laws
(m(t\,;{\mu_{\text{init}}}):={\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t})}})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies (at least in a distributional sense) the
nonlinear
Fokker-Planck equation:

(1.4)

\partial_{t}m(t\,;\mu)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta m(t\,;\mu)-\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl[m(t\,;\mu)b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigr],\quad t\geq 0\ ;\quad m(0\,;\mu)=\mu.

Asymptotically, any finite subset
of particles becomes independent of each other. This phenomenon is known as
propagation of chaos
. Precisely,
on any finite time interval
[0,T]
and, for any fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(Y^{1,N},\ldots,Y^{k,N})\implies(X^{1},\ldots,X^{k})
, as
N\to\infty,

where
\{X^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d. copies of (
1.3
) and ‘
\implies
’ denotes weak convergence on the space
C([0,T],(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{k})
. The main reference in this direction is
[
69
]
, where
propagation of chaos is proved by means of a coupling argument.
The proof works for a jointly Lipschitz drift
b
(w.r.t.
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
in the measure argument)
and yields a quantitative convergence estimate which we describe in the next paragraph. Another result from
[
69
]
asserts that propagation of chaos is equivalent to weak convergence of the measure-valued random variables
(\mu^{N}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
to the limiting laws
(\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
. This paves the way for another approach
consisting in proving tightness of
(\pi^{N}_{t}:={\mathcal{L}}{{(\mu^{N}_{t})}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})))_{0\leq t\leq T}
, see
[
43
,
59
,
69
]
.

Errors in finite time.

The quantitative analysis of propagation of chaos can be carried out in various ways, whether the geometry of the state space is Euclidean or toroidal (it is only when it comes to the long time behaviour of
(
1.1
)
and
(
1.3
)
that compactness of the torus makes a substantial difference.)
For instance, for a given metric on the space of probability measures, one may simply estimate the distance between the marginal law of
Y^{1,N}_{t}
and the measure

{\mathcal{L}}(X_{t})
, for a fixed
t
in some finite interval
[0,T]
. More generally, one may compare the joint law of the
k
first particles
(Y^{1,N}_{t},\cdots,Y_{t}^{k,N})
with the product measure

{\mathcal{L}}(X_{t})^{\otimes k}
. Another approach is to compare the empirical measure
\mu^{N}_{t}
with

{\mathcal{L}}(X_{t})
. For sure, one may also address the supremum of any of these distances over
t\in[0,T]
, which is very similar to what is done in the analysis of the strong error
for discretisation schemes of SDEs.
For instance, in the case where
b
depends on the measure component linearly, i.e., is of the form
b(x,\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}B(x,y)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}y),
with
B
being Lipschitz continuous in both variables, it follows from a simple calculation
(
[
69
]
) that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(\mathcal{L}(Y^{1,N}_{t}),\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))={\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1/2})
,
where here and throughout

{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
stands for the big
{\mathcal{O}}
Landau notation.
This result has been improved in several contributions. Notably,
the
1
-Wasserstein distance between
{\mathcal{L}}(Y_{t}^{1,N},\cdots,Y_{t}^{k,N})

and

{\mathcal{L}}(X_{t})^{\otimes k}

has been shown to be
{\mathcal{O}}((k/N)^{2})

in the recent work
[
54
]
,
the proof relying on the analysis of the relative entropy between both laws and on the so-called BBGKY hierarchy.
With similar tools,
quantitative estimates are established for models with singular interactions
in
[
48
]
. Models with singular interactions have been also treated by means of the modulated energy method, in which the metric used for studying the convergence is adapted to the form
of the interactions, see for instance
[
68
]
. We refer to
[
11
]
for combinations of relative entropy and modulated energy methods.
When
b
has a general (but regular) measure dependence, the rate of convergence deteriorates with the dimension
d
, since it is then needed to estimate the Wasserstein distance between the empirical law of I.I.D. samples and the limiting measure. This follows from results such as
[
36
]
or
[
41
]
in which the dimension explicitly shows up. Dimension-free rates may be retrieved in this more general setting by requiring a strong form of smoothness of the drift
b
with respect to the measure argument
(see
[
34
, Lemma 5.10]

and
[
70
]
).

The rate of convergence can be also addressed by testing the statistical distribution of the empirical measure against real-valued functions
defined on
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, i.e., by estimating quantities of the form

(1.5)

\Big|\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})]-\Phi\bigl({\mathcal{L}}(X_{t}))\bigr)\Big|,

where
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is a test functional chosen within a suitable class.
For instance,
\Phi(\cdot)={\mathcal{W}}_{2}(\cdot,{\mathcal{L}}(X_{t}))
may be one such test functional, but more regularity may be required on
\Phi
to obtain relevant bounds.
Accordingly,
(
1.5
) should be understood as a weak error for the law of
\bar{\mu}^{N}_{t}
when acting on a given class of test functionals
\Phi
.
This direction of research has been introduced in independent works
[
5
,
51
,
60
,
61
]
, for various forms of test functionals
\Phi
. Among others,
\Phi
is a linear function in

[
5
]
, i.e.
\Phi(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}F(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
for some function
F:\mathbb{T}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
;
\Phi
is a polynomial function in

[
60
,
61
]
, i.e. a product of linear functions;
and,
\Phi
is a quite general nonlinear function in

[
51
]
. Under appropriate smoothness conditions on the test functional and on the coefficients of (
1.1
),
this gives a rate of convergence of
{\mathcal{O}}(1/N)
, plus the error due to the approximation of the functional of the initial law (as for the latter, see
[
61
, Lem. 4.6]
for a dimension-dependent estimate and
[
25
, Th. 2.11]
for an
{\mathcal{O}}(1/N)
bound). The key idea of the analysis (highlighted in

[
51
, Th. 9.2.1]

and

[
61
, Th. 6.1]
) is to work with a semigroup that acts on the space of functions of measures (expounded in the next section). A similar idea has been used in

[
16
]
in order to study the convergence problem for mean field games, up to the difference that the equation for the semigroup then becomes a nonlinear equation.
Also, another
recent work
[
25
]
provides an extension of
[
5
,
51
,
61
]
in the form
of a weak error expansion
and the companion works

[
26
,
27
]
address cases with coefficients having lower Hölder regularity in the spatial variable.

Long time analysis.

Propagation of chaos is said to be uniform whenever the quantitative estimates (of propagation of chaos) are uniform in time. This problem is hence more challenging as it involves two large parameters,
N
and
t
. It is thus related to the long time behaviour of the McKean-Vlasov and non-linear Fokker-Planck equations
(
1.3
) and (
1.4
) themselves.

Actually, the ergodic analysis of McKean-Vlasov and corresponding nonlinear Fokker-Planck equations has been an intense topic of research on its own for more than twenty years. In the earlier papers

[
3
,
4
,
6
,
10
,
22
,
21
]
, convergence towards a (unique) invariant measure has been mostly studied for drifts of the form
b(x,\mu)=-\nabla V(x)-\int\nabla W(x-y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)
, with confinement and interaction potentials

V
and
W
satisfying suitable convexity properties,
W
being symmetric.
In this framework,
a key conceptual feature is that the Fokker-Planck equation (
1.4
) can be regarded as a gradient flow on the space of probability measures.
However, as demonstrated
in

[
47
]
,
uniqueness of the invariant measure may be easily lost under a small modification of the shape of the potentials, which obviously raises challenging questions about the long-time behaviour of the Fokker-Planck equation. In fact, regardless of the precise form of the drift
b
, a possible strategy to force uniqueness and in turn to get convergence towards the hence unique invariant measure is to assume that the mean field interaction is small enough (see for instance

[
9
,
14
,
40
]
).
When convergence towards a unique invariant measure is no longer true, a case-by-case stability analysis of all the existing stationary solutions may be carried out, depending on the shape of the dynamics.
We refer for instance to

[
7
,
44
]

for results on the super-critical (toroïdal) Kuramoto model, which we revisit in Section

4
. More examples may be found in
[
20
,
33
,
73
]
.

Uniform in time propagation of chaos
is even more challenging. To wit, it may fail even in cases where there exists a globally attracting invariant measure to the Fokker-Planck equation (
1.4
). In the Euclidean setting,
a well-known example by now may be found in
[
58
]
. Therein, the center of mass of the
N
particles in (
1.1
) is shown to behave (for a suitable choice of
b
) like a Brownian motion of intensity of
1/\sqrt{N}
, which becomes macroscopic in size for time

t
larger than
N
.
That said, several positive results have already been proven under relevant conditions.
For instance, the same example as in

[
58
]
, but with an additional confining convex potential,
is shown to satisfy

\sup_{t\geq 0}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(\mathcal{L}(Y^{1,N}_{t}),\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))={\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1/2})
, see
the earlier work

[
57
]

together with the recent contribution

[
55
]
in which the bound is improved into
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1})
.
In this example,
b
takes the aforementioned special form

b(x,\mu)=-\nabla V(x)-\int\nabla W(x-y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)
,
with
V
and
W
satisfying strong convexity conditions.
In
[
46
,
64
]
, the potential
W
is even allowed to be singular, with the uniform in time estimates being possibly established under weaker distances and with weaker rates.
In

[
28
]
, the gradient flow structure is addressed in a more systematic manner, but assuming the potential on top of the gradient to be convex with respect to the measure argument in a functional sense.
Outside the convex regime, quantitative bounds may be obtained under relevant conditions that force
W
to be small enough, see
[
39
,
67
]
.
From a different perspective, close to
[
48
]
,
the authors of

[
45
]

have addressed on the torus the case when
\nabla W
is replaced by a possibly singular divergence free vector field.
Lastly,
for uniform propagation of chaos when
b
is a general drift with a small enough McKean-Vlasov dependence,
we refer again to

[
55
]
,
in which
the authors extend

[
54
]

and show
that

\sup_{t\geq 0}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(\mathcal{L}(Y^{1,N}_{t},\cdots,Y_{t}^{k,N}),\mathcal{L}(X_{t})^{\otimes k})
is
{\mathcal{O}}((k/N)^{2})
,
and to

[
2
]
, in which
the error
(
1.5
)
is shown to be
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1}+N^{-1/d}\exp(-\lambda t))
, for some
\lambda>0
.

1.2
Our contribution.

Our contribution here is to address the weak error
(
1.5
)
when
\Phi
is a general (smooth enough) test functional and
to provide an (almost)
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1})
bound for it under suitable generic assumptions on the long time behaviour of
(
1.5
), with a particular emphasis on the role of the invariant measures to (
1.5
).

The first main statement in this regard is Theorem

3.1

below, which asserts among others that such a bound holds true for a bounded drift
b
that is sufficiently smooth in the measure argument
under the following two requirements:
(a)
the equation
(
1.5
) has a (hence unique) globally attracting invariant measure
\nu_{\infty}
;
(b)
the linearised
version of
(
1.5
)
at

\nu_{\infty}

has ergodic properties, which are spelled out in the introduction of
Section

3
, see

(
Erg
)
.
We refer (for a tiny example) to

[
31
,
38
,
60
,
61
]

for
earlier (and related) uses
of the linearised version of the nonlinear Fokker-Planck equation.
We provide several examples of applications:
(i)
when
b
is a general function with a small enough
dependence with respect to the measure argument
\mu
;
(ii)

when

b
derives from an
H
-stable (periodic symmetric) potential, see
[
65
]
, i.e., all the Fourier coefficients of
W
are non-negative;

(iii)

when
b
is divergence-free in
x
.
Obviously,
similar cases have been already addressed, although in somewhat different contexts, in the aforementioned references
(see
[
39
,
2
]

for
(i)
,

[
28
]

for
(ii)
, which is somehow the analogue of the convex case treated therein but on the torus,

[
45
]

for
(iii)
).
While this may seem a fair criticism of our work, it is interesting to note that our statement covers all three cases.

What is more, our tools allow us to take the analysis a step further and to obtain local results under locally attracting properties of the invariant measures.
For instance, we obtain metastability bounds (i.e., quantitative propagation of chaos over time interval of any polynomial length in
N
), see
again
Theorem
3.1
(together with
Theorem
3.19
for a refined version), when

\nu_{\infty}
in
the former condition
(a)
is
simply assumed to be an
invariant measure, possibly not globally attracting (in fact, it becomes locally attracting under
(b)
).
Moreover, in Theorem
4.3
, we provide a quantitative in time estimate for one specific model, namely the super-critical Kuramoto model, with an infinite set of invariant measures.
It corresponds to
b(x,\mu)=-\int\nabla W(x-y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)
with

W(x)=-2\pi\kappa\cos(2\pi x)
for
\kappa>1
.
This case is challenging and does not fit any of the aforementioned situations
(i)
,
(ii)
or
(iii)
.
The invariant measures are the Lebesgue measure, which is unstable, and a collection of non-trivial measures obtained by rotating a common density profile on the torus, see

[
7
,
44
]
. Uniform propagation of chaos then fails, see

[
8
]
and the best result that has been proven so far is due to
[
30
]
: it says that, with high probability, the empirical distribution of
(
1.1
) stays close to the collection of non-trivial invariant
measures up to times that are subexponential in
N
.
Here, we prove that the weak error
(
1.5
)
is of order
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1})
, uniformly in time, for functionals
\Phi
that are rotation invariant (see Definition
4.2
) and for initial distributions
\mu_{\text{init}}
that are at a positive distance from the Lebesgue measure. This result is new and, although it does not directly follow from our main statement Theorem
3.1
, it follows from the same approach.

In all our results, the function
\Phi
is a sufficiently smooth ‘nonlinear’ functional on
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. To make it clear,
\Phi
has two ‘linear functional’ (or ‘flat’) derivatives with respect to the measure argument (see Section
2
for the details) that are Hölder continuous in the spatial variables (the derivatives of
\Phi
at a measure
\mu
are functions on the torus; Hölder continuity is thus interpreted in terms of the standard distance on the torus).
When specialising to a linear function
\Phi(\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}F(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
, this says that

F
has to be merely Hölder continuous. In contrast,
F
is required to be twice differentiable (with bounded derivatives) in

[
2
]
. Our strategy of allowing such weaker conditions combines two main ingredients. First, we exploit in a systematic manner the smoothing effect of the Laplace operator in (
1.4
) (using Schauder’s estimates, very like in the finite time analysis carried out in
[
26
,
27
]
); Second,
we use a mollification method for
\Phi
, introduced in the first arXiv version
[
35
]
of this work and then studied in a systematic manner in
[
23
]
, that permits to work with a smoother
\Phi
, provided that the resulting bounds for the weak error only depend on the regularity properties of the unmollified
\Phi
.
In the end, a typical instance of nonlinear function is:

\Phi(\mu)=\|\mu-\mu_{0}\|^{2}_{-(d/2+\varepsilon),2},\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

for some
\varepsilon>0
, where

\mu_{0}
is a fixed ‘target’ probability measure on
\mathbb{T}^{d}

and
\|\cdot\|_{-(d/2+\varepsilon),2}
is the norm on the dual of the standard Sobolev space
H^{d/2+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(see
Proposition
2.3
). We find it very useful, especially when
\mu_{0}
is chosen
as
m(t\,;\mu)
in (
1.4
) or as
m(\infty\,;\mu):=\lim_{t\nearrow\infty}m(t\,;\mu)
. In particular, this permits to retrieve a dimension-free bound, at the price of working with a weaker distance than
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
.
We refer to

[
64
]
for a similar occurrence of this distance in related questions and
to
[
32
]
for further mollification arguments that would permit to retrieve dimension-dependent rates in
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
-distance.

Strategy of proof.

Our approach is heavily based on the so-called
master equation
satisfied by the semigroup generated by the McKean-Vlasov equation (
1.3
). Equivalently, the latter is the semigroup
(\textsf{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
generated by the (deterministic) Fokker-Planck equation (
1.4
), whose action on a (bounded and measurable) test functional
\Phi:{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

reads:

(1.6)

{\sf P}_{t}\Phi:{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\ni\mu\mapsto\Phi\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr),\quad t\geq 0,

where
(m(t\,;\mu))_{t\geq 0}
solves (
1.4
).

A key fact is that
(\textsf{P}_{t}\Phi)_{t\geq 0}
is a classical solution of the aforementioned master equation
whenever
\Phi
is smooth enough. In PDE theory, the trajectories
(m(t\,;\mu))_{t\geq 0}
should be regarded as the characteristics of the master equation. A systematic analysis of the smoothness of

(\textsf{P}_{t}\Phi)_{t\geq 0}
is provided in
[
13
,
27
,
72
]
(see also

[
16
]
for a similar study in a nonlinear setting). The analysis of the weak error is then carried out in two steps. The first one is to ‘test’
(\textsf{P}_{t}\Phi)_{t\geq 0}
onto the empirical distribution of
the
N
-particle system; the resulting bound is shown to be
{\mathcal{O}}(1/N)
, with the leading constant
in the symbol
{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
depending on the bounds for the derivatives of order 1 and 2
of
(\textsf{P}_{t}\Phi)_{t\geq 0}
. The second step is to provide uniform-in-time bounds for the derivatives of
(\textsf{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, which is the main challenge in the proof.

The derivatives of
(\textsf{P}_{t}\Phi)_{t\geq 0}
may be explicitly computed by linearising the Fokker-Planck equation (
1.4
). This is a well-known fact in PDE theory: The derivatives of the solution of a transport equation
can be expressed in terms of the derivatives of the corresponding characteristics with respect to the initial point.
In our setting, the derivatives of the characteristics are indeed obtained by linearising the Fokker-Planck equation with respect to the measure argument. See for instance
the results exposed in
Subsection
3.1
, which are mostly borrowed from

[
72
]
.
Thus, not only does the long-time behaviour of the
Fokker-Planck equation matter in our analysis, but also
the long-time asymptotics of the linearised Fokker-Planck equation are important, which explains the formulation of our main
Theorem

3.1
.

Of course, this approach is reminiscent of the approach initiated in

[
60
,
61
]
. In particular, the proof of
property (A4) in both papers is also based upon the linearised version of
(
1.4
), but computed along any solutions. Here, Theorem

3.1
is just formulated in terms of the properties of the linearised version of
(
1.4
)
at ‘the’ (or ‘an’ when uniqueness does not hold) invariant measure. In this respect, it is fair to say that

[
60
, Sec. 6]
contains a similar use to ours of the existence
of a globally attracting invariant measure. One of our contribution is to make clear the
role of the latter and, in turn, to extend the analysis to locally attracting invariant measures. Generally speaking,
the linearisation is of a richer structure (and thus of an easier study) at the invariant measure and this plays a key role in the examples that are treated below. For instance, our analysis of Kuramoto’s model (as stated in Theorem

4.3
, which is clearly out of reach of the results obtained in

[
60
,
61
]
) mostly relies on the properties of the linearised equations at each of the non-trivial invariant measures. From a different perspective,
the metastability properties provided by
Theorems
3.1

and

3.19

(which are no longer addressed in

[
60
,
61
]
)
are also stated in terms of
the linearised equation at a stable equilibrium, without any further global constraints.
In the same vein, we believe that the application of Theorem

3.1

to gradient and conservative systems provided
in Subsection

3.6
is also greatly simplified by the fact the linearised equation is just computed at the invariant measure.
On another matter, it is worth mentioning that the constraints we impose on the drift
b
are weaker than those required in

[
61
]
. This requires an additional substantial effort to handle the small time singularities of the
solutions to the various linearised equations under study.
For instance, we here allow the drift to be merely bounded in the spatial position: to the best of our knowledge (and apart from the
case by case singular examples
treated in

[
45
,
46
,
64
]
), this is
something new.
By comparison, the drift is assumed to be at least Hölder continuous in

[
26
,
27
]
, in which propagation of chaos is just addressed
in finite time.

Further prospects.
We are confident that similar results could be obtained in other cases, including cases with a non-constant diffusion coefficient or defined in the Euclidean setting, provided that a suitable form of confining drift is added to the dynamics.

1.3
Organisation and notations

We start the paper by reviewing the theory of differential calculus in Wasserstein spaces and the master equation in Section
2
. We also provide the key semi-group expansion that serves as bounding the weak error (see Lemma

2.2
). Subsequently, Section
3
is dedicated to the statement and the proof of
Theorem
3.1
, in which
we derive uniform in time estimates for
(
1.5
)
from general ‘ergodic’ properties
of
(
1.4
)
and its linearised version. In Section
4
, we explore the special case related to the Kuramoto model in which the associated Fokker-Planck equation does not have a unique invariant measure (see Theorem

4.3
).

Useful Notations.

The scalar product between two vectors
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
a\cdot b
. For each
i\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
e_{i}
denotes the vector with
1
in the
i
th component and 0 elsewhere. For any vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
x_{i}
denotes the
i
th component of
x
. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
a\vee b
denotes
\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b
denotes
\min\{a,b\}
.
The set
{\mathbb{N}}
is the set of integers, including
\{0\}
.
For any real
s
, we call
\lfloor s\rfloor
the floor part of
s
.
For two probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we call

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\textrm{\rm TV}}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}f(x)d(\mu-\nu)
the total variation distance between
\mu
and
\nu
, where

\|f\|_{\infty}

is the essential sup norm of

f:\mathbb{T}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
\overline{z}
is the complex conjugate of
z
. Moreover,
{\mathrm{i}}
is the complex number such that
{\mathrm{i}}^{2}=-1
.

2
Main method of proof in this paper

In this section, we introduce the main ingredients needed in our approach.
The space

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is equipped with the
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
distance, where we recall that
convergence for
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is equivalent to weak convergence on
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Moreover, the vector field
b
is assumed to be at least bounded and measurable on

\mathbb{T}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. As a result,
existence and uniqueness hold for (
1.3
)
and the marginal law of the solution depends on the initial condition
only through the statistical distribution of the latter.
Then, we call
(m(t\,;\mu))_{t\geq 0}
the evolving law of the process
X
in (
1.3
) when starting at law
\mu
; it solves
(
1.4
) in a distributional sense.

2.1
Master equation

Our framework of analysis relies on
the so-called master equation for the semigroup
(\textsf{P}_{t}\Phi)_{t\geq 0}

defined in
(
1.6
). This requires
a notion of differentiation w.r.t. measures in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, called
linear functional derivatives,
see
[
16
,
18
,
25
,
34
,
63
]
. A function

\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is said to
have directional derivatives at some
m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
if
there exists a bounded (measurable) function

\frac{\delta\mathcal{V}}{\delta m}(m,\cdot):\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

such that, for any
m^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}{\mathcal{V}}\bigl(\epsilon m^{\prime}+(1-\epsilon)m\bigr)=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}} \frac{\delta\mathcal{V}}{\delta m}(m,y)\bigl(m^{\prime}-m\bigr)(\mathrm{d}y).

The function
{\mathcal{V}}
is said to be
continuously differentiable if
it has directional derivatives at any
m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
and the
resulting function

\frac{\delta\mathcal{V}}{\delta m}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

is (jointly) continuous, in which case it satisfies,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(m^{\prime})-\mathcal{V}(m)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{V}}{\delta m}\bigl((1-s)m+sm^{\prime},y\bigr)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

The function

\frac{\delta\mathcal{V}}{\delta m}

is said to be the
linear functional derivative
of
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
.
It is uniquely defined up to an additive constant, which is fixed by the convention

(2.2)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{V}}{\delta m}(m,y)m(\mathrm{d}y)=0.

If
[\delta\mathcal{V}/\delta m](m,\cdot)
is differentiable (w.r.t
y
), then
we let
\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{V}(m,y)=\partial_{y}[\delta\mathcal{V}/\delta m](m,y)
.
By induction, we then introduce higher-order derivatives:
for any integer
p\geq 2
,
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
y\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{p-1}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{p-1}\mathcal{V}}{\delta m^{p-1}}(m^{\prime},y)-\frac{\delta^{p-1}\mathcal{V}}{\delta m^{p-1}}(m,y)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\frac{\delta^{p}\mathcal{V}}{\delta m^{p}}\bigl((1-s)m+sm^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\bigr)\,(m^{\prime}-m)(\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}s,

provided that the
(p-1)
-th order derivative is well defined.
In order to ensure uniqueness, they are required to satisfy

(2.3)

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\frac{\delta^{p}\mathcal{V}}{\delta m^{p}}(m,y_{1},\ldots,y_{p})\,m(\mathrm{d}y_{p})=0,\quad{y_{1},\cdots,y_{p-1}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d-1}.}

There is a related notion called
Wasserstein derivative
\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{V}
(
[
1
,
13
,
15
,
18
]
).
In short, it is the gradient field of the linear functional derivative, i.e.,

\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{V}(m,y)=\partial_{y}[\delta V/\delta m](m,y)
.
Propositions 5.48 and 5.51
in

[
18
]
serve as a dictionary to enable us to pass from one to the other.

For a bounded measurable function
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
we let (see (
1.6
)):

(2.4)

\mathcal{U}(t,\mu):=\textsf{P}_{t}\Phi(\mu)=\Phi\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr),\quad t\geq 0,\ \mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

It is proven in Theorem 7.2 of
[
13
]
(see also Theorem 3.5 in
[
27
]
) that
\mathcal{U}
satisfies the
master equation

(2.5)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}{\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{x_{i}}\frac{\delta{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m}(t,\mu,x)b_{i}(x,\mu)+\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial^{2}_{x_{i}x_{i}}\frac{\delta{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m}(t,\mu,x)\biggr]\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x),\end{split}

for
(t,\mu)\in[0,+\infty)\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,
with

{\mathcal{U}}(0,\mu)=\Phi(\mu)
as boundary condition,
provided that
b
and
\Phi

are smooth enough.
While we could state properly the conditions required
in
[
13
]
, we feel useless to do so at this stage.
We formulate below stronger sets of conditions on
b
and
\Phi
which subsume the conditions needed in

[
13
]
.
In fact,
the equation
in
[
13
, Theorem 7.2]

is set in the Euclidean setting, which requires, when translated in the periodic setting, to check that the various derivatives are periodic functions in
y
. We refer to
the appendix in
[
16
]
.In this respect, it is worth noticing that in

[
13
, Theorem 7.2]
,
the measure argument
\mu
in (
2.5
) is required to have a finite second moment. In our setting, such a restriction
no longer exists since the torus is compact.
Very importantly, the result
of

[
13
, Theorem 7.2]

says that:

Proposition 2.1
.

If
b
and
\Phi
are smooth enough then, for every
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, the derivatives

\partial_{t}{\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu),\quad\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\frac{\delta{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m}(t,\mu,y_{1}),\quad\partial_{(y_{2})_{j}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,y_{1},y_{2}),\quad\partial_{(y_{1})_{j}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\frac{\delta{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m}(t,\mu,y_{1})

exist
and
are
globally Lipschitz continuous w.r.t.
(\mu,y_{1},y_{2})
for the Euclidean and
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
norms, uniformly in time
t
in compact subsets, and are continuous in time (and hence jointly continuous).

In fact,

[
13
, Theorem 7.2]

is stated for the
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
-distance.
The adaptation to the
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
-distance may be found in

[
19
, Theorem 5.10]
, noticing that the equation for
{\mathcal{U}}
has a more general nonlinear form. In particular, the restriction that
T
has to be small enough (in the latter statement) can be easily removed in our linear setting. Also, our function
{\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu)
corresponds
in the notation of
[
19
]
to
\int{\mathcal{U}}(t,x,\mu)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
.

2.2
Expansion along the particle system

The starting point of our analysis is to make use of the identity

\Phi(\mu)={\mathcal{U}}(0,\mu)
, as given by
the initial condition of (
2.4
).
Recalling the notation

\mu^{N}_{t}
from
(
1.1
), this gives the decomposition

(2.6)

\begin{split}\Phi(\mu^{N}_{{t}})-\Phi(\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))&=\big(\mathcal{U}(t,\mu^{N}_{{0}})-\mathcal{U}(t,{\mu_{\text{init}}})\big)+\big(\mathcal{U}(0,\mu^{N}_{{t}})-\mathcal{U}(t,\mu^{N}_{{0}})\big).\end{split}

To treat the last term, we define, for
t>0
, the finite dimensional projection
{U}_{t}:[0,t]\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
by

{U}_{t}(s,x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}):={\mathcal{U}}\bigg(t-s,\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\bigg).

Then

{\mathcal{U}}(0,\mu^{N}_{{t}})-{\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu^{N}_{{0}})={U}_{t}(t,Y^{1,N}_{t},\ldots,Y^{N,N}_{t})-{U}_{t}(0,Y^{1,N}_{0},\ldots,Y^{N,N}_{0}).

We can now apply Itô’s formula to this equality. By combining our Proposition

2.1

with
[
24
, Proposition 3.1]
, we can conclude that
{U}_{t}
is differentiable in the time component and twice-differentiable in the space components. Very much in the spirit of

[
18
, (5.131)]
, this allows us to use (
2.5
) to obtain a cancellation of all the terms apart from one term which gives us the rate of convergence of
1/N
:

(2.7)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\mathcal{U}(0,\mu^{N}_{{t}})-\mathcal{U}(t,\mu^{N}_{{0}})\bigr]&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg(\partial_{(y_{2})_{i}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}}{\delta m^{2}}(t-s,\mu^{N}_{s},z,z)\bigg)\,\mu^{N}_{s}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

More importantly, this formula holds regardless of the assumptions on the initial
data
Y^{1,N}_{0},\cdots,Y^{N,N}_{0}
. In particular, they are not required to be I.I.D.
In fact, the I.I.D. assumption becomes useful in order to estimate the first term in
the expansion
(
2.6
). Indeed, by
[
25
, Thm. 2.14]

(which we can apply in our context thanks to
Proposition
2.1
), we have

(2.8)

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\mathcal{U}(t,\mu^{N}_{{0}})\bigr]-\mathcal{U}(t,{\mu_{\text{init}}})=\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\bigg[s\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta})-s\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}},\tilde{\eta},{{\eta}_{1}})\bigg]\mathrm{d}s_{1}\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

where

\eta_{1}
is as
in
(
1.1
),
\tilde{\eta}

is independent of
(\eta_{1},\cdots,\eta_{N})
with law
{\mu_{\text{init}}}
and

\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}:=\frac{ss_{1}}{N}(\delta_{\tilde{\eta}}-\delta_{{\eta_{1}}})+{\mu_{\text{init}}}+s(\mu^{N}_{0}-{\mu_{\text{init}}}),\quad\quad s,s_{1}\in[0,1].

By combining the above equation with
(
2.6
)
and
(
2.7
), we deduce the following
lemma:

Lemma 2.2
.

If
b
and
\Phi
are smooth enough (so that the master equation has a
classical solution satisfying the conclusion of Proposition

2.1
), then
for any integer
N\geq 1
such that

(Y_{0}^{1,N},\cdots,Y_{0}^{N,N})
are I.I.D. with common law
{\mu_{\text{init}}}:={\mathcal{L}}(X_{0})
and for any

t\geq 0
,

(2.9)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})]-\Phi({\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t})}})&=\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\bigg[s\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta})-s\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}},\tilde{\eta},{{\eta}_{1}})\bigg]\mathrm{d}s_{1}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg(\partial_{(y_{2})_{i}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}}{\delta m^{2}}(t-s,\mu^{N}_{s},z,z)\bigg)\,\mu^{N}_{s}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

Equation (
2.9
) is in fact a key in our analysis.
In order to bound the left-hand side by
1/N
, uniformly in

t\geq 0
, we must be able:
(a)

to bound
\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, uniformly in time;
(b)

to bound
\partial_{(y_{2})_{i}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, with
an integrable decay
in long time and, possibly, an integrable blow-up in small time.
Observe that, implicitly, those bounds are required to be uniform in space. Obviously, this is
a very strong constraint, which we are however able to relax partially in two different ways:
(i)
In Subsection

3.5
, where we
just use local-in-time bounds for the derivatives (in the right-hand side in
(
2.9
))
and then obtain bounds for the left-hand side
up to times that are polynomial in
N
;

(ii)
In the analysis of the super-critical Kuramoto model
provided in Section
4
, where we use bounds for the deriavtives that are uniform away from an unstable equilibrium and where we obtain bounds for the left-hand side for a smaller class of test functionals
\Phi
.

2.3
Main assumptions

2.3.1
Functional spaces

We shall use two types
of functional spaces in our analysis:

W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
spaces (and their duals), and
W^{s,2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

spaces (and their duals), for
s>0
. Following

[
12
,
37
]
, we define the following notations.

1.

For any integer
n\geq 0
, we call
W^{n,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
the space of functions

f
that are
(n-1)
-times differentiable and whose
(n-1)^{\rm th}
-derivative is Lipschitz continuous.
The derivatives up to order
n-1
are denoted by
(\nabla^{k}f)_{k=1,\cdots,n-1}
,
with each
\nabla^{k}f
taking values in
({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{k}
.
The function
\nabla^{n-1}f
itself has a generalised derivative
\nabla^{n}f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d};({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n})
. The
W^{n,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
-norm is written

\|f\|_{n,\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{\infty}
.

2.

For any integer
n\geq 0
and any real
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
we call
W^{n+\alpha,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
the space of functions that are

n
-times differentiable such that their
n^{\rm th}
-derivatives
are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous.
The
W^{n+\alpha,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
-norm is written as

\|f\|_{n+\alpha,\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}\|\nabla^{k}f\|_{\alpha,\infty}
,
where
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha,\infty}

is the standard Hölder norm

\|f\|_{\alpha,\infty}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}|f(x)|+\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{T}^{d}:x\not=y}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}.

3.

For any integer
n\geq 0
and any real
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
we call
(W^{n+\alpha,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime}
the dual
space of
W^{n+\alpha,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
The dual norm is denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{(n+\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
.
Notice that

\|\cdot\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
, when restricted to the space of probability measures,
identifies with

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}
.

We merely write
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
for
\|\cdot\|_{0,\infty}
.
In the text, we make use of the following interpolation inequality:

(2.10)

\|\phi\|_{a+\eta,\infty}\leq\|\phi\|_{a,\infty}^{(\gamma-\eta)/\gamma}\|\phi\|_{a+\gamma,\infty}^{\eta/\gamma},

which holds for any
a\geq 0
,
\eta,\gamma\in[0,1]
with
\eta\leq\gamma
.
Above,
\phi\in W^{a+\gamma,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(see

[
12
]
).

In order to introduce
W^{s,2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we feel more convenient to use Fourier analysis.
For a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we denote
its Fourier coefficients by

f^{\bm{n}}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}f(x)e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi{\bm{n}}\cdot x}\mathrm{d}x,\quad{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}.

For
s>0
, we call
W^{s,2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
the space of functions
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

such that
\|f\|_{s,2}^{2}:=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}(1+n^{2})^{s}|f^{\bm{n}}|^{2}<\infty
. The
W^{s,2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
-norm is
\|\cdot\|_{s,2}
. The dual space is identified with
W^{-s,2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, which is defined in a similar manner, by extending the notation

(q^{\bm{n}})_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}
for the Fourier coefficients
of a Schwartz distribution
q
(acting on smooth functions of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
).
Then,

W^{-s,2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is the space of distributions
q

such that
\|q\|_{-s,2}^{2}:=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}(1+n^{2})^{-s}|q^{\bm{n}}|^{2}<\infty
.
The
W^{-s,2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
-norm is
\|\cdot\|_{-s,2}
.
For brevity, we write
\|\cdot\|_{2}
for
\|\cdot\|_{0,2}
.

For any vector field
f=(f^{1},\cdots,f^{d})
, we write

\|f\|=\max_{i=1,\cdots,d}\|f^{i}\|
for any norm on the space in which
the
f^{i}
’s are taken.
Most of the time, the duality product between
a function
f
and a distribution
q
is merely denoted by

\langle f,q\rangle
, with the spaces to which
f
and
q
belong
being implicitly understood.
For
z\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, we define
D_{z}
as the Dirac distribution at point
z
and
D_{z}^{\prime}
for the
opposite of its derivative. In short,
\langle D_{z}^{\prime},f\rangle=f^{\prime}(z)
.
For time-dependent function
f
and distribution
q
,
we often write
f(t,x)
for the former and
q(t)
for the latter.
Finally, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{0\}
and
any
n
-time differentiable
function
\Phi
on
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we write
\frac{\delta^{n}\Phi}{\delta m^{n}}(\mu)(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
,
for distributions
q_{1},\cdots,q_{n}
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
to denote

\frac{\delta^{n}\Phi}{\delta m^{n}}(\mu)(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}):=\langle\frac{\delta^{n}\Phi}{\delta m^{n}}(\mu,\ldots),q_{1}\otimes q_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes q_{n}\rangle,

if the duality product makes sense, where we recall that the function in the left-hand side of the duality product is defined on
(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}
. Note that

q_{1}\otimes q_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes q_{n}
is the tensor product of
q_{1},\cdots,q_{n}
(see
[
71
, Definition 40.3]
).
The Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
is denoted by

\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}
.
The constant function on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, equal to 1, is denoted by
{\mathbbm{1}}
.

2.3.2
Main assumptions

We use the following assumptions, with
n
and
k
denoting two integers and
\alpha
a real in
[0,1)
:

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{n+\alpha,k}
))

We say that
b
satisfies
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{n+\alpha,k}
))
if, for any
i\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
,
the function
b_{i}
is
k
times differentiable with respect to the measure argument

m
, and for any
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\ell\in\{0,\cdots,k\}
, the function

(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\ell+1}\ni(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})\mapsto\frac{\delta^{\ell}b_{i}}{\delta m^{\ell}}(x,m,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})

has crossed derivatives
\partial_{x}^{n_{0}}\partial_{y_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\partial_{y_{\ell}}^{n_{\ell}}\frac{\delta^{\ell}b_{i}}{\delta m^{\ell}}(x,m,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})

for any
n_{0},n_{1},\ldots,

n_{\ell}
in
\{0,\cdots,n\}
, with all these crossed derivatives being bounded w.r.t.
(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})
, uniformly in
m
, and
\alpha
-Hölder continuous w.r.t.

(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})
, uniformly in
m
, if
\alpha>0
.

(
Lip
-
b
-(
{n,k}
))

We say that
b
satisfies
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{n,k}
))
if it satisfies

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{n,k}
))
and, for any
i\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
and
\ell\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
,
for any
n_{0},n_{1},\ldots,n_{\ell}
in
\{0,\cdots,n\}
,
the derivatives
\partial_{x}^{n_{0}}\partial_{y_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\partial_{y_{\ell}}^{n_{\ell}}\frac{\delta^{\ell}b_{i}}{\delta m^{\ell}}

are Lipschitz continuous in
m
with respect to
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
.

We proceed similarly with the test functional
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
.
For two integers
k
and
n
, we define

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{n+\alpha,k}
))

by replacing
b_{i}(x,m)
by
\Phi(m)

in

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{n+\alpha,k}
))
. In particular, the crossed derivatives
\partial_{y_{1}}^{n_{1}}\ldots\partial_{y_{\ell}}^{n_{\ell}}\frac{\delta^{\ell}\Phi}{\delta m^{\ell}}(m,y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})

for
n_{1},\ldots,n_{\ell}
in
\{0,\cdots,n\}
, are bounded, uniformly in
m
,
and
\alpha
-Hölder continuous w.r.t.

(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})
, uniformly in
m
, if
\alpha>0
.

In the sequel, we will use several values
of
(n,k)

and
\alpha
in these assumptions. While we use higher values of

(n,k)

for intermediary steps, we eventually recover
the main results under the sole

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))

and

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
,
for some
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
by
a mollification argument.
Importantly, we observe that, under the latter assumption, the mapping

y_{1}\mapsto[(\delta^{2}\Phi/\delta m^{2})(m,y_{1},\cdot):y_{2}\mapsto(\delta^{2}\Phi/\delta m^{2})(m,y_{1},\cdot)\in W^{\gamma/2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})]
is

\gamma/2
-Hölder continuous, from which we deduce that

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
implies

(2.11)

\sup_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|q_{1}\|_{(\gamma/2,\infty)^{\prime}},\|q_{2}\|_{(\gamma/2,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\biggl|\frac{\delta^{2}\Phi}{\delta m^{2}}(\cdot,m)(q_{1},q_{2})\biggr|<+\infty.

2.4
Examples

2.4.1
Linear interaction

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose that for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
,
F_{i}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

is
n
-times continuously differentiable and that
G:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is
n
-times differentiable. We then define

b_{i}(x,\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}F_{i}(x,y)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}y),\quad\quad\Phi(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}G(y)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}y).

It can be shown easily that, for any integer
k\geq 1
,

\begin{split}&\frac{\delta^{k}b_{i}}{\delta m^{k}}(x,\mu,y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})=(-1)^{k}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}F_{i}(x,y)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}y)-F_{i}(x,y_{k})\bigg),\end{split}

and similarly for
\Phi
.
It is easily seen (
[
72
]
) that
b
satisfies
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{n,k}
))
and

(
Lip
-
b
-(
{n-1,k}
))
(if
n\geq 1
), whereas
\Phi
satisfies
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{n,k}
))
.

2.4.2
A completely non-linear example

The following ‘completely non-linear’ example will be very useful.

Proposition 2.3
.

For given
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\nu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
the function
\Phi
below satisfies

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\alpha/4,2}
))
:

\Phi(\mu)=\bigl\|\mu-\nu_{0}\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}^{2},\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

Moreover, there exists a real
C
such that, for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, the

\alpha/4
-norm of
[\delta\Phi/\delta m](\mu,\cdot)
is less than

C\sqrt{\Phi(\mu)}
and
the (joint)
\alpha/4
-norm
of
[\delta^{2}\Phi/\delta m^{2}](\mu,\cdot,\cdot)

(w.r.t. the two dot arguments)
is less than
C
.

We let
s:=(d+\alpha)/2
.
Then,
it is obvious to see that (writing
\bar{z}
for the conjugate of
z
)

(2.12)

\begin{split}\Phi(\mu)&=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\frac{1}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}\bigl(\mu^{\bm{n}}\bar{\mu}^{\bm{n}}+\nu_{0}^{\bm{n}}\bar{\nu}_{0}^{\bm{n}}-\mu^{\bm{n}}\bar{\nu}_{0}^{\bm{n}}-\nu_{0}^{\bm{n}}\bar{\mu}^{\bm{n}}\bigr).\end{split}

Throughout the proof, we use the fact that

(2.13)

\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\frac{1}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}<\infty,\quad\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\frac{|{\bm{n}}|^{\alpha/2}}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}<\infty.

Writing the product
\mu^{\bm{n}}\bar{\mu}^{\bm{n}}
in the form

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi{\bm{n}}\cdot(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime})

(and similarly for the other products) and using
(
2.13
), we
get that

\begin{split}\frac{\delta\Phi}{\delta m}(\mu)(x)&=\Phi^{(1)}(\mu,x)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Phi^{(1)}(\mu,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y),\\
\Phi^{(1)}(\mu,x)&=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\frac{1}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Bigl(e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi{\bm{n}}\cdot(\theta-x)}+e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi{\bm{n}}\cdot(x-\theta)}\Bigr)\bigl(\mu-\nu_{0}\bigr)(\mathrm{d}\theta),\quad x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.\end{split}

We then compute the derivative
\delta^{2}\Phi/\delta m^{2}
in a similar manner. We have

\begin{split}&\frac{\delta^{2}\Phi}{\delta m^{2}}(\mu)(x,x^{\prime})=\Phi^{(2)}(x,x^{\prime})-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Phi^{(2)}(x,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)-\Bigl(\Phi^{(1)}(\mu,x^{\prime})-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Phi^{(1)}(\mu,y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\Bigr).\\
&\Phi^{(2)}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\frac{1}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}\Bigl(e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi{\bm{n}}\cdot(x-x^{\prime})}+e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi{\bm{n}}\cdot(x^{\prime}-x)}\Bigr),\quad x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.\end{split}

Then, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we can find a constant
C
, depending on
\alpha
, such that for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,

\begin{split}\bigl|\Phi^{(1)}(\mu,x)-\Phi^{(1)}(\mu,x^{\prime})\bigr|&\leq C|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha/4}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\frac{|{\bm{n}}|^{\alpha/4}}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}|(\mu-\nu_{0})^{\bm{n}}|\\
&\leq C|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha/4}\biggl(\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\frac{|{\bm{n}}|^{\alpha/2}}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\frac{|(\mu-\nu_{0})^{\bm{n}}|^{2}}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}\biggr)^{1/2}\\
&\leq C\sqrt{\Phi(\mu)}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha/4}.\end{split}

Proceeding in a similar way with
\Phi^{(2)}
,
the conclusion easily follows.
∎

3
Uniform weak propagation of chaos for McKean-Vlasov equations

This section is dedicated to the analysis of the general

d
-dimensional case.
Most of our analysis is based upon the properties of the following linearised operator:

(3.1)

L_{m}q=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta q-\textrm{\rm div}\bigl(b(\cdot,m)q\bigr)-\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl(m\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,m)(q)\Bigr),

for a probability measure
m\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
a distribution
q
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. For an initial condition
q_{0}\in(W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime}
and a source term
r\in\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{\beta,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
,
for some
k\in[0,2)
and
\beta\in[0,2)
, we denote by
Linear
-[
\mu
,
q_{0}
,
r
]

the related Cauchy problem,
defined by

(3.2)

\partial_{t}q(t)-L_{m(t;\mu)}q(t)-r(t)=0,\quad t\geq 0\ ;\quad q(0)=q_{0},

interpreted in the weak sense. In particular, we study the behaviour of the master equation
(
2.5
) under
suitable ergodic properties of the operators

(L_{m(t;\mu)})_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
, that are stated in terms of

Linear
-[
\mu
,
q_{0}
,
r
]
:

(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
)-[
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
,
\lambda
])

For

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

\alpha,\beta\in[0,2)
and
non-negative constants
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
and
\lambda
, we say that
L_{m(t;\mu)}
in
(
3.1
) satisfies
(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
)-[
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
,
\lambda
])
if,
for any

k\in[\alpha,2)
,

q_{0}\in(W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime}
, with
\langle q_{0},{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
and
r\in\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{\beta,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
,
with
\left\langle r(t),{\mathbbm{1}}\right\rangle=0
,
t\geq 0
,
the unique solution in
\bigcap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
of the Cauchy problem
Linear
-[
\mu
,
q_{0}
,
r
]

satisfies (existence and uniqueness are guaranteed by Lemma
3.2
below):

(3.3)

\forall t>0,\quad\|q(t)\|_{(k-{\alpha})^{\prime},\infty}\leq C_{k}\biggl[{\frac{\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}}e^{-\lambda t}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}}{(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\mathrm{d}s\biggr].

(
Erg
)

We say
that
b
satisfies
(
Erg
)
if
there exists
\lambda>0
(depending on
b
), and, for any

\alpha,\beta\in[0,2)
, there exist
constants

(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
(depending on
b
,
\alpha
and
\beta
), such that

L_{m(t;\mu)}
satisfies

(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
)-[
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
,
\lambda
])
for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

The following remarks are in order. First, we sometimes say that

(
Erg
)
holds but only for a given probability measure
\mu
, in which case the
property is just assumed ‘for this
\mu
’ (and not ‘for all
\mu
’, as written in the last line of the definition).
Second,
we often use the notation

(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
))

(resp.

(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
))
)
to say that we invoke
(
Erg
)
(resp.

(
Erg
)
)
for this choice of
\alpha
and
\beta
, without specifying what the values of
(C_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2}

(which may depend on
(\alpha,\beta)
)
and
\lambda
are in that case.

Here is the main statement of this section, which summarises several results that are proven next.

Theorem 3.1
.

Assume
that
b
satisfies

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{\eta,2}
))

for some
\eta\in[0,1)

and that

\Phi

satisfies

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
, for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
Assume that there exists a measure
\nu_{\infty}
satisfying

(
Erg
)

and attracting the solutions
of
(
1.4
), uniformly with respect to the initial point, i.e.,
for any
\delta>0
, there exists

t\geq 0
such that

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}(m(t\,;\mu),\nu_{\infty})<\delta
for any

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
Then, there exists a collection of constants
(C_{\delta})_{\delta\in[0,1)}
such that, for any
N\geq 1
,

(3.4)

\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})]-\Phi({\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t})}})\Big|\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{0}N^{-1}&\textrm{\rm if}\quad\eta>0,\\
C_{\delta}N^{{-1+\delta}}&\textrm{\rm if}\quad\eta=0,\quad\textrm{\rm for any}\ \delta>0.\end{array}\right.

Moreover,
if
\nu_{\infty}

satisfies
(
Erg
)
but is not a global attractor, then the above two bounds remain true up to
any time
t\in[0,N^{p}]
, for any integer
p
, in which case the constants
C_{0}

and
C_{\delta}
also depend on
p
.

Notice
that the second result is just local and that the invariant measure
\nu_{\infty}
is not required to be unique.
We call this regime ‘metastable’.
As a corollary of this statement, we get
that the distance between the empirical measure and
the solution to the Fokker-Planck equation is typically of size
N^{-1/2}
in the norm

\|\cdot\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}
,
see Corollary
3.16

for the globally attracting regime
and Theorem
3.19
for the metastable case (in which case the estimate holds true up to polynomial times).

It is worth noticing that similar bounds can be proven in finite time, without any further need to
assume
(
Erg
)
nor the existence of an invariant measure. The corresponding statement would share some similarities with the result obtained in

[
26
,
27
]
, and would even provide some
improvement since
the drift and its derivatives in
m
are required to be Hölder continuous in

[
26
,
27
]
.
In order to clarify the finite in time versions of our statements, it is useful to formulate the following local (in time) version of

(
Erg
)
:

(
Local
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
,
T
)-[
(C_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2}
])

For

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

\alpha,\beta\in[0,2)
,
T\geq 0
and non-negative constants
(C_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2}
, we say that
L_{m(t;\mu)}
in
(
3.1
) satisfies
(
Local
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
,
T
)-[
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
])

if, for any
k\in[\alpha,2)
,
(
3.3
) holds true under the same
choice of
q
and
r
, but with
t
in
[0,T]
and

\lambda=0
.

(
Local
)

We say
that
b
satisfies
(
Local
)
if, for any
\alpha,\beta\in[0,2)
and
T\geq 0
,
there exist
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
(depending on
b
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
T
), such that

L_{m(t;\mu)}
satisfies

(
Local
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
,
T
)-[
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
])
for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

The rest of the section is organised as follows:
In Subsection
3.1
, we collect preliminary results on
the master equation (
2.5
).
In Subsection

3.2
, we explain how to use
(
Erg
)
to get long time bounds on the derivatives of the master equation and we achieve a first step in the proof of Theorem

3.1
.
Subsection

3.3
addresses the verification of
(
Local
)
in finite time. The connection
between

(
Erg
)
and the long time behaviour
of
(
1.4
)
is studied in
Subsection
3.4
,
with a special
treatment of the metastable case in
Subsection
3.5
.
Concrete examples are discussed in Subsection

3.6
.

3.1
Second order mixed spatial derivatives of the second order linear functional derivative of
\mathcal{U}

We first invoke a local estimate for forward Kolmogorov equations. The proof is an obvious variant of
[
72
, Theorem 2.3]
(see also

[
16
, Subsection 3.3]
).

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))

and let
q_{0}\in(W^{n,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime}
and
r\in\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{n,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
, for some

n\in\{0,1,2,3\}
. Then, the Cauchy problem

Linear
-[
\mu
,
q_{0}
,
r
]
has a unique solution in the space
\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{n,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|q(t)\|_{(n,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C\Big(\|q_{0}\|_{(n,\infty)^{\prime}}+{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|r(t)\|_{(n,\infty)^{\prime}}}\Big),

for some constant
C>0
, independent of the inputs
q_{0}
and
r
(but depending on
T
and on
b
).

We also recall
[
72
, Theorem 4.5]
, which gives a representation of the second order linear functional derivative of
\mathcal{U}
in terms of solutions of forward Kolmogorov equations.
It is quite easy to see (see the arXiv version v1 of this work
[
35
]
) that the assumptions stated below are enough to
apply
[
72
]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Under
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
and
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
,

{\mathcal{U}}
is twice differentiable with respect to
m
and the
first and
second-order derivatives

\frac{\delta{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m}

and
\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}
are given by

(3.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m}(t,\mu)(z)=\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z})\Big),

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{1}}),m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{2}})\Big)+\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{1}},\delta_{z_{2}})\Big),

where, for any
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
m^{(1)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu)\in\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],{(W^{0,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime}})
satisfies the Cauchy problem

Linear
-[
\mu
,
\nu-\mu
,0]
,
and, for any
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
m^{(2)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\in\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],{(W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime}})
satisfies the Cauchy problem

Linear
-[
\mu
,
\mu-\nu_{2}
,
r
]
,
with
r=(r(t))_{t\geq 0}=\emph{\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{Source}-[$\mu$,$m^{(1)}(\cdot;\mu,\nu_{1})$,$m^{(1)}(\cdot;\mu,\nu_{2})$]}}}
,
where

Source
-[
\mu
,
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
]
is a generic notation for

(3.6)

\begin{split}\emph{\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\text{Source}-[$\mu$,$q_{1}$,$q_{2}$]}$(t)$}}&:=-\text{\emph{div}}\Big(q_{1}(t)\,\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu))\big(q_{2}(t)\big)\Bigr)-\text{\emph{div}}\Big(q_{2}(t)\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu))\big(q_{1}(t)\big)\Bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\text{\emph{div}}\Big(m(t\,;\mu)\frac{\delta^{2}b}{\delta m^{2}}(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu))\big(q_{1}(t),q_{2}(t)\big)\Big).\end{split}

Intuitively,
m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu)
is equal to

[\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon]_{|\varepsilon=0+}m(t,(1-\varepsilon)\mu+\varepsilon\nu)

and

m^{(2)}(t;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

to

[\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon]_{|\varepsilon=0+}m^{(1)}(t\,;(1-\varepsilon)\mu+\varepsilon\nu_{2},\nu_{1})
.

Notice that
Source
-[
\mu
,
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
]
(t)
belongs to
(W^{k+1,\infty}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))^{\prime}
when

q_{1}(t)
and
q_{2}(t)
belong to

(W^{k,\infty}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))^{\prime}
, for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
.
We now differentiate each of the two terms on the right hand side of (
3.5
) respectively.

Proposition 3.4
.

Under
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
and
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
, for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
,
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
the derivative below exists and

\begin{split}&(\partial_{z_{2}})_{j}(\partial_{z_{1}})_{i}\biggl\{\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{1}}),m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{2}})\Big)\biggr\}=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),d^{(1)}_{j}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big),\end{split}

where
d^{(1)}_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,{z})\in\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
satisfies the Cauchy problem

Linear
-[
\mu
,
(D^{\prime}_{z})_{i}
,
0
]
, with the distribution

(D^{\prime}_{z})_{i}
being defined by

\left\langle\xi,(D^{\prime}_{z})_{i}\right\rangle=\partial_{x_{i}}\xi(z)
.

As made clear in the proof below,

d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z)=[d/d\varepsilon]_{\varepsilon=0+}m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z+\varepsilon e_{i}})
. Moreover,
notice that, from the conservative form of
(
3.1
), the integral of
d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)
with respect to the Lebesgue measure is zero, i.e.

\langle d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z),{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
, for all
t\geq 0
.

Existence of solution to
Linear
-[
\mu
,
(D^{\prime}_{z})_{i}
,
0
]
in
\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
is guaranteed by Lemma
3.2
.
For given
T>0
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
, we define, for

t\in[0,T]
,
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
z\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
h\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
,

(3.7)

\rho^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,h):=\frac{1}{h}\Big(m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z+he_{i}})-m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z})\Big)-d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z),\quad t\in[0,T].

By linearity,
\rho^{(1)}_{i}(\cdot;\mu,z,h)
solves

Linear
-[
\mu
,
h^{-1}(\delta_{z+he_{i}}-\delta_{z})-(D^{\prime}_{z})_{i}
,
0
]
.
Obviously,

\begin{split}\|h^{-1}(\delta_{z+he_{i}}-\delta_{z})-(D^{\prime}_{z})_{i}\|_{{(2,\infty)^{\prime}}}&=\sup_{\|\xi\|_{2,\infty}\leq 1}\bigg[\frac{\xi(z+he_{i})-\xi(z)}{h}-\partial_{x_{i}}\xi(z)\bigg]\leq\frac{|h|}{2}.\end{split}

By Lemma
3.2
(with
n=2
),
we get

(3.8)

\lim_{h\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\bigl\|\rho^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,h)\bigr\|_{(2,\infty)^{\prime}}=0.

Therefore, using the smoothness of
\delta^{2}\Phi/\delta m^{2}
,

(3.9)

\begin{split}&(\partial_{z_{1}})_{i}\biggl\{\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{1}}),m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{2}})\Big)\biggr\}\\
&=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(\frac{m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{1}+he_{i}})-m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{1}})}{h},m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{2}})\Big)\\
&=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{2}})\Big).\end{split}

The result follows by repeating the same procedure on
z_{2}
.
∎

The following result may be proven in the same way.

Proposition 3.5
.

Under
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
and
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
, for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
,

\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
the derivative below exists and

(\partial_{z_{2}})_{j}(\partial_{z_{1}})_{i}\biggl\{\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{1}},\delta_{z_{2}})\Big)\biggr\}=\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(d^{(2)}_{i,j}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\Big),

where
d^{(2)}_{i,j}(\cdot\,;\mu,{z_{1}},z_{2})\in\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
satisfies the Cauchy problem

Linear
-[
\mu
,
0
,
r
]

with

(r(t))_{t\geq 0}=
Source
-[
\mu
,
d^{(1)}_{i}(\cdot;\mu,z_{1})
,
d^{(1)}_{j}(\cdot;\mu,z_{2})
]
, see (
3.6
).

Similar to the interpretation of

d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z)
in Proposition

3.4
,
we have
the representation formula

d^{(2)}_{i,j}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})=[\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{1}]_{\varepsilon_{1}=0+}[\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}\varepsilon_{2}]_{\varepsilon_{2}=0+}m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z+\varepsilon_{1}e_{i}},,\delta_{z+\varepsilon_{2}e_{j}})
.

As before, existence of solution to

Linear
-[
\mu
,
0
,
r
]
(with the same
r
as in the statement)
in the space
\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
is guaranteed by Lemma
3.2
.
By Lemma 2.2.4 in

[
17
]
, we can exchange
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
in
(
3.6
) when the latter two satisfy

\langle q_{1}(t),{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=\langle q_{2}(t),{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
, for all
t\geq 0
.

Next,
for given
T>0
and
i\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
, we define, for

t\in[0,T]
,
\mu,\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
z\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
h\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
,

\Pi_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,\nu_{2},h):=\frac{1}{h}\Big(m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z+he_{i}},\nu_{2})-m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z},\nu_{2})\Big)-\Theta_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,\nu_{2}),

for
t\in[0,T]
,
where

\Theta_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,z,\nu_{2})\in L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
satisfies the Cauchy problem

Linear
-[
\mu
,
0
,
r_{\Theta}
]
, with

r_{\Theta}(t)=
Source
-[
\mu
,
m^{(1)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu_{2})
,
d^{(1)}_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,z)
]
.
By linearity and
with the same notation as in
(
3.7
)
for
\rho^{(1)}_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,z,h)
,

\Pi_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,z,\nu_{2},h)\in L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
satisfies

Linear
-[
\mu
,
0
,
r_{\Pi}
]

with

r_{\Pi}(t)=
Source
-[
\mu
,
m^{(1)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu_{2})
,
\rho^{(1)}_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,z,h)
]
.

By estimate (
3.8
), along with condition
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
,

(3.10)

\begin{split}&\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big\|-\text{{div}}\Big[m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{2})\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigl(\rho^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,h)\Big)\Bigr]\Bigr\|_{(3,\infty)^{\prime}}\\
&=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\|\xi\|_{3,\infty}\leq 1}\Bigl\langle\xi,-\text{{div}}\Bigl[m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{2})\,\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu))\Bigl(\rho^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,h)\Bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr\rangle\\
&=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\|\xi\|_{3,\infty}\leq 1}\Big\langle\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigl(\rho^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,h)\Bigr)\cdot\nabla\xi,m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{2})\Big\rangle=0.\end{split}

Similarly, one can show that

(3.11)

\begin{split}&\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Bigl\|-\text{{div}}\Big[m(t\,;\mu)\frac{\delta^{2}b}{\delta m^{2}}\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(\rho^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,h),m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{2})\Big)\\
&\hskip 60.0pt{+\rho^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,h)\,\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\bigl(m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{2})\bigr)}\Bigr]\Bigr\|_{(3,\infty)^{\prime}}=0.\end{split}

We then conclude by (
3.10
), (
3.11
) and Lemma
3.2
that

\lim_{h\to 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\Pi_{i}(t\,;\mu,z,\mu_{2},h)\|_{(3,\infty)^{\prime}}=0.

Consequently, by repeating the same argument as (
3.9
),
it follows that

(3.12)

(\partial_{z_{1}})_{i}\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{1}},\delta_{z_{2}})\Big)=\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(\Theta_{i}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},\delta_{z_{2}})\Big).

By repeating the same analysis on the variable
z_{2}
, the proof is complete.
∎

By combining the above results (using the
notations from
Propositions

3.4

and

3.5
), we obtain:

Proposition 3.6
.

Under
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))

and
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
, the derivative
(\partial_{z_{2}})_{j}(\partial_{z_{1}})_{i}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}
exists
for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}

and, for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
(\partial_{z_{2}})_{j}(\partial_{z_{1}})_{i}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,z_{1},z_{2})
is uniformly bounded in
t,z_{1},z_{2}
and Lipschitz continuous in
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, uniformly in time
t
in segments. Moreover, it can be represented by

\displaystyle(\partial_{z_{2}})_{j}(\partial_{z_{1}})_{i}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),d^{(1)}_{j}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big)+\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(d^{(2)}_{i,j}(t\,;\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})\Big).

A similar statement holds true for

\partial_{z}\frac{\delta{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m}(t,\mu,z)
,

\partial_{z_{1}}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,z_{1},z_{2})

and

\partial_{z_{2}}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,z_{1},z_{2})
.
Namely,

\begin{split}&(\partial_{z})_{i}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,z)=\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\big(d^{(2)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z)\big),\\
&(\partial_{z_{1}})_{i}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\delta_{z_{2}})\Big)+\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}(m(t\,;\mu))\Big(\Theta_{i}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},\delta_{z_{2}})\Big),\end{split}

with

\Theta_{i}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},\delta_{z_{2}})

as in
(
3.12
)
(and similarly for the derivative w.r.t.
z_{2}
).

3.2
From ergodic estimates on the tangent processes to uniform propagation of chaos

The following two propositions illustrate how assumption
(
Erg
)
is used next.

Proposition 3.7
.

Assume

(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))

and
(
Erg
)
and let
m^{(1)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu)
and
d^{(1)}_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,{z})

be defined as in Propositions

3.3

and

3.4
. Then, for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\begin{split}&\sup_{\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\|m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C_{0}^{(1)}e^{-\lambda_{1}t},\ \ \sup_{z\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\|d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,{z})\|_{({1-\alpha,\infty})^{\prime}}\leq\frac{C_{\alpha}^{(1)}}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}e^{-\lambda_{1}t},\quad t>0,\end{split}

where
\lambda_{1}
is chosen as

\lambda
in

(
Erg
)
,

C^{(1)}_{0}
as
C_{0}
in
(
Erg
-(0,0))

and

C^{(1)}_{\alpha}
as

C_{1}
in
(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,0))
.

This result is immediate from
(
Erg
)
applied to

m^{(1)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu)

(with
(k,\alpha,\beta)
therein given by
(0,0,0)
)
and

d^{(1)}(\cdot\,;\mu,z)
(with
(k,\alpha,\beta)
therein given by
(1,\alpha,0)
).
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Assume
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))

and
(
Erg
)
and
let

m^{(2)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

and

d^{(2)}_{i,j}(\cdot\,;\mu,{{z}_{1},z_{2}})

be defined as in Propositions

3.3

and

3.5
. Then, for any

\alpha\in[1,2)
and any
\epsilon\in(0,1]
,

\begin{split}&\sup_{\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\|m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C^{(2)}_{0}e^{-\lambda_{2}t},\ \sup_{z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\|d^{(2)}_{i,j}(t\,;\mu,{z_{1}},z_{2})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq{C^{(2)}_{1,\epsilon}K_{\epsilon}}e^{-\lambda_{2}t},\end{split}

for
t>0
,
where

\lambda_{2}>0
is chosen as
\lambda
in

(
Erg
)
;

C^{(2)}_{0}
only depends on

C_{0}
in

(
Erg
-(0,1))
,
C^{(1)}_{0}
in Proposition
3.7

and
K_{0}
below;
C^{(2)}_{1,\epsilon}
only depends on
\epsilon
,

C_{1}
in
(
Erg
-(
1
,
1
))
,
and

C^{(1)}_{1-\epsilon}
and
C^{(1)}_{1}
in Proposition
3.7
; and

\forall\eta\in[0,1),\quad K_{\eta}:=\sup_{m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\biggl[\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl\|\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(x,m,\cdot)\Bigr\|_{\eta,\infty}+\sup_{x,y\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl\|\frac{\delta^{2}b}{\delta m^{2}}(x,m,y,\cdot)\Bigr\|_{\eta,\infty}\biggr].

We just study
d^{(2)}_{i,j}(\cdot\,;\mu,{z_{1}},z_{2})
, the proof being similar for
m^{(2)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

(with the small difference that the initial condition of the latter is not equal to
0
but is bounded
in
\|\cdot\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
). We notice
from Proposition

3.7
(applied twice, with
\alpha=1
and

\alpha=1-\epsilon
respectively)
that, for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

\begin{split}&\bigg\|-\text{div}\bigg[d^{(1)}_{i}(t\ ;\mu,z_{2})\,\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu))\big(d^{(1)}_{j}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\big)\bigg]\bigg\|_{({1},\infty)^{\prime}}\\
&=\sup_{\|\xi\|_{{{1},\infty}}\leq 1}\bigg|\left\langle\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu))\bigl(d^{(1)}_{{j}}(t\,;\mu,{z_{1}})\bigr)\cdot\nabla\xi(\cdot),d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\right\rangle\bigg|\leq\frac{C^{(1)}_{1}C^{(1)}_{1-\epsilon}K_{\epsilon}}{{1\wedge t^{1-\epsilon/2}}}e^{-2\lambda_{1}t},\end{split}

for
t>0
. (Notice that the exponent
1-\epsilon/2
should be understood as
(1+(1-\epsilon))/2
, with
1
corresponding to our first choice of
\alpha
and
1-\epsilon
to our second choice for
\alpha
.) The other terms
appearing in (
3.6
) can be handled in the same way.
Noting that

d^{(2)}_{i,j}(0\,;\mu,{z_{1}},z_{2})=0

and
applying
(
Erg
)

with
(k,\alpha,\beta)=(1,1,1)
(note that the source term
r
, with an obvious choice for
r
therein, clearly satisfies

\langle r(t),{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
and has an integrable singularity in zero), the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Under

(
Local
)
,
Propositions

3.6

and

3.7

remain true, provided that
t
is restricted to
[0,T]
for some
T>0
,
and
\lambda_{1}
and

\lambda_{2}
are set equal to
0
.
In this case, the various constants may depend on
T
.

We now return to the original problem of the weak error estimate between the particle system (
1.1
) and the equation (
1.3
).
We are now in a position to prove
a
preliminary version of
Theorem
3.1
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Assume
that the drift
b

is bounded and
satisfies

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
.
For
a sequence of mollifiers

(\rho^{n}=n^{d}\rho(n\cdot))_{n\geq 1}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
with
\rho
a smooth symmetric density on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with a compact support,
define
the drifts

(b^{n}:{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\ni(x,m)\mapsto b(\cdot,m*\rho^{n})*\rho^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d})_{n\geq 1}

and assume that for any
n\geq 1
and any
\alpha,\beta\in[0,2)
,

b^{n}
satisfies

(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
))

with respect to constants

(C_{k}[\alpha,\beta])_{0\leq k<2}
and
\lambda
independent of
n
.
Then, for a function

\Phi

that
satisfies

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))

(for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
)
and
for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists
a constant
C>0
, only depending on
\epsilon
,
on
K_{0}
in the statement of
Proposition

3.8
, on
the

L^{\infty}
and Hölder bounds in

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))

and on

\max_{\alpha=0,1}\max_{\beta=0,1/2,1-\epsilon/2}\max_{k=0,1}(C_{k}[\alpha,\beta]),

such that, for any
\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

and any two integers
N,n\geq 1
,

(3.13)

\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})\bigr]-\Phi\bigl({\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t}^{n})}}\bigr)\Big|\leq{\frac{C}{\min(n^{1-\epsilon},N)}{\bigl(1+K_{\epsilon}^{n}\bigr)}},

where
K_{\epsilon}^{n}
is defined as
in the statement of Proposition

3.8
, but for
b^{n}
instead of
b
, and

(X_{t}^{n})_{t\geq 0}
stands for the solution of
(
1.3
)
with
b^{n}
as drift
and
\mu_{\rm init}^{\otimes N}
as initial distribution.

In brief, the impact of
K_{\epsilon}^{n}
in (
3.13
) is clarified in the proof of Theorem

3.1
. When
b
satisfies
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{\alpha,2}
))
,
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we choose
\epsilon=\alpha
, in which case

K_{\alpha}^{n}
can be bounded independently of
n
.
Then,
the right-hand side is
less than
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1})
when
n\geq N^{1/(1-\alpha)}
.
When
b
just satisfies
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
,

\epsilon
is arbitrary and
K_{\epsilon}^{n}={\mathcal{O}}(n^{\epsilon})
, in which case the right-hand side is
less than
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{-1+2\epsilon})
when
n=N
.

In the proof, we make use of two statements from the appendix: Theorem
5.1
, which is a regularisation result
for real-valued functions defined on
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, and Lemma
5.2
, which provides estimates for the marginal densities of large
particle systems. For the time being, we notice that

(3.14)

\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}(x,m)(y)=\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,m*\rho^{n})(\cdot)*\rho_{n}^{\otimes 2}(x,y),\quad{\frac{\delta^{2}b^{n}}{\delta m^{2}}(x,m)(y,z)=\frac{\delta^{2}b}{\delta m^{2}}(\cdot,m*\rho^{n})(\cdot,\cdot)*\rho_{n}^{\otimes 3}(x,y,z)}.

For
each
n\geq 1
,

b^{n}
satisfies

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
, with constants depending on
n
, and

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
independently of
n
.
However,
b^{n}
may not satisfy

(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))

since the second order derivative in
m
may not be Lipschitz in
m
. We invoke Theorem

5.1
below, from which we deduce that, for any given
\varepsilon>0

(which is distinct from
\epsilon
in the statement)
and each
n\geq 1
, there exists a new drift, denoted

\tilde{b}^{n}
, satisfying
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
w.r.t. constants depending on
(n,\varepsilon)
and satisfying
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
independently of

(n,\varepsilon)
, such that

(3.15)

\sup_{n\geq 1}\Bigl[\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}|(\tilde{b}^{n}-b^{n})(x,\mu)|+\sup_{x,y\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\bigl|\frac{\delta\tilde{b}^{n}}{\delta m}(x,\mu,y)-\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}(x,\mu,y)\bigr|\Bigr]\leq\varepsilon.

Thanks to
Theorem

5.1
,

\tilde{K}^{n}_{\epsilon}
, defined as in Proposition

3.8
but for
\tilde{b}^{n}
, is less than

CK_{\epsilon}^{n}
, for
C
independent of
(n,\varepsilon)
.
Importantly, the forthcoming
Remark

3.15

says that, for
\varepsilon
small enough and

\alpha,\beta\in[0,2)
,

\tilde{b}^{n}
satisfies

(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
))

w.r.t. constants
that depend only on

(C_{k}[\alpha,\beta])_{0\leq k<2}
and
\lambda
and not on
(n,\varepsilon)
.

Next, we can expand the difference
[b-b^{n}](x,m)
into

\displaystyle\bigl[b-b^{n}\bigr](x,m)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(b(x,m)-b(\cdot,m)*\rho^{n}(x)\Bigr)+\Bigl(b(\cdot,m)*\rho^{n}-b(\cdot,m*\rho^{n})*\rho^{n}\Bigr)(x)

\displaystyle=b(\cdot,m)*\bigl(\delta_{0}-\rho^{n}\bigr)(x)+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}r\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}\Bigl(z,rm+(1-r)m*\rho^{n},y\Bigr)\rho^{n}(x-z)\mathrm{d}z\biggr)\bigl[m*(\delta_{0}-\rho^{n})\bigr](\mathrm{d}y)

(3.16)

\displaystyle=:B_{0}^{n}(x,m)+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}B_{1}^{n}(x,m,y)\,m(\mathrm{d}y),

where

(3.17)

\begin{split}&B_{1}^{n}(x,m,y):=\Bigl[b_{1}^{n}(x,m,\cdot)*(\delta_{0}-\rho^{n})\Bigr](y),\\
&b_{1}^{n}(x,m,y):=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}\Bigl(z,rm+(1-r)m*\rho^{n},y\Bigr)\rho^{n}(x-z)\mathrm{d}z.\end{split}

Clearly,

B_{0}^{n}
and
B_{1}^{n}

are bounded and Lipschitz continuous in
m
(w.r.t.
\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}
) uniformly in
the other variables and in

n\geq 1

(which follows from the fact that
b
and
\delta b/\delta m

are Lipschitz continuous in
m
, uniformly in the other variables, with the latter being a consequence of the boundedness of

\delta^{2}b/\delta m^{2}
).

The value of
\epsilon

in the statement
is fixed throughout the proof
and we denote by
C
a generic constant as in the statement (whose value is allowed to vary from line to line).
Also, we let
(with
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
being as
in Propositions
3.7

and

3.8
)

\lambda:=\min(1,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.
We first assume that

\Phi
satisfies
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
.
This is only in the end that we relax this assumption,
just assuming
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))

(in brief, the relaxation is possible because
C
only depends on

\Phi
through the bounds in

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
).

First Step.

For each
n\geq 1
, we consider
{\mathcal{U}}^{n}
the solution to
(
2.5
),
with
b

replaced by
\tilde{b}^{n}
.
Accordingly,
we call
m^{(1),n}
,
m^{(2),n}
,
d^{(1),n}
and
d^{(2),n}
the various
functionals in
Propositions

3.3
,

3.4

and

3.5
.
