Following the derivation of
Lemma

2.2
,
we can apply
Itô’s formula to the process
({\mathcal{U}}^{n}(\mu^{N}_{t}))_{t\geq 0}
,
with
(\mu^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denoting the same
flow of empirical measures as in
(
1.1
) (driven by the unmollified drift
b
).
In comparison with
(
2.9
)
in the statement of Lemma

2.2
,
we get an additional term coming from the fact that the drift of
(
1.1
) is not the same as the drift of (
2.5
).
This additional term is

(3.18)

T_{\textrm{\rm add}}(t):=\frac{1}{N}{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl[b-\tilde{b}^{n}\bigr]\bigl({Y_{s}^{i,N}},\mu^{N}_{s}\bigr)\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}\bigl(s,\mu^{N}_{s}\bigr)(X_{s}^{i})\,\mathrm{d}s.

Second Step.
We address the right-hand side
of
(
2.9
). By
Propositions
3.3

and

3.8
,
we have a bound for
\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}/\delta m^{2}
, independent of
(n,\varepsilon)
(as the quantities therein are controlled independently of
(n,\varepsilon)
),
namely

\|[\delta^{2}/\delta m^{2}]{\mathcal{U}}^{n}(s,\cdot,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq Ce^{-\lambda t}
. Thus,

(3.19)

\sup_{t\geq 0}\bigg|\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\bigg[s\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}})(\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\eta})-s\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}})(\tilde{\eta},{{\eta}_{1}})\bigg]\mathrm{d}s_{1}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg|\leq C.

Next, we bound the second term
in the right-hand side
of
(
2.9
). By
Propositions

3.6
,

3.7

(together with (
2.11
))
and
3.8
,
we have
the following bound for

\partial_{y_{2}}\partial_{y_{1}}[\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}/\delta m^{2}]
:

(3.20)

\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\sup_{y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg|(\partial_{y_{1}})_{i}(\partial_{y_{1}})_{j}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}^{n}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,y_{1},y_{2})\bigg|\leq\frac{C{(1+\tilde{K}_{\epsilon}^{n})}}{1\wedge t^{1-\gamma}}e^{-\lambda t}\leq\frac{C{(1+K_{\epsilon}^{n})}}{1\wedge t^{1-\gamma}}e^{-\lambda t}.

that depends on
n

through
\tilde{K}^{n}_{\epsilon}
and thus

K^{n}_{\epsilon}
.
And then,

(3.21)

\begin{split}&\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg(\partial_{y_{2}}\partial_{y_{1}}\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}}{\delta m^{2}}(t-s,\mu^{N}_{s},z,z)\bigg)\,\mu^{N}_{s}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr|\leq C{(1+{K^{n}_{\epsilon}})}.\end{split}

Third Step.

We now address
T_{\textrm{\rm add}}
in
(
3.18
).
We recall
Propositions

3.6

and
3.7
.
We have the bound
\|\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}(s,\cdot,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq Ce^{-\lambda t}
.
Next,
as pointed out
in the statement of Proposition

3.6
,
the analogue of
Proposition

3.6

holds true, but for

\partial_{y_{1}}[\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}/\delta m^{2}]

and, similar to

\partial_{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}[\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}/\delta m^{2}]
,

\partial_{y_{1}}[\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}/\delta m^{2}]
can be bounded by

(3.22)

\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\sup_{y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg|(\partial_{y_{1}})_{i}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}^{n}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,y_{1},y_{2})\bigg|\leq\frac{C}{1\wedge t^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda t}.

Intuitively, the exponent
1/2
comes from the fact that there is only one derivative
\partial_{y_{1}}
(and no derivative
\partial_{y_{2}}
). For the same reason,
there is no need to add the additional factor
K_{n}
in the right-hand side.
The details are as follows.
In the formula
for

\partial_{y_{1}}[\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}^{n}/\delta m^{2}]

displayed in
Proposition

3.6
, the first term in the right-hand side gives the
singular behaviour in small time. As for the second term in the right-hand side,
it can be estimated by means of
the definition of
\Theta_{i}
in Proposition

3.5
.
Indeed, we know that

\Theta_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,z,\nu_{2})
satisfies the Cauchy problem

Linear
-[
\mu
,
0
,
r_{\Theta}
]
, with

r_{\Theta}(t)=
Source
-[
\mu
,
m^{(1)}(\cdot\,;\mu,\nu_{2})
,
d^{(1)}_{i}(\cdot\,;\mu,z)
]
.
Here,
\|m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{2})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}

is bounded and decays exponentially fast in long time whereas

\|d^{(1)}_{i}(t\,;\mu,z)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}

blows up like
1/\sqrt{t}
in small time and decays exponentially fast
in long time. To get the above inequality, it then suffices to
recall that,
for
each
n\geq 1
,

b^{n}
satisfies

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
with constants independent of
n
.

We now use
(
3.15
)
together with the
expansion
(
3.16
).
By the bound for

\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}
, we have

(3.23)

\begin{split}T_{\textrm{\rm add}}(t)&=\frac{1}{N}{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{N}B_{0}^{n}\bigl({Y_{s}^{i,N}},\mu^{N}_{s}\bigr)\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}\bigl(s,\mu^{N}_{s},{Y_{s}^{i,N}}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\hskip-1.0pt\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\frac{1}{N^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}B_{1}^{n}\bigl({Y_{s}^{i,N}},\mu^{N}_{s},{Y_{s}^{j,N}}\bigr)\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}\bigl(s,\mu^{N}_{s},{Y_{s}^{i,N}}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon),\end{split}

where
|{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)|\leq C\varepsilon
. By exchangeability and once again by the bound for

\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}
,
(
3.23
) may be rewritten as

\begin{split}T_{\textrm{\rm add}}(t)&:={\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(B^{n}_{0}\bigl({Y_{s}^{1,N}},\mu^{N}_{s}\bigr)+B^{n}_{1}\bigl({Y_{s}^{1,N}},\mu^{N}_{s},{Y_{s}^{2,N}}\bigr)\Bigr)\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}\bigl(s,\mu^{N}_{s},{Y_{s}^{1,N}}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s+{\mathcal{O}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}+\varepsilon\Bigr),\end{split}

where
|{\mathcal{O}}(1/N)|\leq C/N
, with
C
as in the statement.

The goal next is to replace

\mu^{N}_{s}

by

{\mu}_{s}^{N-(1,2)}
, with the latter standing for the empirical measure of
the
(N-2)
-vector
({Y_{s}^{3,N}},\cdots,{Y_{s}^{N,N}})
.
To do so,
we notice that, for any two
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}(s,\nu,y)-\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}(s,\mu,y)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}r\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\partial_{y}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}}{\delta m^{2}}\bigl(s,r\nu+(1-r)\mu,y,z\bigr)\,\bigl(\nu-\mu\bigr)(\mathrm{d}z),

for
\mu,\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
and
y,z\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.
And then, by
(
3.22
),

(3.24)

\Bigl|\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}(s,\nu,y)-\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}(s,\mu,y)\Bigr|\leq\frac{C}{1\wedge t^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda t}\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu).

Noticing that

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}({\mu}_{s}^{N-(1,2)},{\mu}_{s}^{N})\leq 4/N
, we deduce from the Lipschitz property
of
B_{0}^{n}
and
B_{1}^{n}
in the measure argument that

\begin{split}T_{\textrm{\rm add}}(t)&:={\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(B^{n}_{0}\bigl({Y_{s}^{1,N}},\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s}\bigr)+B^{n}_{1}\bigl({Y_{s}^{1,N}},\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s},{Y_{s}^{2,N}}\bigr)\Bigr)\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}\bigl(s,\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s},{Y_{s}^{1,N}}\bigr)ds\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+{\mathcal{O}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N}+\varepsilon\Bigr).\end{split}

Fourth Step.

In order to handle the above display, we
denote by

[{\mathbb{T}}^{d}]^{N}\ni{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\mapsto p_{t}^{N}({\bm{x}})
the marginal density at time

t
of the particle system (
1.1
).
We start with the analysis of
B_{1}^{n}
in
(
3.17
).

Denoting by

\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{{\bm{x}}}
the empirical measure on
\{x_{3},\cdots,x_{N}\}
when

{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})
, we have

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}B^{n}_{1}\bigl({Y_{s}^{1,N}},\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s},{Y_{s}^{2,N}}\bigr)\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}\bigl(s,\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s},{Y_{s}^{1,N}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\biggl[\int_{[{\mathbb{T}}^{d}]^{N}}b^{n}_{1}(x_{1},\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{{\bm{x}}},x_{2})\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}\bigl(s,\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{{\bm{x}}},x_{1}\bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\times\bigl[p_{s}^{N}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\cdots)-p_{s}^{N}(x_{1},x_{2}+z,x_{3},\cdots)\bigr]\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{n}\biggr]\rho^{n}(z)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}z.\end{split}

We now
apply
Lemma

5.2
, using the fact that
b_{1}
has same smoothness in

(x,y)
as
\delta b/\delta m
and that
the
m
-derivative of

\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}

satisfies
(
3.22
).
In the statement of
Lemma

5.2
, we use

\rho=1-\epsilon
. We get

(3.25)

\begin{split}&\biggl|{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}B^{n}_{1}\bigl({Y_{s}^{1,N}},\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s},{Y_{s}^{2,N}}\bigr)\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{{n}}\bigl(s,\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s},{Y_{s}^{1,N}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr|\\
&\leq\frac{C}{n^{{1-\epsilon}}}\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\frac{1}{1\wedge s^{(1-\epsilon)/2}}+s\Bigr)\frac{e^{-\lambda s}}{1\wedge s^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{C}{n^{{1-\epsilon}}}.\end{split}

Proceeding in the same way with

B_{0}
in (
3.16
), we get

(3.26)

\begin{split}&\biggl|{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}B^{n}_{0}\bigl({Y_{s}^{1,N}},\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s}\bigr)\cdot\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}^{n}\bigl(s,\mu^{N-(1,2)}_{s},{Y_{s}^{1,N}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr|\leq\frac{C}{n^{{1-\epsilon}}}.\end{split}

Conclusion.

We
now combine
Lemma
2.2

with (
3.19
)–(
3.21
)–(
3.25
)–(
3.26
).
We get, for any
t\geq 0
,

\begin{split}&\Big|\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})]-\Phi({\mathcal{L}}{{(\tilde{X}_{t}^{n})}})\Big|\leq{\frac{C}{\min(n^{1-\epsilon},N)}\bigl(1+K_{\epsilon}^{n}\bigr)}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon),\end{split}

with
(\tilde{X}_{t}^{n})_{t\geq 0}
standing for the solution of
(
1.3
)
with
\tilde{b}^{n}
as drift.
By letting
\varepsilon
tend to
0
in (
3.15
)
(using for instance the arguments from

[
53
]
to pass to the limit in the McKean-Vlasov SDE),
we obtain

(3.27)

\begin{split}&\Big|\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})]-\Phi({\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t}^{n})}})\Big|\leq{\frac{C}{\min(n^{1-\epsilon},N)}\bigl(1+K_{\epsilon}^{n}\bigr)}.\end{split}

This is the result for
\Phi
smooth. We
can extend it to
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
thanks to Theorem

5.1
.
We can find a sequence
(\Phi_{k})_{k\geq 1}
that converges uniformly to

\Phi
, such that each
\Phi_{k}
satisfies
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
(for constants depending on

k
) and all the functions
\Phi_{k}
satisfy

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
independently of
k
.
Applying
(
3.27
)
to each
\Phi_{k}
and letting
k
tend to
\infty
, we complete the proof (as
C
above is then independent of
k
).
∎

3.3
Estimates in finite time

We here prove

(
Local
)
under the sole assumption

(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
.
We start with

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
t>0
,
\xi\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
V
be a vector field from
[0,t]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
into
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
that is
Hölder continuous in time and space. Then the Cauchy problem

(3.28)

\partial_{s}w(s,\cdot)+\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}w(s,\cdot)+V(s,\cdot)\cdot\nabla_{x}w(s,\cdot)=0,\quad\quad s\in[0,t]\,;\quad w(t,\cdot)=\xi,

admits a unique solution
(w(s,\cdot))_{0\leq s\leq t}

that is continuous on
[0,t]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
and classical on
[0,t)\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Moreover,
there are constants
C,\lambda>0
(only depending on

V
through

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|V(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}
) such that

(3.29)

\bigg\|w(s,\cdot)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\bigg\|_{\infty}\leq C\|\xi\|_{\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)},\quad\quad\forall s\in[0,t],

and, for any
\alpha\in[0,2)
and
\beta\in[0,1)
with
\alpha\leq 1+\beta
(also allowing
C
to depend on
\alpha,\beta
),

(3.30)

\big\|\nabla_{x}w(s,\cdot)\big\|_{\beta,\infty}\leq{\frac{C}{1\wedge(t-s)^{(1+\beta-\alpha)/2}}}\|\xi\|_{{\alpha},\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)},\quad\quad\forall s\in[0,t].

Notice that, within the framework of Lemma
3.11
,
the
\alpha
-Hölder semi-norm
of
w(s,\cdot)
, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
is less than
C(1\wedge(t-s)^{\alpha/2})^{-1}\|\xi\|_{\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}
, for
C,\lambda
as in (
3.30
) (with
\alpha=\beta=0
therein).
This follows from an obvious interpolation argument
(see (
2.10
))
combining
(
3.29
) and
(
3.30
) (with
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)
therein).

The well-posedness of
(
3.28
)
in the classical sense is a standard fact (see
[
42
, Thm. 5, Chap. 3]
). Estimate (
3.29
) is a direct consequence of
[
17
, Lem. 7.4]
.
In order to prove
(
3.30
),
we recall the following standard property. For
\alpha\in[0,2)
and
\beta\in[0,1)
with
\alpha\leq 1+\beta
,

(3.31)

\bigl\|w(s,\cdot)\bigr\|_{1+\beta,\infty}\leq\frac{C}{({t-s})^{({1+\beta-\alpha})/2}}\bigl\|w\bigl(s+({t-s})\vee 1,\cdot\bigr)\bigr\|_{\alpha,\infty},\quad{s<t},

for a constant
C
only depending on

\alpha
,
\beta
and

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|V(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}
.
This gives
(
3.30
) when
t-s\leq 1
.
When
t-s\geq 1
,
we apply
(
3.31
)
to the function
(w(r,\cdot)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}w(s+1,y)\mathrm{d}y)_{s\leq r\leq s+1}
.
By
(
3.29
),

(3.32)

\bigl\|\nabla w(s,\cdot)\bigr\|_{\beta}\leq C\Bigl\|w\bigl(s+1,\cdot\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}w(s+1,y)\mathrm{d}y\Bigr\|_{\infty}\leq Ce^{-\lambda(t-s-1)},

from which we complete the proof of
(
3.30
).
∎

Lemma

3.11

allows us to check

(
Local
)
in a general setting.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
b
satisfy
(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
. Then,
it satisfies
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\text{Local})}
and, for each
\alpha
,
{\beta}

and
T
as in the statement of

(
Local
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
,
T
))
, the constants
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}

in

(
Local
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
,
T
)-[
(C_{k})_{0\leq k\leq 2}
])

depend on
b
only through
the quantities

(3.33)

\sup_{m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|b(\cdot,m)\|_{\infty},\quad\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\bigl\|\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}\big(x,m\big)(\cdot)\bigr\|_{0,\infty}.

For
\alpha
,
\beta
and
T
as in

(
Local
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
,
T
))
,
for
k\in[\alpha,2)
,
for

r\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty),(W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
,
with
\left\langle r(t),{\mathbbm{1}}\right\rangle=0
,
and
for

q_{0}\in(W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime}
, with
\langle q_{0},{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
,
we consider the solution
q
to

Linear
-[
\mu
,
q_{0}
,
r
]

(see (
3.2
))
within the space
L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})

(which exists under the standing assumption on
b
).

First Step.

We provide a bound for
q
in
L^{\infty}([0,{T}],(W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
.
We adopt a duality approach. For a smooth function
\xi
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
and for
t\in[0,T]
, we consider the following Cauchy problem

(3.34)

\partial_{s}w+\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}w+b\bigl(x,m(s\,;\mu)\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}w=0,\quad\quad(s,x)\in[0,t]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\ ;\qquad w(t,x)=\xi(x).

The above problem fits the assumption of Lemma

3.11
:
since
b
is bounded,
the path

(m(s\,;\mu))_{0\leq s\leq t}

is
1/2
-Hölder continuous in
s
w.r.t.
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and,
by

(
Lip
-
b
-(
{4,2}
))
,
the transport coefficient in
(
3.34
)
has time-space continuous derivatives.
Since
b
has derivatives in
x
of order 1 and 2,

w
has derivatives up to the order 4.
Therefore, we can expand
the duality product
\langle w(s,\cdot),q(s)\rangle
.
By (
3.1
)
and
(
3.2
),

\displaystyle\langle\xi,q(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{w}(0,\cdot),q(0)\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\bar{w}(s,\cdot),r(s)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}\big(x,m(s,\mu)\big)\bigl(q(s)\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}w(s,x)\,m(s,\mu)(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.35)

\displaystyle=:T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3},

with
\bar{w}(s,\cdot)=w(s,\cdot)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w(s,y)\mathrm{d}y

(notice that we can write
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
in terms of
\bar{w}
since
q(0)
and

r
are centred).
By
Lemma

3.11
,

(3.36)

\big|T_{1}\big|\leq{\|\bar{w}(0,\cdot)\|_{k,\infty}\|q(0)\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}}\leq\frac{C_{\alpha,{k},b}}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}\|\xi\|_{k-\alpha,\infty}\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}},

where
C_{\alpha,{k},b}
only depends on
\alpha
,
k
and
b

through the quantity

\sup_{m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|b(\cdot,m)\|_{\infty}
.
As for
T_{3}
, we have, by
(
3.30
) (with

(\alpha,\beta)
therein given by

({0},0)
),

(3.37)

\begin{split}\big|T_{3}\big|&\leq C_{\alpha,b}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{x}w(s,\cdot)\|_{{\infty}}\|q(s)\|_{({0,\infty})^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{\alpha,b}{\|\xi\|_{{\infty}}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|q(s)\|_{({0,\infty})^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Similarly,

(3.38)

\begin{split}\bigl|T_{2}\bigr|&\leq C_{\alpha,\beta,b,T}\|\xi\|_{{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Third Step.

By combining (
3.35
), (
3.36
), (
3.37
) and (
3.38
),
we have

(3.39)

\begin{split}\|q(t)\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C_{\alpha,k,b}\biggl[\frac{\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}ds\biggr]+C_{\alpha,\beta,b,T}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Multiplying
by
\exp(-ct)(\tau-t)^{-1/2}
, for some
c>0
, and integrating with respect to

t
from
0
to
\tau
, for some
\tau>0
,
we obtain, for
C=C_{\alpha,\beta,k,b,T,\tau}
,

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-ct}\frac{\|q(t)\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}}{(\tau-t)^{1/2}}dt&\leq C\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+C\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-cs}\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\biggl(\int_{s}^{\tau}\frac{e^{-c(t-s)}}{(\tau-t)^{1/2}[1\wedge(t-s)^{{1}/2}]}\mathrm{d}t\biggr)\mathrm{d}s.\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-cs}\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}\biggl(\int_{s}^{\tau}\frac{e^{-c(t-s)}}{(\tau-t)^{1/2}[1\wedge(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}]}\mathrm{d}t\biggr)\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

and then
(for a possibly new choice of
C
),

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-ct}\frac{\|q(t)\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}}{(\tau-t)^{1/2}}dt&\leq C\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+C\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-cs}\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\biggl(\int_{s}^{\tau}\frac{e^{-c(t-s)}}{(\tau-t)^{1/2}(t-s)^{{1}/2}}\mathrm{d}t\biggr)\mathrm{d}s.\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-cs}\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}\biggl(\int_{s}^{\tau}\frac{e^{-c(t-s)}}{(\tau-t)^{1/2}(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}}\mathrm{d}t\biggr)\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

Choosing
c
large enough, the first integral from
s
to
\tau
in the right-hand side can be made small.
Then,

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-ct}\frac{\|q(t)\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}}{(\tau-t)^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}t\leq C\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-cs}\frac{\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{(\tau-s)^{{1}/2}}\mathrm{d}s+C\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-cs}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(\tau-s)^{\beta/2}}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Obviously, this gives a bound for the left-hand side when
k=\alpha=0
(provided that it is finite).
Assuming first that
\|q_{0}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
and
\sup_{0\leq t\leq\tau}\|r(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}

are finite
(so that
\sup_{0\leq t\leq\tau}\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}

is finite thanks to Lemma
3.2
),
and inserting above bound in
(
3.39
),
we get

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\text{Local})}

(for this type of initial condition). The assumptions on
q_{0}
and
r
can be easily dropped by mollification:
we get
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\text{Local})}

for any initial condition.
∎

3.4
Connection between the assumption
(
Erg
)
and
the long time behaviour of the McKean-Vlasov equation

We establish connections between
(
Erg
)
and the long time behaviour of
(
1.3
)–(
1.4
). We start with

Proposition 3.13
.

Under

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))

and

(
Erg
)
,
the equation (
1.3
) has a unique invariant measure
\nu_{\infty}
and it is exponentially stable, i.e., there exist two constants
C,\lambda>0
such that, for any

\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

(3.40)

\textrm{\rm dist}_{{\rm TV}}\Bigl(m(t\,;\mu),\nu_{\infty}\Bigr)\leq Ce^{-\lambda t},\quad t\geq 0.

For a smooth test function
f
on the torus, we choose

\Phi(m)=\langle f,m\rangle
in the statement of
Proposition

3.3
.
And then,
by
Proposition
3.7
, we obtain that, for any two measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

(3.41)

\begin{split}\bigl|\langle f,m(t\,;\mu_{1})-m(t\,;\mu_{2})\rangle\bigr|&=\biggl|\int_{0}^{1}\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}m^{(1)}\bigl(t\,;\lambda\mu_{1}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{2},\delta_{z}\bigr)(f)\mathrm{d}\bigl(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1}\bigr)(z)\biggr)\mathrm{d}\lambda\biggr|\\
&\leq C^{(1)}_{0}\|f\|_{\infty}e^{-\lambda_{1}t}\textrm{\rm dist}_{{\rm TV}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\leq C^{(1)}_{0}\|f\|_{1,\infty}e^{-\lambda_{1}t}\textrm{\rm dist}_{{\rm TV}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).\end{split}

By choosing
\mu_{1}
and

\mu_{2}
as two candidates for being an invariant measure,
this shows that an invariant measure (if it exists) must be unique. Existence follows by choosing
\mu_{1}=\mu
and then
\mu_{2}=m(s\,;\mu)
in
(
3.41
). By the flow property of McKean-Vlasov dynamics, we have
m(t\,;m(s\,;\mu))=m(t+s\,;\mu)
, and thus

\begin{split}\bigl|\langle f,m(t+s\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\mu)\rangle\bigr|\leq 2C_{0}^{(1)}\|f\|_{\infty}e^{-\lambda_{1}t}.\end{split}

By completeness of
({\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),{\mathcal{W}}_{1})
, we deduce that

(m(t\,;\mu))_{t\geq 0}
has a limit. We call it
\nu_{\infty}
.
Writing the above left-hand side in the form

|\langle f,m(s\,;m(t\,;\mu))-m(t\,;\mu)\rangle|
and letting
t
tend to
\infty
, it is easy to deduce that
m(s\,;\nu_{\infty})=\nu_{\infty}
. Finally, by choosing

\mu_{1}=\nu_{\infty}
in
(
3.41
), we get that

\nu_{\infty}

is exponentially stable.
∎

An interesting question is to determine to which extent the converse to
Proposition

3.13

holds true:

Proposition 3.14
.

Assume
that
b
satisfies

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))

and that there exists an invariant measure
\nu_{\infty}
satisfying

(
Erg
)
. For

(\rho^{n}=n^{d}\rho(n\cdot))_{n\geq 1}
,
with
\rho
a smooth symmetric compactly supported density on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
define
the same drifts

(b^{n}:{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\ni(x,m)\mapsto b(\cdot,m*\rho^{n})*\rho^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d})_{n\geq 1}

as in
Proposition

3.10
.

Then, there exist
\lambda_{0}>0
and
C_{0}\geq 0
, and for any
\alpha\in[0,2)
, there exist
a_{\alpha}>0
and
(C_{\alpha,\beta}\geq 0)_{0<\beta<1}
, such that, for any

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
with
\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq a_{\alpha}
,
any integer
n\geq 1
and any
\beta\in(0,1)
,

\forall t\geq 0,\quad\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\mu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq\frac{C_{\alpha,\beta}}{n^{\beta}},\quad\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C_{0}e^{-\lambda_{0}t}+{\frac{C_{\alpha,\beta}}{n^{\beta}}},

where
(m^{n}(t\,;\mu))_{t\geq 0}
is the solution to
(
1.4
)
with
b^{n}
as drift.
And,
for any large integer
n
and any
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

with
\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq a_{\alpha}
,

b^{n}
satisfies
(
Erg
)
at
\mu
, with
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}
and
\lambda
therein being uniform w.r.t.

\mu
.

We first recall the following preliminary result, see for instance

[
50
, Prop. 2.3]
.
For any
t>0
,
m(t\,;\mu)
has a density, denoted by

x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\mapsto p(t,x\,;\mu)
. For any
\beta\in(0,1)
, it satisfies

(3.42)

p(t,x\,;\mu)\leq Cg_{\mu}(t,x)\ ;\quad\bigl|p(t,x\,;\mu)-p(t,x^{\prime}\,;\mu)\bigr|\leq\frac{C_{\beta}}{1\wedge t^{\beta/2}}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\beta}\Bigl(g_{\mu}(t,x)+g_{\mu}(t,x^{\prime})\Bigr),

where
g_{\mu}(t,\cdot)
is a density that satisfies
\|g_{\mu}(t,\cdot)\|_{\varrho}\leq C_{\varrho}\min(1,t)^{d(1/\varrho-1)/2}
, for

\varrho\in(1,+\infty]
.

First Step.
We provide a bound for the distance between
(m^{n}(t\,;\mu))_{t\geq 0}
and
(m(t\,;\nu))_{t\geq 0}
.
We write

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\bigl[m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr] -\frac{1}{2}\Delta\bigl(m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\\
&\hskip 5.0pt +\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl(b^{n}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\bigl[m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr]\Bigr)+\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl(\bigl[b^{n}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)-b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\bigr]m(t\,;\nu)\Bigr)=0,\end{split}

with
\mu-\nu
as initial condition. We rewrite the last term in the above left-hand side as

\begin{split}&b^{n}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)-b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\\
&=\Bigl[b^{n}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)-b^{n}\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\Bigr]+\Bigl[b^{n}\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)-b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\Bigr]\\
&=\biggl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigl(m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\Bigr)\biggr]+\Bigl[b^{n}\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)-b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\biggl[\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,rm^{n}(t\,;\mu)+(1-r)m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)-\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigr]\Bigl(m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr]\\
&=:\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigl(m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\Bigr)+\vartheta^{n}_{1}(t)(\cdot)+\vartheta^{n}_{2}(t)(\cdot).\end{split}

We thus have

(3.43)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\bigl[m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr] -L^{n}_{m^{n}(t\,;\mu)}\bigl(m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)+{\rm div}\Bigl(\bigl[\vartheta^{n}_{1}(t)+\vartheta^{n}_{2}(t)\bigr]m(t\,;\nu)\Bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+{{\rm div}\Bigl(\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigl(m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\Bigr)\bigl(m(t\,;\nu)-m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigr)}=0.\end{split}

Above,
L^{n}_{m^{n}(t,\mu)}

denotes the linearised operator
(
3.1
)
associated with
b^{n}
.
By using the fact that
\delta^{2}b^{n}/\delta m^{2}
is bounded,
we get
\|\vartheta^{n}_{2}(t)\|_{\infty}\leq C\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\|^{2}_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
,
the constant
C
being allowed (here and in the rest of the proof) to depend
on the bounds in

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
. We refer to the preliminary step in the proof of Proposition
3.10
for the fact that
each
b^{n}
satisfies

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
with constants that are independent of
n
.
As for
\vartheta^{n}_{1}(t)
, we
write it in the form:

\begin{split}\vartheta^{n}_{1}(t)&=\Bigl[b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)*\rho^{n}\bigr)-b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\Bigr]*\rho^{n}+\Bigl[b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)*\rho^{n}-b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\Bigr]=:\vartheta^{n}_{1,1}(t)+\vartheta^{n}_{1,2}(t).\end{split}

Recalling
(
3.42
), we obtain

\begin{split}\|\vartheta^{n}_{1,1}(t)\|_{\infty}\leq C\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}\bigl(m(t\,;\nu),m(t\,;\nu)*\rho^{n}\bigr)&\leq C\int_{[{\mathbb{T}}^{d}]^{2}}|p(t,x;\nu)-p(t,x-y;\nu)|\rho^{n}(y)\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\frac{C_{\beta}n^{-\beta}}{(1\wedge t^{\beta/2})}.\end{split}

Moreover, by the same argument,

\begin{split}\Bigl\|\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl(\vartheta_{1,2}^{n}(t)m(t\,;\nu)\Bigr)\Bigr\|_{(1+\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq\sup_{\|\xi\|_{1+\beta,\infty}\leq 1} \Bigl\langle\Bigl[\Bigl(b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)*\rho^{n}-b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr)\Bigr)m(t\,;\nu)\Bigr],\nabla\xi\Bigr\rangle\\
&=\sup_{\|\xi\|_{1+\beta,\infty}\leq 1} \Bigl\langle b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\nu)\bigr),\bigl(m(t\,;\nu)\nabla\xi\bigr)*\rho^{n}-m(t\,;\nu)\nabla\xi\Bigr\rangle\leq\frac{C_{\beta}n^{-\beta}}{(1\wedge t^{\beta/2})}.\end{split}

Applying

(
Local
)

(i.e., the finite time version of
(
3.3
))
to
(
3.43
)
three times,
once
with
k=\alpha
,

\mu-\nu
as initial condition and
0
as remainder,
once
with
k=0
,
0
as initial condition and
\textrm{\rm div}(\vartheta^{n}_{1}m(t\,;\nu))
as remainder
(which is in
(W^{(1+\beta,\infty)}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))^{\prime}
)
and another time with
k=0
,
0
as initial condition and
\textrm{\rm div}(\vartheta^{n}_{2}m(t\,;\nu))
as remainder
(which is in
(W^{(1,\infty)}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))^{\prime}
), we get, for any time
S\geq 0
and any
t\in[0,S]
,

(3.44)

\begin{split}\bigl\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C_{\alpha,S}\frac{\|\mu-\nu\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}+C_{S}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bigl\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-m(s\,;\nu)\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}^{2}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{C_{\beta,S}}{n^{\beta}}.\end{split}

Second Step.

By upper bounding

\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-m(s\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}^{2}
by

2\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-m(s\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
,
we recover an inequality very similar to
(
3.39
), from which we deduce that

(3.45)

\bigl\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C_{\alpha,S}\frac{\|\mu-\nu\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}+\frac{C_{\beta,S}}{n^{\beta}},\quad t\in(0,S],

By choosing
\mu=\nu
, this provides the rate of convergence of
m^{n}(\cdot\,;\mu)
to
m(\cdot\,;\mu)
in finite time.

Now, we choose
\nu=\nu_{\infty}
.
For a fixed
a>0
, we can choose
t_{0}
small enough and
n_{0}
large enough such that
the sum of the last two terms on
(
3.44
) (with
t=t_{0}
and
n\geq n_{0}
) is less than
a/2
. Next, for this
t_{0}
, we
can choose

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
small enough such that
the first term on the right-hand side is also less than

a/2
.
We deduce that
\|m^{n}(t_{0}\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq a
for
n\geq n_{0}
.
This says that, when

\mu
is close to
\nu_{\infty}
for
the norm
\|\cdot\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
,

m^{n}(\cdot\,;\mu)

is close to
\nu_{\infty}
for
\textrm{\rm dist}_{\textrm{\rm TV}}
at time
t_{0}
. Equivalently, by
restarting
m^{n}
at time
t_{0}
, we can assume that
the initial condition is close to
\nu_{\infty}
for
\textrm{\rm dist}_{\textrm{\rm TV}}
(and not only for the norm

\|\cdot\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
).
Hence, we now study the long time behaviour of

({\rm dist}_{\rm TV}(m^{n}(t\,;\mu),\nu_{\infty}))_{t\geq 0}

under the assumption
that

{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu_{\infty})
is small. The strategy is to repeat the analysis of the first step, but exchanging the roles of
b^{n}
and
b
, and using
(
Erg
)
for
b
(instead of using

(
Local
)
for
b^{n}
). This is the key point to get long time estimates, but this barely changes the proof. In fact, this leads to the following variant of
(
3.44
):

(3.46)

\begin{split}\bigl\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda t}+C\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}^{2}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\mathrm{d}s+\frac{C_{\beta}}{n^{\beta}}.\end{split}

We choose
\beta=1/2
.
For some
a>0
(whose value is fixed next and which has nothing to do with the parameter
a
used in the previous paragraph), we now assume that

\mu
and
n
satisfy

(3.47)

C\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu_{\infty})+\frac{C_{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\leq a.

We argue by contradiction to show that

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu_{\infty})
remains small. We thus assume that the hitting time
t_{0}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\geq 2a\}

is finite.
Then, by continuity (in
s
) of

\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
, we get
from (
3.46
) that

2a\leq a+4Ca^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}/{(1\wedge s^{1/2})}\mathrm{d}s,

which is impossible if
a
is small enough. This says that,
for
a
small enough and under the prescription
(
3.47
), we have

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq 2a
.
By a similar analysis (or letting

n
tend to
\infty
in
(
3.45
), an argument invoked in the proof of
Proposition
3.10
),
we also have

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|m(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq 2a
when
\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}=\textrm{\rm dist}_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu_{\infty})<a/C
.

This explains how to obtain
a global in time version of
(
3.45
).
Assume indeed for a while that, for some
\mu
satisfying
(
3.47
)
and for any
n
large enough,

(
Erg
)
holds
true for
the version of
(
1.4
) driven by
b^{n}
and for constants
(C_{k})_{0\leq k<2}

and
\lambda
that are independent of
n
(this is proven in the two steps below).
Then,
for two measures
\mu
and
\nu
satisfying
(
3.47
) and for

n
large,
we obtain the
following
long time version
of (
3.44
)
(whose derivation is similar to
(
3.46
)):

\begin{split}\bigl\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq\|\mu-\nu\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda t}+C\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-m(s\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}^{2}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{C_{\beta}}{n^{\beta}}.\end{split}

Inserting
the two bounds

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq 2a

and

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|m(t\,;\nu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq 2a
and using in addition the inequality

e^{-\lambda t}/(1\wedge t^{1/2})\leq Ce^{-\lambda t/2}/t^{1/2}
, we obtain, for
t\geq 0
,

(3.48)

\begin{split}\bigl\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C\|\mu-\nu\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda t}+Ca\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-m(s\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{(t-s)^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)/2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{C_{\beta}}{n^{\beta}}.\end{split}

Multiplying by

e^{-\lambda(\tau-t)/2}/(\tau-t)^{1/2}
,
integrating w.r.t.
t\in[0,\tau]
, we obtain (for a new value of
C
)

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{(\tau-t)^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda(\tau-t)/2}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq C\|\mu-\nu\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda\tau/2}+Ca\int_{0}^{\tau}\bigl\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-m(s\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\Bigl(\int_{s}^{\tau}\frac{e^{-\lambda(\tau-s)/2}}{(t-s)^{1/2}(\tau-t)^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}t\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{C_{\beta}}{n^{\beta}}\\
&\leq C\|\mu-\nu\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda\tau/2}+Ca\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-m(s\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{(\tau-s)^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda(\tau-s)/2}\mathrm{d}s+\frac{C_{\beta}}{n^{\beta}}.\end{split}

For
Ca\leq 1/2
(recalling
(
3.47
) for
\mu
,
\nu
and
n
), we get
a bound for the left-hand side.
Back to
(
3.48
),

(3.49)

\bigl\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C\|\mu-\nu\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda t/2}+\frac{C_{\beta}}{n^{\beta}},\quad t\geq 0.

Choosing
\nu=\mu
, we get the first inequality in the statement (noticing from
(
3.45
) that
the bound also holds true in small time). Choosing

\nu=\nu_{\infty}
, we get the second claim.

Third Step.

We now show that

m^{n}(\cdot\,;\mu)
(whose dynamics are driven by
b^{n}
) satisfies
(
Erg
)
when

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
is small, which property we have just used to prove
(
3.48
)
and
(
3.49
). By Proposition

3.12
, we already know that it satisfies
(
Local
)

on any finite interval
[0,T]

w.r.t. constants that depend on
T
but are independent of
n

(since the quantities in
(
3.33
), with

b
replaced by
b^{n}
, are independent of
n
).
The point is mainly to establish
(
3.3
) for time indices
t
greater than or equal to

1
. In fact, by applying
(
3.3
) at fixed
t_{0}>0
, we already have a bound for

\|q(t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
, and by
the paragraph below
(
3.45
), we also know
that
\|m(t_{0}\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
is small
when

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
is small
and
n
and
t_{0}
are large.
The main goal is thus to prove
(
3.3
)
for
k=\alpha=0
under (
3.47
), which implies

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq 2a
.

For an initial condition
q_{0}
and for a source term as in
(
3.3
), we rewrite the solution of the equation

(3.50)

\partial_{t}q(t)-L^{n}_{m^{n}(t;\mu)}q(t)-r(t)=0,\quad\quad t\geq 0,

as
q(t):=q_{1}(t)+q_{2}(t)
with

\partial_{t}q_{1}(t)-L_{\nu_{\infty}}q_{1}(t)-r(t)=0,\quad t\geq 0\,;\quad q_{1}(0)=q_{0}\,;\quad\partial_{t}q_{2}(t)-L_{\nu_{\infty}}q_{2}(t)-r_{2}^{n}(t)=0,\quad t\geq 0\,;\quad q_{2}(0)=0,

where

r^{n}_{2}(t)=[L^{n}_{m^{n}(t;\mu)}-L_{\nu_{\infty}}]q(t)
.
By
(
Erg
)
(for
\nu_{\infty}
), it is easy to estimate

q_{1}(t)
. Next, we rewrite

r_{2}^{n}(t)
as

(3.51)

\begin{split}r^{n}_{2}(t)&=\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl(\bigl[b^{n}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)-b(\cdot,\nu_{\infty})\bigr]q(t)\Bigr)+\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl(\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t;\mu)\bigr)\bigl(q(t)\bigr)\bigl[m^{n}(t;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\bigr]\Bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\textrm{\rm div}\Bigl(\Bigl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,m^{n}(t;\mu)\bigr)\bigl(q(t)\bigr)-\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,\nu_{\infty})\bigl(q(t)\bigr)\Bigr]\nu_{\infty}\Bigr).\end{split}

The difficulty here is that we have no
L^{\infty}
estimates on
b^{n}-b
(since
b
may not be continuous in the spatial variable).
We proceed as follows.
For
\delta>0
, we
let
p_{n}(\delta):=\textrm{\rm Leb}_{d}(\{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}:|b^{n}(x,\nu_{\infty})-b(x,\nu_{\infty})|\geq\delta\})+\textrm{\rm Leb}_{2d}(\{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2d}:|[\delta b^{n}/\delta m](x,\nu_{\infty},y)-[\delta b/\delta m](x,\nu_{\infty},y)|\geq\delta\})
, which
tends to
0
as
n
tends to
\infty
(see (
3.14
)).
Then, for
\|\nabla\xi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
(and observing from (
3.42
) that
\nu_{\infty}
has a bounded density),

(3.52)

\displaystyle\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(x,\nu_{\infty}\bigr)-\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(x,\nu_{\infty})\Bigr]\bigl(q(t)\bigr)\cdot\nabla\xi(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\infty}(x)\biggr|

\displaystyle=\biggl|\biggl\langle\Bigl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,\nu_{\infty},\cdot\bigr)-\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,\nu_{\infty},\cdot)\Bigr],\bigl(\nabla\xi\cdot\nu_{\infty}\bigr)\otimes q(t)\biggr\rangle\biggr|\leq C\delta\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}+C\sup_{\|\zeta\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\|\zeta\|_{1}\leq p_{n}(\delta)}\bigl\langle q(t),\zeta\bigr\rangle,

with
\zeta
in the supremum being
a real-valued measurable function on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.
To obtain the second line right-above, we used the distributional version of Fubini’s theorem together with the fact that

\begin{split}&\biggl\langle\Bigl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,\nu_{\infty},\cdot\bigr)-\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,\nu_{\infty},\cdot)\Bigr],\bigl(\nabla\xi\cdot\nu_{\infty}\bigr)\otimes q(t)\biggr\rangle\\
&=\biggl\langle\Bigl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,\nu_{\infty},\cdot\bigr)-\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,\nu_{\infty},\cdot)\Bigr]{\mathbbm{1}}_{A_{n}},\bigl(\nabla\xi\cdot\nu_{\infty}\bigr)\otimes q(t)\biggr\rangle\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\biggl\langle\Bigl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(\cdot,\nu_{\infty},\cdot\bigr)-\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(\cdot,\nu_{\infty},\cdot)\Bigr]{\mathbbm{1}}_{A_{n}^{\complement}},\bigl(\nabla\xi\cdot\nu_{\infty}\bigr)\otimes q(t)\biggr\rangle,\end{split}

where we let
A_{n}:=\{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2d}:|[\delta b^{n}/\delta m](x,\nu_{\infty})(y)-[\delta b/\delta m](x,\nu_{\infty})(y)|\geq\delta\}
.
In order to get the very last term in
(
3.52
), we let

\zeta(x):=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{A_{n}^{\complement}}(y,x)\Bigl[\frac{\delta b^{n}}{\delta m}\bigl(y,\nu_{\infty},x\bigr)-\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(y,\nu_{\infty},x)\Bigr]\cdot\nabla\xi(y)\nu_{\infty}(\mathrm{d}y),

which satisfies
\|\zeta\|_{\infty}\leq C
and
\|\zeta\|_{1}\leq Cp_{n}(\delta)
. The claim
(
3.52
) easily follows.

Proceeding in the same way for the other two terms in the expression of

r^{n}_{2}(t)
,
we have
(using
the bound
\sup_{t\geq 0}\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq 2a
in order to handle the last term in the definition
(
3.51
)
of
r_{2}^{n}(t)
)

(3.53)

\begin{split}\bigl\|r^{n}_{2}(t)\bigr\|_{(1,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\bigl(\delta+\bigl\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\bigr)+C\sup_{\|\zeta\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\|\zeta\|_{1}\leq p_{n}(\delta)}\bigl\langle q(t),\zeta\bigr\rangle\\
&\leq C\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\bigl(\delta+a\bigr)+C\sup_{\|\zeta\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\|\zeta\|_{1}\leq p_{n}(\delta)}\bigl\langle q(t),\zeta\bigr\rangle.\end{split}

By
(
Erg
)
at
\nu_{\infty}
(applied twice,
once to
q_{1}
and once to
q_{2}
, see
the decomposition of (
3.50
) ), we get

\begin{split}&\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq Ce^{-\lambda t}\|q(0)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|r(s)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-\lambda(t-s)}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{\beta/2}}\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\delta+a\bigr)\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-\lambda(t-s)}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}s+C\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{\|\zeta\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\|\zeta\|_{1}\leq p_{n}(\delta)}\bigl\langle q(s),\zeta\bigr\rangle\frac{e^{-\lambda(t-s)}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

We now proceed as in the second step. We multiply by

e^{-\lambda(\tau-t)/2}/(\tau-t)^{1/2}
, for some
\tau>0
and then integrate
w.r.t
t\in[0,\tau]
.
Assuming w.l.o.g that
\delta+a
is small enough (compared to
1/C
),
we obtain

(3.54)

\begin{split}\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C\|q(0)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda t/2}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|r(s)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-\lambda(t-s)/2}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{\beta/2}}\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{\|\zeta\|_{\infty}\leq 1,\|\zeta\|_{1}\leq p_{n}(\delta)}\bigl\langle q(s),\zeta\bigr\rangle\frac{e^{-\lambda(t-s)/2}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Fourth Step.

The problem now is to handle the last term in
(
3.54
). This asks us to revisit the proof of
Proposition
3.12
.
For

t>0
, we consider the solution to
(
3.34
)
with
w(t,\cdot)=\zeta
as terminal condition, where

\|\zeta\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\|\zeta\|_{1}\leq p_{n}(\delta)
. Then,
we already have the two bounds
(
3.29
)
and
(
3.30
), but we can improve them using the assumption on
\|\zeta\|_{1}
.
Following
(
3.42
),
for
s\in[(t-1)\vee 0,t]
,
for any real
\varrho>1
,

\begin{split}\|w(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}&\leq C\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}} \zeta(y)g_{\delta_{x}}(t-s,y)\mathrm{d}y\leq C\|\zeta\|_{\varrho}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\|g_{\delta_{x}}(t-s,\cdot)\|_{\varrho/(\varrho-1)}\leq C_{\varrho}\frac{p_{n}(\delta)^{1/\varrho}}{(t-s)^{d/(2\varrho)}}.\end{split}

and then, by treating the case
s\leq t-1
by regarding
w(t-1,\cdot)
as a new initial condition, we deduce from
(
3.29
) that

\Big\|w(s,\cdot)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w(s,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\Big\|_{\infty}\leq C\frac{p_{n}(\delta)^{1/\varrho}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{d/(2\varrho)}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)},

first for
t-s\geq 1
and, then, for
t-s>0
.
Letting
\epsilon:=1/\varrho
and repeating
(
3.31
)
and
(
3.32
), we obtain

\|\nabla_{x}w(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq C\frac{p_{n}(\delta)^{\epsilon}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2+\epsilon d/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}.

Then,
assuming
1/2+\epsilon d/2<1
(which is always doable by choosing
\varrho
large enough)
and
expanding
(\langle w(s),q(s)\rangle)_{0\leq s\leq t}
as
in the proof of
Proposition

3.12
,
we can insert
the above estimate in the analysis of
T_{3}
in
(
3.37
)
(whereas (
3.36
)
and (
3.38
)
do not change).
We get

\begin{split}\big|T_{3}\big|&\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{x}w(s,\cdot)\|_{{\infty}}\|q(s)\|_{({0,\infty})^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq Cp_{n}(\delta)^{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2+\epsilon d/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

and then,

\begin{split}\langle\zeta,q(t)\rangle&\leq Ce^{-\lambda t}\|q(0)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{\beta/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\mathrm{d}s+Cp_{n}(\delta)^{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2+\epsilon d/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

We complete the proof by inserting the above estimate in
(
3.54
).
Proceeding as
in
(
3.48
) and choosing
n
large enough so that
p_{n}(\delta)
becomes small enough,
we get

(3.55)

\begin{split}\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C\|q(0)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda t/4}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|r(s)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-\lambda(t-s)/4}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{\beta/2}}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

which is
(
3.3
)
when

q(0)
is in
(W^{0,\infty}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))^{\prime}
.

Now, we must explain what happens when we just have a bound for

q(0)
in
(W^{k,\infty}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))^{\prime}
for some
k\in(0,2)
.
As we already said, we can apply (
3.55
) but for the dynamics restarted at some
small
t_{0}>0
. In fact,
t_{0}
can be chosen in some interval
[S/2,3S/2]
, for
S
small.
We then
have (
3.55
)
but with

\|q(0)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
replaced by

\|q(t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
and next, by averaging w.r.t.

t_{0}
,
we have (
3.55
)
but with
\|q(0)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}

replaced by
S^{-1}\int_{S/2}^{3S/2}\|q(t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}t_{0}
. It remains to see that, by applying Proposition

3.12

to (
3.50
), we already have a bound in finite time, which writes:

(3.56)

\begin{split}\|q(t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq\frac{C_{S}}{t_{0}^{k/2}}\|q(0)\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+C_{S}\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t_{0}-s)^{\beta/2}}\mathrm{d}s,\quad t_{0}\in\bigl[\frac{S}{2},\frac{3S}{2}\bigr],\end{split}

for a constant
C_{S}
depending on the time index
S
. By integrating w.r.t
t_{0}

over
[S/2,3S/2]
, we get

(3.57)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{S}\int_{S/2}^{3S/2}\|q(t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}t_{0}&\leq C_{S}\|q(0)\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+C_{S}\int_{0}^{3S/2}\|r(s)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

which can be inserted in
(
3.55
),
when

\|q(0)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}

therein is replaced by
S^{-1}\int_{S/2}^{3S/2}\|q(t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}t_{0}
.
This gives
(
3.3
)
for
t\geq S
.
The result, for
t<S
, is a direct consequence of

(
Local
)
.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

The reader will observe that the proof of Proposition

3.14

relies on a stability argument. As such, it
can be easily adapted to prove the following:
if the original dynamics
(
1.4
)
satisfies
(
Erg
)
at a given
\mu
and if

\tilde{b}
is another drift, satisfying

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
, such that

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}|(\tilde{b}-b)(x,\mu)|+\sup_{x,y\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\bigl|\frac{\delta\tilde{b}}{\delta m}(x,\mu,y)-\frac{\delta b}{\delta m}(x,\mu,y)\bigr|\leq\varepsilon,

for some
\varepsilon>0
, then, for
\varepsilon
small enough,
the dynamics driven by

\tilde{b}
satisfy
(
Erg
)
at
\mu
w.r.t. to constants that are independent of

\varepsilon
.
We used this observation in the proof of Proposition
3.10
.

Also, in the sequel, we sometimes refer to
(
3.45
)
as a stability estimate in finite time. Importantly, it
is proven by means of the sole assumption

(
Local
)

for the drift
b^{n}
, which follows from
Proposition
3.12
.
In particular, letting
n
tend to
\infty
in (
3.45
), we obtain
a bound for

\|m(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
, but it blows-up as
t
tends to
0
.
To make it clear,

\bigl\|m(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C_{\alpha,S}\frac{\|\mu-\nu\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}},\quad t\in(0,S].

To overcome this drawback, one may use the same norm on the two sides of the inequality. In fact, by the same argument,
we can easily show the following inequality:

(3.58)

\bigl\|m(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C_{\alpha,S}\|\mu-\nu\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}},\quad t\in[0,S],

where
\alpha>0
.
It suffices to return to
(
3.44
) and to estimate the left-hand side therein by means of

\| \cdot\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
.
Whereas, in the derivation of (
3.44
), we applied

(
Local
)

one
first time
with
k=\alpha
,

\mu-\nu
as initial condition and
0
as remainder
and with
\alpha
as parameter in
(
3.3
), we here replace
the latter choice of

\alpha
as parameter by
0
in
(
3.3
). This gives

\begin{split}\bigl\|m^{n}(t\,;\mu)-m(t\,;\nu)\bigr\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C_{\alpha,S}\|\mu-\nu\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}+C_{S}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bigl\|m^{n}(s\,;\mu)-m(s\,;\nu)\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}^{2}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{C_{\beta,S}}{n^{\beta}},\end{split}

from which
(
3.58
)
follows.

We can now complete:

The first part of the statement
of Theorem
3.1
(the case when
\nu_{\infty}
is a global attractor)
follows from the combination of
Propositions
3.10
and

3.14
, provided we can prove that
b^{n}
satisfies

(
Erg
)
at any
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
It suffices to notice that, by assumption,
there exists
t_{0}>0
such that, for any

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

\|m(t_{0},\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq a_{0}
,
for
a_{0}
as in the statement of Proposition
3.14
.
By
Proposition
3.14
itself,
the same holds with
m^{n}(t_{0},\mu)
instead of

m(t_{0},\mu)
, namely

\|m^{n}(t_{0},\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq a_{0}
,
for
n
large enough.
By Proposition
3.14
again,

(m^{n}(t\,;m^{n}(t_{0},\mu)))_{t\geq 0}

and
b^{n}

(with the former being also

((m^{n}(t+t_{0},\mu))_{t\geq 0}
)
satisfies
(
Erg
)
(for
n
large enough).
The proof of
(
Erg
)
for
b^{n}
at
\mu
and on the whole
[0,+\infty)
is then achieved as (the end of) the fourth step of the proof of Proposition

3.14
,
using in particular
(
3.56
)
and
(
3.57
).
The details are as follows.
For an initial condition
q_{0}
and for a source term as in
(
3.3
), we rewrite the solution (after time
t_{0}
)
of the equation

\partial_{t}q(t)-L^{n}_{m^{n}(t;\mu)}q(t)-r(t)=0,\quad\quad t\geq 0,

as

\partial_{t}q(t+t_{0})-L^{n}_{m^{n}(t;m^{n}(t_{0},\mu))}q(t+t_{0})-r(t+t_{0})=0,\quad\quad t\geq 0.

This yields

\begin{split}\|q(t+t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C\|q(t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda t}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|r(t_{0}+s)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-\lambda(t-s)}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{\beta/2}}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

for
t\geq 0
, or equivalently,

\begin{split}\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}&\leq C\|q(t_{0})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda(t-t_{0})}+C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\|r(s)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-\lambda(t-s)}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{\beta/2}}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

for
t\geq t_{0}
.
At this point, we can invoke
(
3.56
)
and
(
3.57
)
in order to conclude.

The remaining difficulty is to estimate
(in the statement of
Proposition
3.10
)

\Bigl|\Phi\bigl({\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t}^{n})}}\bigr)-\Phi\bigl({\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t})}}\bigr)\Bigr|.

Since
\delta\Phi/\delta m
is bounded, it suffices to
give a bound for

d_{\rm TV}({\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t}^{n})}},{\mathcal{L}}{{(X_{t})}})
, but this a consequence of
Proposition
3.14
again: when
t\leq t_{0}
, we get

Cn^{-\beta}

as a direct consequence
of
Remark

3.15
,
and this
without using

(
Erg
)
(for sure,
C
depends on
t_{0}
); when

t>t_{0}
, we get the same bound, but now using

(
Erg
)
and restarting the two dynamics (with
b
and
b^{n}
as respective drifts)
from
two initial conditions in
the neighbourhood of
\nu_{\infty}
, which framework fits
exactly
(
3.49
).
Here,
\beta
is a fixed parameter in
(0,1)
:
when

b
satisfies
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{\alpha,2}
))
,
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we choose
\beta=1-\alpha

and
n=N^{1/(1-\alpha)}
in
Proposition
3.10
;
when
b
just satisfies
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{0,2}
))
,
we choose
\beta=1-\epsilon
and
n=N
.
∎By combining
the last two statements with
Proposition

2.3
, we get the following result:

Corollary 3.16
.

Assume
that
b
satisfies

(
Reg
-
b
-(
{\eta,2}
))

for some
\eta\in[0,1)
.
Assume that there exists a measure
\nu_{\infty}
satisfying

(
Erg
)

that attracts the solutions
of
(
1.4
), uniformly w.r.t. the initial point. Then, there exists
\lambda>0
and, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists also a collection of constants
(C_{\delta})_{\delta\in[0,1)}
such that, for any
N\geq 1
,

\forall t\geq 0,\quad{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl\|\mu^{N}_{t}-\nu_{\infty}\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}^{2}\Bigr]\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{0}\Bigl(N^{-1}+e^{-\lambda t}\Bigr)&\textrm{\rm if}\quad\eta>0,\\
C_{\delta}\Bigl(N^{-1+\delta}+e^{-\lambda t}\Bigr)&\textrm{\rm if}\quad\eta=0,\quad\textrm{\rm for any}\ \delta>0.\end{array}\right.

It suffices to choose
\Phi
as in Proposition

2.3
and to observe from Proposition

3.14
that
(m(t\,;\mu))_{t\geq 0}
converges exponentially fast to
\nu_{\infty}
.
∎

3.5
Metastability

As in the second part of the statement of
Theorem
3.1
,
we now consider an invariant measure

\nu_{\infty}
to
(
1.4
)
that
satisfies
(
Erg
)

but that may not be a global attractor.
So is the case throughout the subsection.

We recall now the Markov property satisfied by the empirical distribution

(\mu^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, which takes values in
{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the collection of probability measures
\mu

that are uniformly distributed
on some finite state
\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}\}
, with
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\in({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}
.
The Markov property of

(\mu^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

follows from the exchangeable structure of the particle system
(
1.1
):
for any
permutation
\sigma
on
\{1,\cdots,N\}
,

(Y_{t}^{1,N},\cdots,Y_{t}^{N,N})_{t\geq 0}
starting from
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})

has the same law as

(Y_{t}^{1,N},\cdots,Y_{t}^{N,N})_{t\geq 0}
starting from
(x_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,x_{\sigma(N)})
. Accordingly,
for any (bounded measurable) test functional
\Phi:{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
,
the conditional expectation

{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi({\mu}_{t}^{N})|{\mathcal{F}}_{s}]

must be a symmetric function of
(Y^{1,N}_{s},\cdots,Y^{N,N}_{s})

and thus a function of
\mu^{N}_{s}
.
Details are as follows. Letting

u^{N}(t,{\bm{x}})={\mathbb{E}}[\Phi({\mu}_{t}^{N})|(Y_{0}^{1,N},\cdots,Y_{0}^{N,N})={\bm{x}}]
,
we
deduce from the exchangeable structure that

u^{N}(t,\cdot)
is symmetric. Also, since the dynamics
(
1.1
) are time-homogeneous,
we have

{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi({\mu}_{t}^{N})|{\mathcal{F}}_{s}]=u^{N}(t-s,Y_{s}^{1,N},\cdots,Y_{s}^{N,N})
. And then,

{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi({\mu}_{t}^{N})|{\mathcal{F}}_{s}]
is indeed a symmetric function of

(Y^{1,N}_{s},\cdots,Y^{N,N}_{s})
. The same argument holds when
s
is replaced by a stopping time.

Below, we often distinguish between two types of initial condition for

(\mu^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. Some properties are stated under the initial condition

\mu^{N}_{0}=\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}}:=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}
for some tuple

{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})\in({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}
, i.e.
{\bm{Y}}^{N}_{0}:=(Y^{1,N}_{0},\cdots,Y_{0}^{N,N})={\bm{x}}
, in which case we write the corresponding
probabilities (resp. expectations) in the form
{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|\,{\bm{Y}}^{N}_{0}={\bm{x}})

(resp.
{\mathbb{E}}[\cdot\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}]
). Alternatively,
we may work with a random initial condition

{\bm{Y}}^{N}_{0}\sim\nu^{\otimes N}
, for some
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, in which case we write
probabilities in the form
{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|\,{\bm{Y}}^{N}_{0}\sim\nu^{\otimes N})
,
and similarly for the expectations.
With these notations,
the expectation in
(
3.13
)
(main display in the statement of Proposition

3.10
) should be rewritten

\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})|{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}]
. Part of the
analysis below relies on the fact that
the arguments in Proposition

3.10
also hold true under
the initial condition

{\bm{Y}}^{N}_{0}={\bm{x}}
.
We clarify this in the text.
Here is a primer.
The first step in the adaptation of
Proposition

3.10

to the case

{\bm{Y}}^{N}_{0}={\bm{x}}

is to come back to (
2.6
).
We throw away the first term in the right-hand side of the identity and
we just retain the following simpler version of
(
2.6
):

\begin{split}\Phi(\mu^{N}_{{t}})-\mathcal{U}(t,\mu^{N}_{{0}})&=\mathcal{U}(0,\mu^{N}_{{t}})-\mathcal{U}(t,\mu^{N}_{{0}}).\end{split}

Under the probability

{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|\,{\bm{Y}}^{N}_{0}={\bm{x}})
,

\mu^{N}_{{0}}
is obviously equal to
\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N}
and the left-hand side is equal to

\Phi(\mu^{N}_{{t}})-\mathcal{U}(t,\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N})
. Then,
the
display
(
2.8
)
becomes useless and one may just focus on
(
2.7
).
The objective is then to give an upper bound
for
|{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{{t}})|Y_{0}^{N}]={\bm{x}}]-\mathcal{U}^{n}(t,\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N})|
by adapting the proof of
Proposition
3.10

(we here use
\mathcal{U}^{n}
and not
\mathcal{U}
to be consistent with the statement of
Proposition

3.10
, in which
\Phi
is computed at
{\mathcal{L}}(X_{t}^{n})
). In the proof,
(
3.19
) is clearly useless, whereas
(
3.21
) remains necessary.
As for the term

T_{\textrm{\rm add}}(t)

in
(
3.18
), the analysis can be carried out in a similar way, except for one main thing:
in the third step of the proof of
Proposition
3.10
, one can no longer invoke the exchangeability property of the particle system.
Instead, one must estimate
the summand in
(
3.23
)
for each pair
(i,j)
of indices with
i\not=j
. Proceeding as in the fourth step of the proof, one gets
the two bounds
(
3.25
)
and
(
3.26
)
for any
i\not=j
. The end of the proof is similar. We retrieve the same bound
as in
Proposition
3.10

but for

|{\mathbb{E}}[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{{t}})|Y_{0}^{N}]={\bm{x}}]-\mathcal{U}^{n}(t,\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N})|
.

Lemma 3.17
.

For any integer
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, any

\alpha,\epsilon\in(0,1)
and any

t\geq 0
, there exists a constant
C
such that,
for any integer
N\geq 1
, any
probability measure
\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

and any tuple
{\bm{x}}\in({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\|\mu_{t}^{N}-m(t\,;\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}^{p}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr]+{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\|\mu_{t}^{N}-m(t\,;\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}})\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}^{p}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}\Bigr]\leq CN^{-p/2+\epsilon}.

We first introduce the following mollification of the norm

\|\cdot\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}
, letting, for some
k>0
,

(3.59)

\aleph_{k}(q)=\sum_{|{\bm{n}}|\leq k}\frac{1}{(1+|{\bm{n}}|^{2})^{s}}|q^{\bm{n}}|^{2},\quad q\in W^{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}),

which definition is very similar to
(
2.12
).
The role of this mollification is very similar to the
role of the mollification used in
the last step of the proof of Proposition

3.10
.
The key point is that all the estimates below are independent of
k
, which allows us to send

k
to
\infty
in the end.
Intuitively, this comes from the fact that
the function

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\ni\mu\mapsto\aleph_{k}(\mu-\nu_{0})
, for some
\nu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,
satisfies the conclusion of Proposition

2.3
uniformly in
k
(taking into account the fact that the sum is truncated in the definition of
\aleph
).
By the way,
it is easy to notice that

\aleph_{k}(q)\rightarrow\|q\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}^{2}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
.
In the rest of the proof (except when indicated),
k
is fixed. For this reason, we omit it in the notation

\aleph_{k}
and we just write
\aleph
.

With this convention, and for given
t_{0}>0
,
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, we let

(3.60)

\Phi_{p}^{n}(\mu):=\Bigl[\aleph\Bigl(\mu-m^{n}(t_{0}\ ;\nu)\Bigr)\Bigr]^{p},

with

(m^{n}(t\,;\nu))_{t\geq 0}

solving
(
1.4
)
when the latter is driven by
\tilde{b}^{n}
, for

\tilde{b}^{n}
as in the proof of Proposition

3.10
, see in particular (
3.15
).
The reason why we force

(m^{n}(t\,;\mu))_{t\geq 0}
to be driven by

\tilde{b}^{n}
and not by
b^{n}

(which is what is done in the proof of Proposition

3.10
)
is explained in the second step below. Moreover,
the precise choice of
\nu
is specified next.
Following (
2.4
),
we call
{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{n}(t,\mu):=\Phi_{p}^{n}(m^{n}(t\,;\mu))
.
We first establish the bound for the second term in the main inequality of the statement,
but with
\|\cdot\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}
replaced by
\aleph
.
As the resulting bound is shown to be independent of
k
, this also implies
the expected bound for the second term in the statement.

First Step.

We start with
p=1
.
Then,
for another integer
q\in{\mathbb{N}}

(with
(1-\epsilon)q>1
)
and

n=N^{q}

in Proposition

3.10
(so that
K^{N^{q}}_{\epsilon}={\mathcal{O}}(N^{\epsilon q})
), the same proof as therein
(but just using
(
2.7
) without invoking
(
2.8
) and using
(
Local
)
instead of
(
Erg
)
) yields

\begin{split}\Bigl|{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl\|\mu^{N}_{t_{0}}-m^{N^{q}}(t_{0}\,;\nu)\bigr\|^{2}_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}-{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{N^{q}}({t_{0}},\mu_{0}^{N})\Bigr]\Bigr|&\leq\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}\Bigl|{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{N^{q}}(t,\mu_{t}^{N})-{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\mu_{0}^{N})\Bigr]\Bigr|\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,q,t_{0}}}{N^{1-{(q+1)}\epsilon}}.\end{split}

(The constant right above also depends on
\alpha
, but we feel useless to indicate the dependence on
\alpha
, which is fixed.)
Replacing
\epsilon
by
\epsilon/(q+1)
, choosing
\nu=\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N}
for some

{\bm{x}}\in({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}
, working under
the initial condition
\mu^{N}_{0}=\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}}
(so that

{\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\mu_{0}^{N})=0
)
and using in addition
Remark

3.15

to compare
m^{N^{q}}(t_{0}\,;\nu)
and

m(t_{0}\,;\nu)
,
we get the second inequality in the statement with
p=1
.

Second Step.

We now work with an integer
p\geq 2
.
We repeat the proof of
Proposition

3.10
, with
n=N^{q}
in
(
3.25
)
and
(
3.26
), except that
we do not upper bound the left-hand side in
(
3.21
).
We obtain

(3.61)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}\Bigl|{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-t,\mu^{N}_{t})-{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\mu^{N}_{0})\bigr]\Bigr|\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,q,t_{0}}}{N^{{q(1-\epsilon)}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\biggl|\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg(\partial_{(y_{2})_{i}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{N^{q}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t_{0}-s,\mu^{N}_{s},z,z)\bigg)\,\mu^{N}_{s}(\mathrm{d}z)\bigg]\biggr|\,\mathrm{d}s+{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\varepsilon\bigr).

In the right-hand side, the last term on the first line
corresponds to
(
3.25
)
and
(
3.26
). The last term on the second line corresponds to
the remainder term in
(
3.23
),
with
\varepsilon
as in
(
3.15
).
As for the first term on the second line, it corresponds to
(
2.7
).

Now, we
use
Proposition

3.6

to represent

[\partial_{(y_{2})_{i}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{N^{q}}/\delta m^{2}](t_{0}-s,\mu,\cdot,\cdot)

in the right-hand side.
As made clear in the latter statement, these derivatives can be represented in terms of

[\delta^{2}\Phi_{p}^{{N^{q}}}/\delta m^{2}](m^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-s\,;\mu),\cdot,\cdot)

and

[\delta\Phi_{p}^{{N^{q}}}/\delta m](m^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-s\,;\mu),\cdot)

and

d^{(1)}_{i}
and
d^{(2)}_{i,j}

in Propositions

3.4

and

3.5
.
Then,
by
Proposition

2.3
, we
observe that, for any

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

(3.62)

\begin{split}&\Bigl\|\frac{\delta\Phi^{{N^{q}}}_{p}}{\delta m}(\mu,\cdot)\Bigr\|_{\alpha/4,\infty}\leq C_{p}\Phi_{p-1}^{N^{q}}\bigl(\mu\bigr)\sqrt{\Phi^{N^{q}}\bigl(\mu\bigr)},\quad\Bigl\|\frac{\delta^{2}\Phi^{{N^{q}}}_{p}}{\delta m^{2}}(\mu,\cdot,\cdot)\Bigr\|_{\alpha/4,\infty}\leq C_{p}\Phi_{p-1}^{N^{q}}\bigl(\mu\bigr).\end{split}

Moreover,

d^{(1)}_{i}
and
d^{(2)}_{i,j}

in Propositions

3.4

and

3.5

can be estimated by means of
(
Local
)
.
Similar to
(
3.20
), we get, for
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

(3.63)

\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\sup_{y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg|(\partial_{y_{1}})_{i}(\partial_{y_{1}})_{j}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}^{N^{q}}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu,y_{1},y_{2})\bigg|\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}{(1+K_{\epsilon}^{N^{q}})}}{1\wedge t^{1-\alpha/4}}\Phi_{p-1}^{N^{q}}\bigl(m^{N^{q}}(t\,;\mu)\bigr).

Then,
choosing as before
\nu=\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N}

in the definition of
\aleph
and noticing
from the fact that

(m^{N^{q}}(t\,;\cdot))_{t\geq 0}

is
driven by
\tilde{b}^{N^{q}}

that

\Phi^{{N^{q}}}_{p-1}(m^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-s\,;\mu))={\mathcal{U}}_{p-1}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-s,\mu)
,
for

0\leq s\leq t_{0}
,
(
3.61
)
yields

(3.64)

\begin{split}&\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-t,\mu^{N}_{t})\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}}{N^{{q(1-\epsilon)}}}+\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}}{N}\bigl(1+K_{\epsilon}^{N^{q}}\bigr)\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{U}}_{p-1}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-s,\mu^{N}_{s})\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}]}{1\wedge(t_{0}-s)^{1-\alpha/4}}\,\mathrm{d}s+{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\varepsilon\bigr).\end{split}

We then proceed by induction on
p
,
assuming that
{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{U}}_{p-1}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-t,\mu^{N}_{t})\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}]\leq C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}N^{-p+1+\epsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
,
for any
q>p
(here, the shape of
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)

does not really matter because
\varepsilon
is sent to to
0
in the end, but a careful inspection would show that it is of the form

|{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)|\leq C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}\varepsilon
).
We get, for
q>p
,

\begin{split}&\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-t,\mu^{N}_{t})\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}\Bigr]\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}}{N^{{q(1-\epsilon)}}}+\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}}{N^{p-\epsilon}}\bigl(1+K_{\epsilon}^{N^{q}}\bigr)+C_{\alpha,\epsilon,p,t_{0}}\varepsilon.\end{split}

Recalling that
q>p
and that

|K_{\epsilon}^{N^{q}}|=|{\mathcal{O}}(N^{\epsilon q})|\leq C_{\epsilon}N^{q\epsilon}

and changing the value of
\epsilon
in terms of
q
,
we prove the induction hypothesis at rank
p
. Choosing

t=t_{0}
,
we get

{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\Bigl[\aleph\Bigl(\mu_{t_{0}}^{N}-m^{N^{q}}(t_{0}\,;\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}})\Bigr)\Bigr]^{p}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}\Bigr]\leq C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}N^{-p+\epsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon).

Letting
\varepsilon
tend to
0
, then
k
to
\infty
and finally
q
to
\infty
, we complete the proof
of the bound of the second term in the main inequality of the statement.

Third Step.

We now complete the proof of the bound of the first term in the main inequality of the statement.
We rewrite the conclusion of the second step in the form

{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl\|\mu_{t_{0}}^{N}-m^{{N^{q}}}(t_{0},\mu_{0}^{N})\bigr\|^{2p}_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\Bigr]\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,t_{0}}}{N^{p-\epsilon}}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon),

which follows by conditioning on the value of
Y_{0}^{N}
.
It thus remains to bound

(3.65)

{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl\|m^{{{N^{q}}}}(t_{0},\mu_{0}^{N})-m^{{{N^{q}}}}(t_{0},\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})\bigr\|^{2p}_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr].

With the same notation as in the previous step, the strategy is to bound

(3.66)

{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\Bigl[\aleph_{k}\Bigl(m^{{{N^{q}}}}(t_{0},\mu_{0}^{N})-m^{{{N^{q}}}}(t_{0},\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})\Bigr)\Bigr]^{p}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr],

independently of the parameter
k
. We rewrite the above quantity as

{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\mu_{0}^{N})\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}]
when
\nu=\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}
in the definition of
\Phi_{p}

in (
3.60
).
When
p=1
, it suffices to use
(
2.8
), which directly says that
(
3.66
)
is bounded by

C_{t_{0}}/N
.
When
p\geq 2
,
we need a bound for
the second-order
derivatives
[\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p}/\delta m^{2}](t_{0},\cdot)
(as the latter appears
in the formula
(
2.8
)). Proceeding as in the second step
(the framework being simpler since there is no derivatives in
(y_{1})_{i}
and

(y_{2})_{i}
),
we have the following analogue of
(
3.63
):

(3.67)

\Bigl\|\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p}}{\delta m^{2}}(t_{0},\mu,\cdot,\cdot)\Bigr\|_{\infty}\leq C_{p}{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}(t_{0},\mu),\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}).

Back to the formula
(
2.8
), we thus must compute

{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}})
.
By the definition
(
3.59
) of
\aleph
, we obtain
(with the same notation as in the formula)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}\bigr)=\Phi^{{N^{q}}}_{p-1}\bigl(m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}})\bigr)

(3.68)

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\aleph\Bigl(m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}})-m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})\Bigr)\Bigr]^{p-1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\Bigl(\Bigl[\aleph\Bigl(m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}})-m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})\Bigr)\Bigr]^{p-1}+\Phi^{{N^{q}}}_{p-1}\bigl(m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\Bigl(\bigl\|m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}})-m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})\bigr\|^{2(p-1)}_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}+{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\Bigr).

By
(
Local
)
(which holds true for
\tilde{b}^{N^{q}}
w.r.t. constants that are independent of
N

and which enforces a form of stability, see (
3.45
)) and by Sobolev embedding (which says that

\|\cdot\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq\|\cdot\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
),

(3.69)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}\bigr)&\leq C_{p,t_{0}}\Bigl(\bigl\|\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}-\bigl(s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}^{2(p-1)}+{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\Bigr)\\
&\leq C_{p,t_{0}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N^{2(p-1)}}+{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\Bigr).\end{split}

Therefore, by formulas
(
2.8
) and
(
3.67
),

(3.70)

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p}\bigl(t_{0},\mu_{0}^{N}\bigr)\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq C_{p,t_{0}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N^{2p-1}}+\frac{1}{N}\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr]\Bigr).\end{split}

In fact, we can easily replace in the analysis

\mu_{0}^{N}
by any
\lambda\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}
. This amounts to repeat the computation with

\mathcal{U}^{{N^{q}}}_{p}(t_{0},\lambda\mu+(1-\lambda)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})

in place of

\mathcal{U}^{{N^{q}}}_{p}(t_{0},\mu)
in formula
(
2.8
), which is licit thanks to

[
25
, Thm. 2.14]
.
Then, the bound
(
3.67
)
with
\mu
replaced by

\lambda\mu+(1-\lambda)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}
remains true.
Denoting by

\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}[\lambda]
the
corresponding version of

\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}

in
(
2.8
), i.e.,

\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}[\lambda]:=\frac{ss_{1}}{N}(\delta_{\tilde{\eta}}-\delta_{{\eta_{1}}})+{\mu_{\text{init}}}+s\lambda(\mu^{N}_{0}-{\mu_{\text{init}}}),\quad\quad s,s_{1}\in[0,1],

we can repeat
(
3.68
), which yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}[\lambda]\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\Bigl(\bigl\|m^{N^{q}}\bigl(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}[\lambda]\bigr)-m^{N^{q}}(t_{0},s\lambda\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s\lambda)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})\bigr\|^{2(p-1)}_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},s\lambda\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s\lambda)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\Bigr).

And then,
(
3.69
)
becomes

\begin{split}{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},\tilde{\mu}^{N}_{s,s_{1}}[\lambda]\bigr)&\leq C_{p,t_{0}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N^{2(p-1)}}+{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},s\lambda\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s\lambda)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\Bigr).\end{split}

In the end, we can replace
\mu_{0}^{N}
in the left-hand side of
(
3.70
)
by
\lambda\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}
.
Taking the supremum over
\lambda
, we obtain

\begin{split}&\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq 1}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p}\bigl(t_{0},\lambda\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq C_{p,t_{0}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N^{2(p-1)}}+\frac{1}{N}\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}^{N^{q}}_{p-1}\bigl(t_{0},s\mu_{0}^{N}+(1-s)\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\bigr)\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr]\Bigr).\end{split}

We deduce by induction (on
p
) that
the left-hand side is less than
C_{p,t_{0}}N^{-p}
.
∎

We now provide metastability properties of an invariant measure

\nu_{\infty}

satisfying
(
Erg
)
. Before we state the results, we claim that, from
Proposition

3.14
, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists
a>0
such that, for

(3.71)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a}\bigl\|m(t\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}=0.

The proof relies on the following observation. Fix
\delta>0
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
,
we can find
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}>0
such that,
for any
\varphi
in the unit ball of
W^{(\delta,\infty)}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,
there exists
\psi\in W^{((d+\alpha)/2,\infty)}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
with

\|\psi\|_{(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq\|\psi\|_{(d+\alpha)/2,\infty}\leq{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}}
and

\|\varphi-\psi\|_{0,\infty}\leq\varepsilon
,
which proves that, for any distribution
q
,

(3.72)

\|q\|_{(\delta,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq\varepsilon\|q\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{\varepsilon}\|q\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}.

Display (
3.71
) follows by choosing
\varepsilon
small and by noting that

\|q\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq 2
when
q
is the difference of two probability measures.
Therefore, for
a_{\alpha}
as in
Proposition

3.14
,
we can find
a\in(0,a_{\alpha})
such that

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/{2},2}\leq a

implies

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq a_{\alpha}
, which allows one to use Proposition

3.14
.

Corollary 3.18
.

Consider an invariant measure

\nu_{\infty}
to
(
1.4
)
at which
(
Erg
)
holds (for the drift
b
) and, for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let

a
be as in (
3.71
).
Then, for
any integer
p\geq 1
,
there exist
\varepsilon>0
and a constant
C_{p}
such that,
for any integer
N\geq 1
,
any
\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
with

\|\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq\varepsilon

and any
{\bm{x}}_{0}\in({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}

with

\|\mu_{{\bm{x}}_{0}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq\varepsilon
,

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(A^{(N)}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr)+{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(A^{(N)}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}_{0}\Bigr)\leq\frac{C_{p}}{N^{p}},\quad{\rm with}\ \ A^{(N)}:=\Bigl\{\sup_{t\leq N^{p}}\|\mu_{t}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq\frac{a}{2}\Bigr\}.\end{split}

First Step.

For
a
as in (
3.71
),
we can find
t_{0}>0
such that

\sup_{\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a}\|m(t_{0}\,;\mu)-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a/2
.
Then,
for any integer

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
we
let

A_{k}:=\{\|\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq 3a/4\}
. For
k\geq 2
,
and
with
{\mathbb{P}}
being implicitly understood as

{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N})

or

{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}_{0})
,
we
have

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(A_{k}\bigr)\leq{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(A_{k}\,|\,A_{k-1}^{\complement}\bigr){\mathbb{P}}\bigl(A_{k-1}^{\complement}\bigr)+{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(A_{k-1}\bigr).\end{split}

By
the homogeneous
Markov
structure of
(\mu_{t}^{N})_{t\geq 0}
, we
have

\begin{split}{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(A_{k}\,|\,A_{k-1}^{\complement}\Bigr)&\leq\sup_{{\bm{x}}:\|\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq 3a/4}{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\bigl\|\mu_{t_{0}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq 3a/4\Bigr\}\,|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}}\Bigr).\end{split}

For
{\bm{x}}
as in the supremum right above,

\|m(t_{0}\,;\,\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}})-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a/2
(by definition of
a
). Therefore,

\begin{split}{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(A_{k}\,|\,A_{k-1}^{\complement}\Bigr)&\leq\sup_{{\bm{x}}\in({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\bigl\|\mu_{t_{0}}^{N}-m(t_{0}\,;\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N})\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq a/4\Bigr\}\,|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N}\Bigr)\leq\frac{C_{p}}{N^{p}},\end{split}

with the second bound following from
Lemma
3.17
.
Provided that
\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}
or
\mu_{{\bm{x}}_{0}}^{N}
in the statement is close enough to

\nu_{\infty}
, we can handle

{\mathbb{P}}(A_{1})
in a similar way and prove it to be less than
C_{p}/N^{p}
. By iteration,
we obtain

{\mathbb{P}}(A_{k})\leq kC_{p}/N^{p}
.

Second Step.

For
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t_{0}
as above, we now compute

{\mathbb{P}}(\{\|\mu_{t}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq 3a/4\})

for
t\in[kt_{0},(k+1)t_{0}]

under the assumption that

\|\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}

(resp.

\|\mu^{N}_{{\bm{x}}_{0}}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}
)
is small enough
and under the initial condition

{\bm{Y}}_{0}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}
(resp.

{\bm{Y}}_{0}={\bm{x}}_{0}
).
By Sobolev embedding
and
by the stability property
(
3.58
),
there exists a real
\delta(\alpha)\in(0,1)
such that

\begin{split}\bigl\|\mu_{t}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}&\leq\bigl\|\mu_{t}^{N}-m(t-kt_{0}\,;\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N})\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}+\bigl\|m(t-kt_{0}\,;\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N})-\nu_{\infty}\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\\
&\leq\bigl\|\mu_{t}^{N}-m(t-kt_{0}\,;\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N})\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}+\bigl\|m(t-kt_{0}\,;\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N})-m(t-kt_{0}\,;\nu_{\infty})\bigr\|_{(\delta(\alpha),\infty)^{\prime}}\\
&\leq\bigl\|\mu_{t}^{N}-m(t-kt_{0}\,;\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N})\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}+C_{t_{0}}\bigl\|\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\bigr\|_{(\delta(\alpha),\infty)^{\prime}},\end{split}

where we used

m(t-kt_{0}\,;\nu_{\infty})=\nu_{\infty}
in the second line.
By
(
3.72
), we can find
a^{\prime}\in(0,a)
such that

\|\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a^{\prime}

implies

C_{t_{0}}\|\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\delta(\alpha),\infty)^{\prime}}\leq a/4
.
Then, proceeding as in the first step, Markov property yields

\begin{split}{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\|\mu_{t}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq 3a/4\Bigr\}\Bigr)&\leq{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\bigl\|\mu_{kt_{0}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq a^{\prime}\Bigr\}\Bigr)\\
&\hskip-10.0pt+\sup_{{\bm{x}}\in({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\bigl\|\mu_{t-kt_{0}}^{N}-m(t-kt_{0}\,;\mu_{{\bm{x}}}^{N})\bigr\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq a/2\Bigr\}\,|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu^{N}_{{\bm{x}}}\Bigr).\end{split}

The term on the second line is handled by means of
Lemma

3.17

whilst the term on the first line is treated by means of the first step, by modifying the value of

a
. We get, for
k\geq 1
and
t\in[kt_{0},(k+1)t_{0})
,

{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\|\mu_{t}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq 3a/4\Bigr\}\Bigr)\leq\frac{C_{p}(k+1)}{N^{p}}.

If
k=0
, we can proceed in a similar way in order to upper bound

{\mathbb{P}}(\{\|\mu_{t}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq 3a/4\})

for
t\in[0,t_{0}]
. One just needs to assume that

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}
(resp.

\|\mu^{N}_{{\bm{x}}_{0}}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}
) is small enough.

Third Step.

We now complete the proof. For
p
as above, with
p/2
even,
and we consider the mesh
(s_{j}=j/N)_{0\leq j\leq{N^{p/4}}}
. Then,
as a consequence of the second step,

{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\bigcup_{j=1}^{N^{{p/4}}}\Bigl\{\|\mu_{s_{j}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq 3a/4\Bigr\}\biggr){\leq}\frac{C_{p}}{N^{{p/2}}}.

Now, back to (
1.1
), it is standard to prove that, for each

j\in\{0,\cdots,N^{{p/4}}-1\}
,

\frac{1}{N}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\sup_{s_{j}\leq s\leq s_{j+1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|Y^{i,N}_{s}-Y^{i,N}_{s_{j}}|^{p}\Bigr]\leq\frac{C_{p}}{N^{p/2}},

from which we deduce that

{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\sup_{s_{j}\leq s\leq s_{j+1}}\|\mu_{s}^{N}-\mu_{s_{j}}^{N}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}^{p}\Bigr]\leq{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\sup_{s_{j}\leq s\leq s_{j+1}}\|\mu_{s}^{N}-\mu_{s_{j}}^{N}\|_{(\delta(\alpha),\infty)^{\prime}}^{p}\Bigr]\leq\Bigl(\frac{C_{p}}{N^{p/2}}\Bigr)^{\delta(\alpha)},

and then, we easily deduce that (changing
p
into
2p/\delta(\alpha)
in the above bound)

{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\sup_{s_{j}\leq s\leq s_{j+1}}\|\mu_{s}^{N}-\mu_{s_{j}}^{N}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq\frac{a}{4}\Bigr\}\Bigr)\leq\frac{C_{p}}{N^{p}},

and then

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}\biggl({\bigcup_{j=1}^{N^{{p/4}}}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\|\mu_{s_{j}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq 3a/4\Bigr\}\cup\Bigl\{\sup_{s_{j}\leq s\leq s_{j+1}}\|\mu_{s}^{N}-\mu_{s_{j}}^{N}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq a/4\Bigr\}\Bigr)\biggr)\leq{\frac{C_{p}}{N^{p/2}}}\leq{\frac{C_{p}}{N^{p/4}}},\end{split}

from which the result easily follows (changing
p/4
into
p
).
∎

We deduce the following statement, which says that
for any polynomial time in
N
,
the empirical measure stays at distance of order
N^{-1/2}

(for
\|\cdot\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}
) to the solution of the Fokker-Planck equation.

Theorem 3.19
.

Consider an invariant measure

\nu_{\infty}
to
(
1.4
)
at which
(
Erg
)
holds (for the drift
b
