). Then, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
there exists

a>0
such that, for any
p\geq 1
and
\epsilon>0
, there is a constant

C
satisfying, for any integer
N
,

(3.73)

\begin{split}&\sup_{\|\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a}\,\sup_{t\leq N^{p}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\|\mu_{t}^{N}-m(t\,;\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}})\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}^{p}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}\sim\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}^{\otimes N}\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\sup_{{\bm{x}}:\|\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a}\,\sup_{t\leq N^{p}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\|\mu_{t}^{N}-m(t\,;\mu^{N}_{\bm{x}})\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}^{p}\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}\Bigr]\leq CN^{-p/2+\epsilon}.\end{split}

The main idea is to localise
the arguments in
(
2.9
) (the proof of which relies on a semi-martingale expansion)
and Proposition

3.10
, using the stopping time
\tau^{N}:=\inf\{s>0:\|\mu_{s}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\geq a\}\wedge t_{0}
, where

t_{0}
is a fixed time in
[0,N^{p}]
that plays the role of

t
in
(
2.9
)
and

a
is chosen in such a way that
(
Erg
)
holds true for

\mu
satisfying

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a
(and for the dynamics driven by the mollified drifts
\tilde{b}^{n}
, for

n\geq N
).
Indeed, by
(
3.72
), we can render

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
small enough by choosing

a
small enough.
Then, Proposition

3.14

and Remark

3.15

guarantee that, for
N
large enough and
n\geq N
,

\tilde{b}^{n}
satisfies

(
Erg
)
holds at any
\mu
satisfying

\|\mu-\nu_{\infty}\|_{(\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}
small enough and thus for
a
small enough.

The next step is to follow the proof of Lemma

3.17

(with the same notation for
{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}
), recalling that we are now looking for constants independent of
t_{0}

(and also of
k
in (
3.59
)). In this respect,
(
Erg
)
is crucial.
Indeed,
thanks to the localisation,

\mu^{N}_{s}
stays in the basin where
(
Erg
)
holds true as long as

s\leq\tau^{N}
.
In the end,
by expanding

{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-s,\mu_{s}^{N})
in (
2.7
)
for
s
between
0
and
t\wedge\tau^{N}
,
(
3.61
)
(with
(
3.60
))
becomes

\begin{split}&\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}\Bigl|{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}\bigl(t_{0}-t\wedge\tau^{N},\mu^{N}_{t\wedge\tau^{N}}\bigr)-{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0},\mu^{N}_{0})\bigr]\Bigr|\\
&\leq\frac{C_{{\epsilon},p,q}}{N^{{q(1-\epsilon)}}}\bigl(1+K_{\epsilon}^{N^{q}}\bigr)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{t_{0}\wedge\tau^{N}}\biggl|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigg(\partial_{(y_{2})_{i}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\frac{\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{N^{q}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t_{0}-s,\mu^{N}_{s})(z,z)\bigg)\,\mu^{N}_{s}(\mathrm{d}z)\biggr|\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}s+{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\varepsilon\bigr),\end{split}

where
q>p
.
The first key point is that

{\mathbb{P}}(\{\tau^{N}<t_{0}\})\leq{C_{p}N^{-p}}
,
which follows from Corollary

3.18
.
The second one is that, in
the above right-hand side, the constant
C_{\epsilon,p,q}
is independent of
t_{0}
, which follows from
(
3.25
)
and
(
3.26
)
together with the fact that
all the involved derivatives of
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{N^{q}}

feature an extra exponential decay (in time) at any
probability measure belonging to the
basin where
(
Erg
)
holds true.
In particular, so is the case when those derivatives are computed at
\mu_{s}^{N}
for
s\leq\tau^{N}
. As a result, and this is our third point,
the available bounds for the derivatives

[\partial_{(y_{2})_{i}}\partial_{(y_{1})_{i}}\delta^{2}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{N^{q}}/\delta m^{2}](t_{0}-s,\mu^{N}_{s},\cdot,\cdot)

(that appear on the second line) also
feature an extra exponential decay
\exp(-\lambda(t_{0}-s))
.

By
inserting this extra exponential decay in
(
3.63
), we get the following variant of
(
3.64
)
(with
\nu=\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N}
in
(
3.60
) and
\|\mu_{\bm{x}}^{N}-\nu_{\infty}\|_{-(d+\alpha)/2,2}\leq a
):

(3.74)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-t,\mu^{N}_{t})\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,q}}{N^{q(1-\epsilon)}}+\frac{C_{\epsilon,p,q}}{N}\bigl(1+K_{\epsilon}^{N^{q}}\bigr)\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{U}}_{p-1}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-s,\mu^{N}_{s})\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}]}{1\wedge(t_{0}-s)^{1-\alpha/4}}e^{-\lambda(t_{0}-s)}\,\mathrm{d}s+{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\varepsilon\bigr).

By induction,
we get
{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{U}}_{p}^{N^{q}}(t_{0}-t,\mu^{N}_{t})\,|\,{\bm{Y}}_{0}^{N}={\bm{x}}]\leq C_{\epsilon,p}N^{-p+\epsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)
,
with
|{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon)|\leq C_{\epsilon,p,q}\varepsilon
.
Together with Proposition

3.14
,
this yields the bound for the second term in (
3.73
).
The bound for the first term in (
3.73
) is obtained as in the third step of the proof of
Lemma

3.17
, except that we use
(
Erg
)
instead of

(
Local
)
in
(
3.69
).
By

(
Erg
)
,
the right-hand side of
(
3.67
)
features an extra exponential decay
and the two constants

C_{p,t_{0}}
in (
3.69
)
and
(
3.70
)
are independent of
t_{0}
, which suffices to conclude.
∎

We can now complete:

To prove the second part of
of Theorem
3.1
(when
\nu_{\infty}
is not a global attractor), it suffices to repeat the localisation argument used in
(
3.74
), except that

\Phi
in
(
3.60
)
is now taken as a general test functional satisfying
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
and that

p
is implicitly taken as
1
(in clear, there is no induction, which makes the proof very much simpler).
As in the proof of
Theorem

3.19
, the key point is to use Corollary

3.18

in order to upper bound

{\mathbb{P}}(\{\tau^{N}<t_{0}\})
by
C_{q}N^{-q}
,
for any
q\geq 1
.
∎

3.6
Examples

3.6.1
Small case interaction

Proposition 3.20
.

Assume that

b
satisfies
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{\eta,2}
))
, for some
\eta\in[0,1)
.
Then, there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0

(
only depending on
\sup_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\|b(\cdot,m)\|_{0,\infty})

such that
(
3.4
)
holds true
if

(3.75)

\sup_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\Bigl\|\frac{\delta{b}}{\delta m}(\cdot,m)(\cdot)\Bigr\|_{0,\infty}<\epsilon_{0}.

The strategy is just to repeat the proof of finite in time result, see Proposition
3.12
(with
b
replaced by
b^{n}
, with

b^{n}
as in the proof of Proposition
3.10
), and then to apply
Proposition
3.10
, noticing that
the constant
C_{\alpha,b}

in
(
3.37
)
(which derives from the analysis of
T_{3}
in (
3.35
))
can be made small if

\epsilon_{0}
in (
3.75
)
is small
(which in turn implies that

\sup_{m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\|[\delta b^{n}/\delta m](\cdot,m)(\cdot)\|_{0,\infty}<\epsilon_{0}
).
We then conclude
as in the proof of
(
3.54
) that each
b^{n}
satisfies
(
Erg
)
(for any probability measure
\mu
): the role played by

\delta+a
in
(
3.54
) and the line before
is here played by
\epsilon_{0}
;
moreover, the last term in
the display
(
3.54
)
does not
appear in this analysis,
see for instance
(
3.35
)
where the only difficulty comes from
T_{3}
.
The fact that Proposition

3.10
holds true permits to conclude directly, as in the proof of
the first part of
Theorem
3.1
.
∎

3.6.2
Conservative case

Proposition 3.21
.

Assume that

b
satisfies
(
Reg
-
b
-(
{\eta,2}
))
, for some
\eta\in[0,1)
, and that, for any
m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,
b(\cdot,m)
is divergence free
in the sense of distribution.
Then,
(
3.4
)
holds true.

A prototype for
b
being divergence free is
b(x,m)=(B*m)(x)
, for a bounded vector field
B
from

{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
into
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with zero divergence (in the sense of distribution).

Obviously, the Lebesgue measure is invariant for (
1.4
).
We prove that it is exponentially stable.
It suffices to observe that the solution to the Fokker-Planck equation
writes (in the sense of distribution):

\partial_{t}m(t\,;\mu)-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta m(t\,;\mu)+b\bigl(\cdot,m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}m(t\,;\mu)=0.

Since
m(t\,;\mu)
is known to have a bounded density for any
t>0
, it is clear that the solution to
the above equation has a continuous gradient (in
x
) at any positive time
t>0
.
By Lemma

3.11
, it satisfies

\bigl\|m(t\,;\mu)-1\bigr\|_{\infty}=\Bigl\|m(t\,;\mu)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}m(t\,;\mu)(x)dx\Bigr\|_{\infty}\leq Ce^{-\lambda t}.

As for the linearized operator
(
3.1
)
at
\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
,
we notice that

[\delta b/\delta m](\cdot,m)(q)
is divergence free.
This is shown (first at any
m
with a positive density and then at any
m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
)
by passing to the limit in

\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}b\bigl(x,m+\epsilon q\bigr)\cdot\nabla\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0,

for
any smooth
q:{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with
\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}q(x)\mathrm{d}x=0
and any
smooth
\varphi:{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
.
Therefore,
(
3.1
)
becomes

L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}}q=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta q-\textrm{\rm div}\bigl(b(\cdot,\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}})q\bigr),

which is local (whilst

L_{m}
in
(
3.1
)
is nonlocal in
q
because of the third term therein).
Accordingly, the term
T_{3}
in the proof of
Proposition
3.12

becomes null, which makes it possible to prove

(
Erg
)
at
\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
.
∎

3.6.3
Gradient systems

We now the study the case when
b
derives from
a symmetric potential
W
, namely

(3.76)

b(x,m)=-\kappa\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla W(x-y)m(\mathrm{d}y),\quad x\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\ m\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

for a positive constant
\kappa
and a twice continuously differentiable potential
W:{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
that is
coordinate-wise even, i.e.

W(x_{1},\cdots,-x_{i},\cdots,x_{d})=W(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i},\cdots,x_{d})
, for

(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d})\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.
This example has received a lot of attention in the literature. Below, we borrow several results from

[
20
]
.

The uniform distribution
\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}
is an invariant measure.
This follows from the simple fact that
b(x,\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}})=0
. The linearised operator
L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}}
at
\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
has the simple form (see
[
20
, Subsection 3.2]
):

(3.77)

L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}}(\cdot)=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta(\cdot)+\kappa\Delta\bigl(W\star\cdot\bigr),

where
\star
stands for the convolution product.
Since
W
is coordinate-wise even,
L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}}
is symmetric.

The following two results follow the analysis carried out in

[
20
, Section]

(with
\beta=2
,
L=1
).

Proposition 3.22
.

Assume that

\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}
is the unique invariant measure
and
that

-1-2\kappa\inf_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\widehat{W}^{\bm{n}}<0
,
then
(
3.4
)
holds true.

For instance, if the potential
W
satisfies

\widehat{W}^{\bm{n}}\geq 0
for any

{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, then it satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
3.22
, see

[
20
, Section 3]
. The latter condition is equivalent to

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}W(x-y)\eta(\mathrm{d}x)\eta(\mathrm{d}y)\geq 0,

for any finite measure
\eta
on

{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
see again

[
20
]
.
The above condition
says the potential

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\ni m\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}W(x-y)m(\mathrm{d}x)m(\mathrm{d}y)
is convex in the functional sense,
which is consistent with the recent result
obtained in
[
28
]
.
More generally,
letting

W_{u}(x):=-\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\bigl(\widehat{W}^{\bm{n}}\bigr)_{-}\cos\bigl(2\pi{\bm{n}}\cdot x),\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},

it is shown in

[
20
]

that

\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}
is
the unique invariant measure if

2\pi^{2}>\kappa\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\|\Delta W_{u}\|_{\infty}
, but this condition is not sharp. For instance, the Kuramoto model addressed in the next section
corresponds to

d=1

and

W(x)=-\cos(2\pi x)
(for
x\in{\mathbb{T}}
).
In this case,

2\pi^{2}>\kappa\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\|\Delta W_{u}\|_{\infty}

if and only if
\kappa<1/2
.
However, the analysis carried out in

[
44
]

(see in particular
Proposition 4.2 therein)
shows that the proof of Proposition

3.22

still works when
\kappa\in[1/2,1)
.

First Step.

The first step is to show that

L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}}
satisfies

(
Erg
)
. This follows from
the analysis performed in

[
20
]
.
The
non-trivial eigenfunctions of the operator
L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}}

are the non-trivial Fourier functions. They form an orthonormal basis of the space
\{f\in{\mathbb{L}}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}):\langle f,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0\}

and, under the assumption of the statement,
all the corresponding eigenvalues
(\lambda_{{\bm{k}}})_{{\bm{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}
are strictly negative, with a non-zero spectral gap, i.e.

\sup_{{\bm{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\lambda_{\bm{k}}<0
.

We then adapt
Lemma

3.11

to our setting in order
to prove
(
3.3
).
To do so, we use the existence of a spectral gap. It says that, for any smooth
u:{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with
\langle u,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
,

-\langle L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}}u,u\rangle\geq\lambda\langle u,u\rangle.

Now, for
a smooth function
\xi
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
and for
t>0
,
we consider
the solution

(w(s))_{0\leq s\leq t}
to the equation

(3.78)

\partial_{s}w(s,\cdot)+L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}}w(s,\cdot)=0,\quad s\in[0,t];\qquad w(0)=\xi.

Then
\langle w,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle
is constant and, by the existence of a spectral gap,

(3.79)

\bigl\|w(s)-\langle w,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\bigr\|_{2}\leq\|\xi\|_{2}e^{-\lambda(t-s)},\quad s\in[0,t].

Noticing
that
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Delta W(x-y)\mathrm{d}y=0
,
we deduce that

(3.80)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigl|\Delta(W\star w)(s,x)\bigr|=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\bigl|\Delta{\bigl(W\star\bigl[w-\langle w,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\bigr]\bigr)}(s,x)\bigr|\leq{C}\|\xi\|_{\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}.

By
recalling the shape of

L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}}
in
(
3.77
)
and regarding the equation
(
3.78
) as the heat equation plus a source term that decays exponentially fast, it is quite standard to show that

w
satisfies the conclusion of
Lemma

3.11
. It remains to adapt the proof of
Proposition
3.12
.
We start from (
3.2
), when driven by
L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}}
.
Instead of considering

w
as the solution of
(
3.34
), we choose
w
as the solution of
(
3.78
). This leads to a new expansion in
(
3.35
)
with
T_{3}=0
(because we included the term

\kappa\Delta(W\star w)
in
(
3.78
)). It then suffices to let appear the exponential decay of

w
and
\nabla_{x}w
in the estimates of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.

Second Step.

The next step is to prove that

L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}}
is
uniformly attracting (as in the statement of Theorem
3.1
). This follows from the
variational structure of the problem and from the additional assumption that

L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}}
is the unique invariant measure.
Indeed, the McKean-Vlasov equation
may be regarded as a gradient flow, with potential

(3.81)

{\mathcal{F}}(m)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\frac{dm}{dx}(x)\ln\bigl(\frac{dm}{dx}(x)\bigr)\mathrm{d}x+\frac{\kappa}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}W(x-y)m(x)m(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

if
m
is absolutely continuous with respect to

L_{\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}}
,
and

{\mathcal{F}}(m)=+\infty
if it is not absolutely continuous.

Recall that the solution of
(
1.4
) has a density
p(t,x;\mu)
in time
t>0
.
Since
\nabla W
is Lipschitz continuous,

p(t,x\,;\mu)
is continuously differentiable in
x
and belongs to a compact subset

{\mathcal{K}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, independent of
\mu
, when
t\geq 1
.
In particular,

{\mathcal{F}}(m(t\,;\mu))<\infty

for any
t>0
.
Moreover, by the gradient flow structure,

(3.82)

\begin{split}\forall 0<t_{1}<t_{2},\quad&{\mathcal{F}}\bigl(m(t_{2};\mu)\bigr)-{\mathcal{F}}\bigl(m(t_{1};\mu)\bigr)\\
&=-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl|\kappa\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\nabla W\bigl(x-x^{\prime}\bigr)m(r;\mu)(\mathrm{d}x^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\nabla_{x}p(r,x;\mu)}{p(r,x;\mu)}\Bigr|^{2}m(r\,;\mu)(\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}r.\end{split}

For
\delta>0
, let
{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}=\{p\in{\mathcal{K}}:\|p-{\mathbbm{1}}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\geq\delta\}
. Clearly,
{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}
is a compact subset of

{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
More importantly, the quantity

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Bigl|\kappa\int_{\mathbb{T}}\nabla W\bigl(x-x^{\prime}\bigr)p(x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\nabla p}{p}(x)\Bigr|^{2}p(x)\mathrm{d}x

cannot vanish on
{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}
, as otherwise there would exist
a non-trivial stationary solution of
(
1.4
).
By compactness of
{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}
and by a straightforward continuity argument, we deduce that there exists
a constant
c>0
such that, for all
p\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}
,
the above quantity is greater than
c
.
In particular, if we take
t_{1}=1

and
t_{2}=\inf\{t\geq 1:p(t,\mu)\not\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}\}
in
(
3.82
), we obtain that

(3.83)

{\mathcal{F}}\bigl(p(t_{2},\mu)\bigr)\leq{\mathcal{F}}\bigl(p(t_{1},\mu)\bigr)-c(t_{2}-t_{1})\leq C-c(t_{2}-t_{1}).

By the same compactness argument,
{\mathcal{F}}
must be lower-bounded on
{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}
,
from which
we deduce that, for any
\mu
,
t_{2}
is finite and that, most of all,
there exists
T<\infty
, independent of
\mu
, such that
t_{2}\leq T
.
∎

We conclude with the following statement, which shows that the results proven in the metastable regime cover
some of the examples
considered in

[
20
]
:

Proposition 3.23
.

We can find potentials
W

for which there are several invariant measures
and
for which
-1-2\kappa\inf_{{\bm{n}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\widehat{W}^{\bm{n}}<0
.
In this case,

\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}

satisfies the second part of
Theorem

3.1
(in the metastable regime) and
Theorem

3.19
.

This follows from (3.5),
Definition 5.1 and Proposition 5.8 in
[
20
]
. With the same notations as therein,
it suffices to have
\kappa_{c}<\kappa_{\sharp}
and to choose
\kappa\in(\kappa_{c},\kappa_{\sharp})
.
Examples
of potentials
W
for which

\kappa\in(\kappa_{c},\kappa_{\sharp})

are provided by
Corollaries 5.13 and 5.14
and Proposition 6.2
in
[
20
]
.
∎

4
Model without a unique invariant measure

The purpose of this section is to address the Kuramoto model.
It is in fact a particular case of example
(
3.76
), with
d=1
and
W(x)=-\cos(2\pi x)
, i.e.

(4.1)

b(y,\mu)=-2\pi\kappa\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\sin(2\pi(y-x))\mu(\mathrm{d}x).

Interestingly, Kuramoto’s model exhibits a phase transition when

\kappa=1
(see for instance
[
7
]
). When
\kappa\leq 1
, the Fokker-Planck equation
(
1.4
)
has a unique invariant probability measure, which is given by

{\textrm{\rm Leb}}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
(and
the method of proof of
Proposition

3.22
covers the case
\kappa<1
).
When
\kappa>1
, it has an infinite number of invariant measures,
namely
{\textrm{\rm Leb}}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
and
a collection of non-trivial ones, all of them being obtained by rotation of a non-constant density

p_{\infty}
(i.e.,
p_{\infty,\psi}:=p_{\infty}(\cdot-\psi)
is an invariant measure for any

\psi\in{\mathbb{T}}
).

In the rest of the section, we focus on the regime
\kappa>1
. In
that case,
propagation of chaos cannot hold at time of order
t=N

(see
[
8
]
together with

[
58
]

for a similar phenomenon in the Euclidean setting).
In fact, the result of

[
8
]

has just been revisited by

[
30
]
(in the even more complex case when the interactions are subjected to a
non-complete graph).
The main idea therein is to show that, even though the particle system may strongly deviate from an invariant profile in time of order

N
, it stays close to the whole collection
1
1

1

Most of the time, we shall identify the densities that belong to
{\mathcal{I}}
together with the probability measures that are driven by those densities.

{\mathcal{I}}:=\{p_{\infty,\psi},\psi\in{\mathbb{T}}\}
for a time period that is nearly exponential in

N
. However, the rate of convergence is not addressed in
[
30
]
. Using the techniques developed in the previous section, we show here that, if the initial condition is not
{\textrm{\rm Leb}}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
, we can retain a uniform
weak error of size
1/N
provided that we force the test functional

\Phi
in
(
2.9
)
to be invariant by rotation (see Theorem

4.3

below).
We stress that the latter requirement on
\Phi
is fully consistent
with the point of view used in

[
30
]
.
Our improvement is thus twofold: Not only do we get an explicit rate for the weak error, but we also manage to get a bound that
holds uniformly in time (not only up until times that are exponential in
N
). Notice however that, in

[
30
]
, the convergence is understood for the sup norm over the trajectory (which is stronger).

Things become more subtle whenever the system is initialized from the invariant measure
{\textrm{\rm Leb}}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
since
Lemma
4.16

below shows that the empirical measure leaves, with a large probability, any sufficiently small neighbourhood
of the uniform distribution in a time that is at most polynomial in
N
. As a result, another study would be necessary to handle this case specifically.

Below, we use freely the same general notations as in the previous section.
In particular,

m(\cdot\,;\mu)
denotes the solution to (
1.4
)
with
\mu
as initial condition and with
b
as in
(
4.1
). Here, it takes the form

(4.2)

\partial_{t}m(t\,;\mu)-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}m(t\,;\mu)-\partial_{x}\Bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigl(J\star m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigr)=0,

where
\star
denotes the standard convolution product and

J(\cdot)=2\pi\kappa\sin(2\pi\cdot)
.

Of course,

m(\cdot\,;\mu)
is absolutely continuous in positive time, we therefore let
p(t\,;\mu)=(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}x)m(t;\mu):x\mapsto p(t\,;\mu)
(which we also write
p(t,x\,;\mu)
) be the density of

m(t\,;\mu)
, for
t>0
. The function
p(t\,;\mu)(\cdot)
is a smooth function of
x
, uniformly in
t\in[t_{0},\infty)
for any
t_{0}>0
.
Also, we recall that each
p_{\infty,\psi}
is (strictly) positive.

4.1
Main result

We focus on initial conditions that are away from
{\textrm{\rm Leb}}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
, namely we let, for any

\eta\in(0,1)
,

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}=\bigl\{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}):|\mu^{1}|\geq\eta\bigr\},\quad\mu^{1}:=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\exp(-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\theta)\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta).

The next result (proven at the end of the subsection) shows that

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
is attracted by
{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

For any
\eta\in(0,1)
,
and any integer
k\geq 1
,
there exist an exponent
\beta>0
and a constant
C
, both depending on
\kappa
,
\eta
and
k
, such that

\forall t\geq 1,\quad\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}}\inf_{\psi\in{\mathbb{T}}}\,\bigl\|p(t\,;\mu)-p_{\infty,\psi}\bigr\|_{k,\infty}\leq C\exp(-\beta t).

Proposition
4.1
plays a key role in our analysis.
Notice that the constraint
t\geq 1
may be easily changed into
t\geq t_{0}
for any
t_{0}>0
, in which case the
constant
C
may depend on
t_{0}
as well.

In order to state our main result precisely, we need the following
additional definition.

Definition 4.2
.

We say that a function
\Phi:{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

is rotation invariant if, for any

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})
and

\psi\in\mathbb{T}
,

\Phi(\mu\circ\tau_{\psi}^{-1})=\Phi(\mu)
,
where

\mu\circ\tau_{\psi}^{-1}
is the image of
\mu
by
the translation
\tau_{\psi}:\mathbb{T}\ni x\mapsto x+\psi
.

We now have all the ingredients to formulate the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 4.3
.

Assume
that

\Phi
is rotation invariant and
satisfies

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))

for some
\gamma\in(0,1]
. Then,
for any
\eta\in(0,1)
,
there exists a constant
C>0
such that,
for any
\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
and any
N\geq 1
,

\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big|\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Phi(\mu^{N}_{t})\bigr]-\Phi\bigl(m(t\,;{\mu_{\text{\emph{init}}}})\bigr)\Big|\leq\frac{C}{N}.

Very much in the spirit of
Proposition
2.3
,
the most useful example for
\Phi
is

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
p_{\infty,+}
denote the unique element of
{\mathcal{I}}
whose first Fourier coefficient
p_{\infty,+}^{1}
is a positive real and let
\mu_{\infty,+}:=p_{\infty,+}\cdot\textrm{\rm Leb}_{\mathbb{T}}
.
For
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and for a smooth non-decreasing cut-off function
\varphi:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
that is equal to
0
on

[0,\delta/2]
and
1
on
[\delta,1]
, for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, let
\Phi
be defined by

\Phi(\mu):=\varphi\bigl(|\mu^{1}|\bigr)\Bigl\|\mu\circ\tau_{\frac{\mu^{1}}{|\mu^{1}|}}^{-1}-\mu_{\infty,+}\Bigr\|_{-(1+\varepsilon)/2,2}^{2}+1-\varphi\bigl(|\mu^{1}|\bigr),

where
\|\cdot\|_{-(1+\varepsilon)/2,2}
is defined
as in Proposition

2.3

with
d=1
therein.
Then
\Phi
satisfies the assumption of
Theorem

4.3
.

We add a few words about the meaning of
p_{\infty,+}
. The elements of
{\mathcal{I}}
are obtained by rotation. Therefore, the collection of their first Fourier coefficients coincides with a circle, whose radius is non-zero (see
[
8
, Subsection 1.2]
). Consequently, we may indeed choose
p_{\infty,+}\in{\mathcal{I}}
such that

p_{\infty,+}^{1}>0
.
Accordingly, we also notice that, in the notation
\mu\circ\tau_{\mu^{1}/|\mu^{1}|}^{-1}
,
we identify
\mu^{1}/|\mu^{1}|
with the unique element
\psi\in{\mathbb{T}}
such that

\mu^{1}=|\mu^{1}|\exp({\mathrm{i}}2\pi\psi)
. In particular,
the first Fourier coefficient
(\mu\circ\tau_{\mu^{1}/|\mu^{1}|}^{-1})^{1}
of

\mu\circ\tau_{\mu^{1}/|\mu^{1}|}^{-1}
, which is
equal to
\exp(-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi\psi)\mu^{1}=|\mu^{1}|
, is
positive (when
\mu^{1}\not=0
), which explains why we compare
\mu\circ\tau_{\mu^{1}/|\mu^{1}|}^{-1}
with
\mu_{\infty,+}
. We refer to

[
56
, Lemma 2.8]
for another
projection onto
{\mathcal{I}}
.

Moreover, we notice that this is precisely the role of the cut-off function
\varphi
in the definition of
\Phi
to remove the measures
\mu
for which
\mu^{1}=0
. In fact, the cut-off function has no real consequence on our result.
Actually, what matters is that
\Phi(\mu)
is small if and only if
\mu
is close enough to
{\mathcal{I}}
. Precisely, we can find a constant
c>0
such that

(4.3)

\Phi(\mu)\geq c\inf_{\psi\in\mathbb{T}}\bigl\|\mu-\mu_{\psi}\|_{-(1+\varepsilon)/2,2}^{2}.

The proof of the above lower bound is quite easy. It suffices to prove it for
|\mu^{1}|
bounded away from zero. To do so, we may observe that

\|\mu\circ\tau_{\mu^{1}/|\mu^{1}|}^{-1}-\mu_{{\infty,+}}\|_{-(1+\varepsilon)/2,2}^{2}

is lower-bounded by

\inf_{\psi\in\mathbb{T}}\bigl\|\mu-\mu_{\psi}\|_{-(1+\varepsilon)/2,2}^{2}
.
The following is a straightforward corollary.

Corollary 4.5
.

For any
\eta\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
there exist two (positive) constants
c
and
C
such that, for
\mu_{\textrm{\rm init}}
in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
,

\forall t\geq 0,\quad{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\inf_{\psi\in\mathbb{T}}\bigl\|\mu_{t}^{N}-\mu_{\psi}\|_{-(1+\varepsilon)/2,2}^{2}\Bigr]\leq\frac{C}{N}+C\exp(-ct).

We take for granted the statements of Theorem

4.3
and
Propositions

4.1
and
4.4
.
By
(
4.3
) and with
\Phi
as in Proposition
4.4
, it suffices to prove that

\Phi(m(t\,;\mu))
decays exponentially fast for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
.
By Proposition

4.1
, there exists
\psi\in\mathbb{T}
such that

\|p(t\,;\mu)-p_{\infty,\psi}\|_{2}\leq C\exp(-ct)
, for
t\geq 1
.
Hence, it is enough to show that

\Bigl\|p_{\infty,\psi}-p_{\infty,+}\circ\tau_{\frac{(p(t,\mu))^{1}}{|(p(t,\mu))^{1}|}}\Bigr\|_{2}\leq C\exp(-ct),

or equivalently that

\Bigl\|p_{\infty,+}\circ\tau_{\frac{(p_{\infty,\psi})^{1}}{|(p_{\infty,\psi})^{1}|}}-p_{\infty,+}\circ\tau_{\frac{(p(t,\mu))^{1}}{|(p(t,\mu))^{1}}|}\Bigr\|_{2}\leq C\exp(-ct),

at least for
t
sufficiently large.
Since
p^{1}_{\infty,+}>0
, we know that

|(p(t,\mu))^{1}|
is lower-bounded by a positive constant, uniformly over all
t
greater than some
t_{0}>0
.
In turn, we have

\biggl|\frac{(p(t,\mu))^{1}}{|(p(t,\mu))^{1}|}-\frac{(p_{\infty,\psi})^{1}}{|(p_{\infty,\psi})^{1}|}\biggr|\leq C\exp(-ct),

for some possibly new value of
C
, which gives the expected result.
∎

Proofs of the auxiliary Propositions

4.1

and

4.4

The reader may skip the proofs of
Propositions

4.1

and

4.4

ahead on a first reading.

We use a simplified notation
\tilde{\mu}
for

\mu\circ\tau_{{\mu^{1}}/{|\mu^{1}|}}^{-1}
.
The tricky point is then to study the smoothness of the mapping

\tilde{\Phi}(\mu)=\bigl\|\tilde{\mu}-\nu_{0}\bigr\|_{-(1+\varepsilon)/2,2}^{2},

at least when
\mu^{1}
stays away from
0
, for
\nu_{0}
fixed.
We observe that we have the following:

\tilde{\mu}^{n}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi nx}d\tilde{\mu}(x)=\Bigl(\frac{|\mu^{1}|}{\mu^{1}}\Bigr)^{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi nx}d\mu(x)=\Bigl(\frac{|\mu^{1}|}{\mu^{1}}\Bigr)^{n}\mu^{n}.

Therefore, following
(
2.12
),
we get

\Phi(\mu)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{1}{(1+n^{2})^{(1+\varepsilon)/2}}\biggl[\mu^{n}\bar{\mu}^{n}+\nu_{0}^{n}\bar{\nu}_{0}^{n}-\Bigl(\frac{|\mu^{1}|}{\mu^{1}}\Bigr)^{n}\mu^{n}\bar{\nu}_{0}^{n}-\Bigl(\frac{|\mu^{1}|}{\bar{\mu}^{1}}\Bigr)^{n}\nu_{0}^{n}\bar{\mu}^{n}\biggr],

which we can then rewrite in the form

\Phi(\mu)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{1}{(1+n^{2})^{(1+\varepsilon)/2}}\biggl[\mu^{n}\bar{\mu}^{n}+\nu_{0}^{n}\bar{\nu}_{0}^{n}-\bigl(\Psi(\mu)\bigr)^{n}\mu^{n}\bar{\nu}_{0}^{n}-\bigl(\overline{\Psi}(\mu)\bigr)^{n}\nu_{0}^{n}\bar{\mu}^{n}\biggr],

with
\Psi(\mu)=|\mu^{1}|/\mu^{1}
.
On the open subset
\{\mu^{1}\not=0\}
(for the

{\mathcal{W}}^{1}
topology), the function
\Psi
is infinitely differentiable with respect to

\mu
. The power
n
creates additional factors that are handled in the same way as in the proof of Proposition

2.3
. As a result, we get that, for the same values of
k
,

\Phi
satisfies the same properties as in Proposition
2.3
, but on any domain where

\mu^{1}
stays away from
0
.
∎

We close this subsection with the following proof.

The proof is achieved in two steps, which are mostly adapted from

[
7
]
and
[
44
]
.

First step.

The first step is to show that

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}}\inf_{\psi\in{\mathbb{T}}}\ \bigl\|p(t\,;\mu)-p_{\infty,\psi}\bigr\|_{2}=0.

In order to do so, we follow the proof of Proposition 1.7 in
[
7
]
and of
Proposition
3.22
.
We recall indeed that the McKean-Vlasov equation
(
4.2
)
may be regarded as a gradient flow, with potential

{\mathcal{F}}
in
(
3.81
) (with
W(x)=-\cos(2\pi x)
).
Then, we know from Proposition 4.4 in
[
44
]
that, after some time

t_{\eta,\epsilon}
(independent of the choice of
\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
),
p(t\,;\mu)\not\in B_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}({\mathbbm{1}},\epsilon)
for a given
\epsilon>0
(here,
B_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}({\mathbbm{1}},\epsilon)
is the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
-ball of center
{\mathbbm{1}}
and radius
\epsilon
).
Also, after the same time
t_{\eta,\epsilon}
, we know that

p(t\,;\mu)
belongs to a compact subset
{\mathcal{K}}
of
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}({\mathbb{T}},(0,+\infty))
, which may be chosen independently of
\mu
.

For
\delta>0
as above, let
{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}=\{p\in{\mathcal{K}}:\|p-{\mathbbm{1}}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\geq\delta,\,\inf_{\psi\in\mathbb{T}}\|p-p_{\infty,\psi}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\geq\delta\}
, which is a compact subset of

{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
. Next, we follow
(
3.82
).
If
we take
t_{1}=t_{\eta,\epsilon}

and
t_{2}=\inf\{t\geq t_{\eta,\epsilon}:p(t,\mu)\not\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\delta}\}
, we obtain the analogue of
(
3.83
).
By the same argument as therein, we deduce that, for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
,
t_{2}
is finite and that
there exists
T<\infty
, independent of
\mu
, such that
t_{2}\leq T
.

Now, if we choose
\delta\leq\epsilon
, then we cannot have
\|p(t\,;\mu)-{\mathbbm{1}}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\leq\delta
for
t\geq t_{1}
, as otherwise
p(t\,;\mu)
would belong to
B_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}({\mathbbm{1}},\epsilon)
. Therefore, the condition that
t_{2}<\infty
implies

\inf_{\psi\in\mathbb{T}}\|p(t_{2}\,;\mu)-p_{\infty,\psi}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}<\delta
.
All in all, we deduce that, for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
, there exists
t_{2}
(possibly depending on

\mu
), less than
T
(which is independent of
\mu
), such that

\inf_{\psi\in\mathbb{T}}\|p(t_{2}\,;\mu)-p_{\infty,\psi}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\leq\delta
.

Second step.

By choosing
\delta
as small as we want, we deduce that, after the same time
t_{2}\leq T
as above,

p(t_{2}\,;\mu)
, regarded as the new initial condition for the Fokker-Planck equation, satisfies
the assumption of Theorem 4.6 in
[
44
]
.
It now remains to see that the exponential decay in
[
44
, Theorem 4.6]
is uniform
on
B_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}(p_{\infty,\psi},\delta)
, provided that
\delta
is small enough (independently of the value of
\psi
), which is exactly
[
44
, Lemma 4.9]
.
By combining with the smoothing estimate in

[
44
, Lemma 2.2]
, this completes the proof.
∎

4.2
Linearised operator

We now address the linearisation of the nonlinear Fokker-Planck equation
at
a (non-trivial) invariant measure (which hence has the form

p_{\infty,\psi}
for some phase
\psi\in\mathbb{T}
).
For simplicity, we write
p_{\psi}
instead of

p_{\infty,\psi}
.
The linearised version
(
3.1
) may be written in the form

\partial_{t}q(t)-L_{m(t;\mu)}q(t)=0,

with the convenient notation

L_{m}q=\tfrac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}q+\partial_{x}\Bigl(q\bigl(J\star m\bigr)+m\bigl(J\star q\bigr)\Bigr),

for any two distributions
m
and
q
acting on smooth functions on the torus (notice that

L_{m}q
always makes sense as a distribution since

J\star m
and
J\star q
themselves should be smooth functions).
When
m=p_{\psi}
for some element
\psi\in\mathbb{T}
, we merely write

L_{\psi}
for
L_{m}
.
Notice in particular that, by choosing
m_{t}=p_{\psi}
(which is hence independent of
t
)
in
(
4.2
) and then by taking the derivative with respect to
\psi
(which coincides with the derivative in
x
),

(4.4)

L_{\psi}p_{\psi}^{\prime}=0,

where
p_{\psi}^{\prime}=(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}x)p_{\psi}
.
The above identity was already used in
[
7
]
and in the subsequent works of the same authors. It plays a key role here in our analysis as well.

We start with
the long-run analysis of the linearised operator, which is the most demanding step. In fact, the proof is made easier by all the existing results on the Kuramoto model, but the reader must realise that this preliminary step is the cornerstone of the whole analysis in this subsection.

Proposition 4.6
.

For a smooth initial condition
q_{0}
on
\mathbb{T}
, with
\langle q_{0},{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
,
let
q
denote the solution of

(4.5)

\partial_{t}q-L_{\psi}q=0,\quad\quad t\geq 0,

and, for any
t\geq 0
, let
Q(t,\cdot)
denote the (unique) periodic primitive
of
q(t,\cdot)
satisfying

\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q(t,x)\mathrm{d}x=0
.

Then there exist two positive constants
\lambda
and
C
, only depending on
\kappa
,
together with a constant
\bar{q}_{1/2}
, depending on
q(0,\cdot)
and
\psi
, such that, for any
t\geq 0
,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(t,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\leq C\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(0,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x,\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(t,x)-\bar{q}_{1/2}(p_{\psi}(x)-1)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\leq Ce^{-\lambda t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(0,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x.

Remark 4.7
.

In fact, the proof shows that

\bar{q}_{1/2}^{2}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(0,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x
.

The fact that
(
4.5
) has a unique solution is a consequence of the analysis performed in the previous section.
Since
\int_{\mathbb{T}}q(t,x)\mathrm{d}x=0
for any
t\geq 0
, it makes sense to define

Q(t,\cdot)
as in the statement.

Thanks to
Remark

4.7
, it suffices to focus on the proof of
(
4.6
).
The proof mostly relies on the work of

[
7
]
.
Following the notation introduced in

[
7
, (1.23)]
, we indeed let

(4.7)

\llangle u,v\rrangle_{\psi}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\bar{U}(x)\bar{V}(x)}{p_{\psi}(x)}\mathrm{d}x,

for any two distributions
u
and
v
on
\mathbb{T}
and any two
\bar{U}
and
\bar{V}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
such that
\bar{U}^{\prime}=u
,
\bar{V}^{\prime}=v
and

\langle\bar{U},p_{\psi}^{-1}\rangle=\langle\bar{V},p_{\psi}^{-1}\rangle=0
.
From

[
7
, (2.14), (2.16), (2.37)]
, there exists a constant
\lambda
, only depending on
\kappa
, such that,
for any
t\geq 0
,

(4.8)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigl\llangle q(t,\cdot),q(t,\cdot)\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}+\lambda\big\llangle q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}(t)p_{\psi}^{\prime},q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}(t)p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}\leq 0,\quad{\rm with}\ \bar{q}_{1/2}(t)=\frac{\big\llangle q(t,\cdot),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\big\rrangle_{\psi}}{\big\llangle p_{\psi}^{\prime},p_{\psi}^{\prime}\big\rrangle_{\psi}}.

By
[
7
, (2.14)]
again, we observe that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big\llangle q(t,\cdot),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\big\rrangle_{\psi}=\big\llangle L_{\psi}q(t,\cdot),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\big\rrangle_{\psi}=\big\llangle q(t,\cdot),L_{\psi}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\big\rrangle_{\psi}=0,

with the last equality following from
(
4.4
). Hence, we can write
\bar{q}_{1/2}(t)
as
\bar{q}_{1/2}
.
In particular,

(4.9)

|\bar{q}_{1/2}|=\biggl|\frac{\big\llangle q(0,\cdot),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\big\rrangle_{\psi}}{\big\llangle p_{\psi}^{\prime},p_{\psi}^{\prime}\big\rrangle_{\psi}}\biggr|\leq C\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(0,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\biggr)^{1/2}.

Moreover,
applying (
4.8
) to
q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}
, we deduce that, for
t\geq 0
,

(4.10)

\big\llangle q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime},q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}\leq\big\llangle q(0,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime},q(0,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}e^{-\lambda t}.

By
(
4.7
),
the right-hand side in (
4.10
)
reads

\big\llangle q(0,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime},q(0,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{|Q(0,x)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}(x)-k(0)|^{2}}{p_{\psi}(x)}\mathrm{d}x,

where
k(0)
is a centring constant that forces the mean of
(Q(0,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}-k(0))/p_{\psi}

to be zero. In particular,
the left-hand side is less than

(4.11)

\displaystyle\big\llangle q(0,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime},q(0,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{|Q(0,x)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}(x)|^{2}}{p_{\psi}(x)}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(0,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x,

where the last inequality is obtained by expanding the square and by invoking
Remark

4.7
, with the constant
C
depending only on
\kappa
.
Back to (
4.10
), we write in a similar manner:

\big\llangle q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime},q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{|Q(t,x)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}(x)-k(t)|^{2}}{p_{\psi}(x)}\mathrm{d}x,

for a new centring constant
k(t)
. Using now an upper bound for

p_{\psi}
and assuming w.l.o.g. that the constant
C
right above is large enough (as long as it only depends on
\kappa
),
we obtain that

\big\llangle q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime},q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}\geq C^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(t,x)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}(x)-k(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x.

Using the fact that
Q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}(p_{\psi}-1)
has zero mean, we deduce that

(4.12)

\big\llangle q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime},q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rrangle_{\psi}\geq C^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|Q(t,x)-\bar{q}_{1/2}(p_{\psi}(x)-1)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x.

Combining
(
4.10
),
(
4.11
)
and
(
4.12
), we get
(
4.6
).
∎

The following result
is the analogue of
Lemma

3.11
in
Section

3.3
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
t>0
,
\psi\in\mathbb{T}
and
\xi\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
and let

L_{\psi}^{*}
denote the adjoint of
L_{\psi}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
. Then the problem

(4.13)

\partial_{s}w+L_{\psi}^{*}w=0,\quad s\in[0,t];\quad w(t,x)=\xi(x),

admits a unique classical solution
(w(s,\cdot))_{0\leq s\leq t}
. Moreover,
there exist constants
C,\lambda>0

(
only depending on

\kappa
and hence independent of
t)
such that

(4.14)

\bigg\|w(s,\cdot)-\int_{\mathbb{T}}w(s,y)\mathrm{d}y\bigg\|_{\infty}\leq C\Bigl(\|\xi\|_{\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}+|\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle|\Bigr),\quad\quad\forall s\in[0,t],

and, for any
\alpha,\beta\in[0,1]

(
allowing, in addition, the constants
C
and
\lambda
to depend on

\alpha,\beta)
,

(4.15)

\begin{split}&\big\|\partial_{x}w(s,\cdot)\big\|_{\beta,\infty}\leq{\frac{C}{1\wedge(t-s)^{(\alpha+\beta)/2}}}\Bigl(\|\xi\|_{1-\alpha,\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}+|\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle|\Bigr),\quad\quad\forall s\in[0,t].\end{split}

Existence and uniqueness of a classical solution to the Cauchy problem is
standard.
The rest of the proof is similar to
the proof of Lemma

3.11
, but with some differences that we clarify below.

First Step.

The first step is to prove the bounds when

t-s\leq 1
.
By expanding the operator
L_{\psi}^{*}
, the equation satisfied by

w
may be rewritten in the form

(4.16)

\partial_{s}w(s,\cdot)+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}w(s,\cdot)+V_{1}\partial_{x}w(s,\cdot)+\int_{\mathbb{T}}V_{2}(\cdot,y)\bar{w}(s,y)\mathrm{d}y=0,\quad s\in[0,t],

where

\bar{w}(s,x)=w(s,x)-\int_{\mathbb{T}}w(s,y)\mathrm{d}y

and
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are smooth functions on
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}^{2}
respectively,
whose derivatives up to any order are bounded in terms of
\kappa
only.
By a standard application of the maximum principle combined with Gronwall’s lemma,
it is easy to show that

(4.17)

\sup_{\max(0,t-1)\leq s\leq t}\|w(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq C\|\xi\|_{\infty}.

Since
V_{2}
is smooth, this provides a bound for the derivatives of any order
of the third term in the left-hand side of
(
4.16
).
We then split
w
into
w=w_{1}+w_{2}
,
with

(4.18)

\begin{split}&\partial_{s}w_{1}(s,\cdot)+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}w_{1}(s,\cdot)+V_{1}\partial_{x}w_{1}(s,\cdot)=0,\quad s\in[0,t]\,;\quad w_{1}(t,\cdot)=\xi,\\
&\partial_{s}w_{2}(s,\cdot)+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{xx}w_{2}(s,\cdot)+V_{1}\partial_{x}w_{2}(s,\cdot)+\int_{\mathbb{T}}V_{2}(\cdot,y)\bar{w}(s,y)\mathrm{d}y=0,\quad s\in[0,t]\,;\quad w_{2}(t,\cdot)=0.\end{split}

By Lemma

3.11
,
we get all the required bounds on

w_{1}(s,\cdot)
and its derivatives,
at least for
t-s\leq 1
.

Now, in the equation for
w_{2}
, the source term
(with
\bar{w}
being frozen)
is smooth. The solution
w_{2}(s,\cdot)
thus has bounded (spatial) derivatives
of any order, for
t-s\leq 1
, with the bounds being independent of
t
.

Second Step.

The rest of the proof is dedicated to the case
t\geq 1
.
We start with the proof of (
4.14
),
using the same notations

q(0,\cdot)
,
q
and
\bar{q}_{1/2}

as in the statement and the proof of
Proposition

4.6
.
By Proposition

4.6
and Remark

4.7
,
there exist
\lambda
and
C
as in the statement (but the values of which are allowed to vary from line to line) such that

\begin{split}\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi,q(t,\cdot)\bigr\rangle\bigr|&\leq\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi,\bigl(q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr)\bigr\rangle\bigr|+\bigl|\bar{q}_{1/2}\bigr|\,\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\bigr|\\
&\leq\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi^{\prime},\bigl(Q(t,\cdot)-\bar{q}_{1/2}[p_{\psi}-1]\bigr)\bigr\rangle\bigr|+C\|Q(0,\cdot)\|_{2}\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\bigr|\leq C\|Q(0,\cdot)\|_{2}\bigl(\|\xi\|_{1,\infty}e^{-\lambda t}+\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\bigr|\bigr).\end{split}

Next,
we use the Sobolev
bound

\begin{split}\|Q(0,\cdot)\|_{2}\leq\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigl|Q(0,x)-Q(0,y)\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\biggr)^{1/2}\leq C\|q(0,\cdot)\|_{1},\end{split}

where we used the
equality
\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q(0,x)\mathrm{d}x=0

together with the
obvious identity

Q(0,x)-Q(0,y)=\int_{y}^{x}q(0,z)\mathrm{d}z
.
By the same duality argument as in the proof of Proposition

3.12
,
we observe that

(4.19)

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\langle w(s,\cdot),q(s,\cdot)\rangle=0,\end{split}

which implies that

\langle w(0,\cdot),q(0,\cdot)\rangle=\langle\xi,q(t,\cdot)\rangle
.
We therefore deduce that

\bigl|\langle w(0,\cdot),q(0,\cdot)\rangle\bigr|\leq C\|q(0,\cdot)\|_{1}\bigl(\|\xi\|_{1,\infty}e^{-\lambda t}+\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\bigr|\bigr).

By choosing
q(0,x)=q^{n}(x)-1
, where
(q^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a standard mollifier of the Dirac
mass at some point
x_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
, and letting
n
tend to
\infty
and then taking the supremum over
x_{0}
, we obtain

(4.20)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\biggl|w(0,x)-\int_{\mathbb{T}}w(0,y)\mathrm{d}y\biggr|\leq C\bigl(\|\xi\|_{1,\infty}e^{-\lambda t}+\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\bigr|\bigr).

The above bound does not exactly fit (
4.14
).
The first point to recover (
4.14
) is to replace

\|\xi\|_{1,\infty}
by
\|\xi\|_{\infty}
.
We apply
(
4.20
),
but on the interval
[0,t-1]
and with
\xi=w(t-1,\cdot)
itself.
For a new value of
C
,

(4.21)

\begin{split}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\biggl|w(0,x)-\int_{\mathbb{T}}w(0,y)\mathrm{d}y\biggr|&\leq C\Bigl(\|w(t-1,\cdot)\|_{1,\infty}e^{-\lambda t}+\bigl|\bigl\langle w(t-1,\cdot),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\bigr|\Bigr)\leq C\Bigl(\|\xi\|_{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}+\bigl|\bigl\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\bigr|\Bigr),\end{split}

where we used the fact that
\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle w(t-1,\cdot),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle
,
which follows
from
(
4.4
)
and then from the same duality argument as in
(
4.19
).
In the above, we also used the bound

\|w(t-1,\cdot)\|_{1,\infty}\leq C\|\xi\|_{\infty}
, which follows from
the first step.

Third Step.

By Lemma
3.11
,

w_{1}
in
(
4.18
) satisfies all the required bounds (in long time).
In particular,
w_{2}=w-w_{1}

satisfies
(
4.21
).
By interior estimates for
the second equation in (
4.18
),
we obtain
(
4.15
).
∎

The following proposition is one key step in our proof.

Proposition 4.9
.

For fixed
\kappa>1
and
\eta\in(0,1)
,
the drift (
4.1
)
satisfies

(
Erg
)
up to the change that, in

(
Erg
-(
\alpha
,
\beta
))
,

\mu
is taken in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}

and that

1.

(
3.3
)
holds when
t\leq 1
;

2.

when
t\geq 1
,
the following variant of
(
3.3
)
holds true:

(4.22)

\bigl\|q(t)-q_{\infty}p^{\prime}(t\,;\mu)\bigr\|_{(k-{\alpha})^{\prime},\infty}\leq C_{k}e^{-\lambda t}\bigl[\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+K\bigr],\ \textrm{\rm with}\ K:=\sup_{t\geq 0}\bigl[\|r(t)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\exp(\lambda_{0}t)\bigr],

under the additional assumption that

\|r(t)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}
decays exponentially fast as
t
tends to
+\infty
(at a rate
\lambda_{0}
,
on which
C_{k}
and

\lambda
may depend)
and
where

q_{\infty}
is a real number depending on the input of the Cauchy problem

Linear
-[
\mu
,
q_{0}
,
r
]
(see (
3.2
)), but independent of
t
, and
is bounded by

C(\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+K)
, for
C
only depending
on
k
,
\eta
and
\kappa
.

Of course, the bound (
3.3
), which
holds for
t\leq 1
,
implies the bound (
4.22
)
for
t\leq 1
.

The result for
t\leq 1
is a direct consequence of
(
Local
)
.
We focus on the case
t>1
.

First Step.

We first choose
\mu=p_{\psi}
for some
\psi\in{\mathbb{T}}
.
We start from (
3.2
) (with the same solution
q
), but
instead of considering

w
as the solution of
(
3.34
), we choose
w
as the solution of
(
4.13
). Following the proof of Proposition

3.12
,
this leads to a new expansion in
(
3.35
):
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are the same, but
T_{3}

is zero.
Following
(
3.36
) and (
3.38
),
we deduce from
(
4.14
)
and
(
4.15
)
that, for
\alpha,\beta\in[0,2)
and
k\in[\alpha,2)
,

(4.23)

\|q(t)\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq\frac{C}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}}\mathrm{d}s,\quad t>0.

Notice that there is no exponential decay at this stage, due to to fact that we have not assumed yet
that
\xi
in
(
3.34
)
satisfies

\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle=0

(which term appears in (
4.14
)
and
(
4.15
)).
However,
since
\|r(t)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}
decays exponentially fast, this says that

\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C(\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+K)
, for
t\geq 1
.

We now assume that
\xi
in
(
4.13
)
satisfies

\langle\xi,p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle=0
.
With the same duality argument, (
4.23
) becomes

(4.24)

\Bigl\|q(t)-\frac{\langle q(t),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle}{\langle p_{\psi}^{\prime},p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\Bigr\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq\frac{C}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda t}+C\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\mathrm{d}s.

Then, for a given
t_{0}\geq 1
and for
t\geq t_{0}
, we expand
q(t)
as
q(t)=q_{1}(t)+q_{2}(t)
, with
q_{1}

solving

Linear
-[
\psi
,
q_{0}
,
0
]

on
[t_{0},+\infty)
, i.e.
q_{1}(t_{0})=q_{0}
,
and
q_{2}
solving

Linear
-[
\psi
,
0
,
r
]
on
[t_{0},+\infty)
, i.e.
q_{2}(t_{0})=0
.
Applying
(
4.23
)
at time
t_{0}
instead of
0
and using the exponential decay of

\|r(t)\|_{(\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}
, we get

\|q_{2}(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq CK\exp(-\lambda_{0}t_{0})
, for
t\geq t_{0}
.
Next, we
prove that

\langle q_{1}(t),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle/\langle p_{\psi}^{\prime},p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle

converges exponentially fast to a constant
q_{\infty}
.
We write

\langle q_{1}(t),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle=-\langle Q_{1}(t,\cdot),p_{\psi}^{\prime\prime}\rangle
, with
Q_{1}
being the primitive of
q_{1}
(in space) with a zero mean. Invoking Proposition

4.6
,

\langle Q_{1}(t,\cdot),p_{\psi}^{\prime\prime}\rangle
converges exponentially fast to some
constant.
The rate of convergence, as given by
(
4.6
),
depends on

\|Q_{1}(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{2}\leq C\|q(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
, from which we deduce that, for some
q_{\infty}
,

\bigl|\langle q_{1}(t),p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle{-q_{\infty}}\bigr|\leq C\|q(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}e^{-\lambda(t-t_{0})}.

Adding the bound for
q_{2}(t)
, we get, for any
t\in[2t_{0}-1,2t_{0}+1]
,

|\langle{q(t)},p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle{-q_{\infty}}|\leq C(\|q(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}+K)e^{-\lambda(t-t_{0})}
,
for

\lambda
depending on
\lambda_{0}
.
Equivalently, we can first fix
t>3
and then choose
t_{0}\in[(t-1)/2,(t+1)/2]
. We obtain

(4.25)

\bigl|\langle{q(t)},p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle{-q_{\infty}}\bigr|\leq C\bigl(\|q(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}+K\bigr)e^{-\lambda t/2},

for

t_{0}\in[(t-1)/2,(t+1)/2]
.
Choosing
t=t_{0}
and
k=\alpha
in
(
4.23
), we can
replace
\|q(t_{0},\cdot)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
by

\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}

in
(
4.25
).
Back to
(
4.24
)
(and replacing

q_{\infty}
by

q_{\infty}\langle p_{\psi}^{\prime},p_{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle
), we obtain, for
t\geq 1
,

(4.26)

\bigl\|q(t)-q_{\infty}p_{\psi}^{\prime}\bigr\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C\bigl(\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+K\bigr)e^{-\lambda t/2}.

Since
p(t\,;\mu)=p_{\psi}
, this is the result for
t\geq 1
.
The bound for
q_{\infty}
follows by letting
t

tend to
+\infty
in
(
4.23
).

Second Step.

When

\mu
is taken in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
, we know from
Proposition

4.1

that
p(t):=p(t\,;\mu)
converges exponentially fast to

p_{\psi}
for some
\psi\in{\mathbb{T}}
.
This allows us to repeat the third step of the proof of Proposition

3.14
, see in particular
(
3.51
). Here, the point is to
rewrite
(
3.2
)
as

\partial_{t}q(t)-L_{{\psi}}q(t)-(r(t)+r_{2}(t))=0
, for
t\geq 0
,

r_{2}(t)=[L_{m(t;\mu)}-L_{\psi}]q(t)
.
Following
(
3.53
), we obtain

\|r_{2}(t)\|_{(1,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\exp(-\lambda t)
.
By
(
4.23
),
but replacing

r
by
r+r_{2}
(in order to force the equation to be driven by
L_{\psi}
), we have, for
t>0
,

(4.27)

\bigl\|q(t)\bigr\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq\frac{C\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge t^{\alpha/2}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}}\,\mathrm{d}s+C\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t>0.

Fix
t_{0}>0
. By
(
Local
)
, we get a bound for
\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty^{\prime})}
for
s\in(0,t_{0}]
. Inserting this bound in
the above display, we get

(4.28)

\bigl\|q(t)\bigr\|_{(k-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C_{t_{0}}\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+C_{t_{0}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|r(s)\|_{({\beta},\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{{\beta}/2}}\mathrm{d}s+C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}}{1\wedge(t-s)^{1/2}}e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\geq t_{0}.

Choosing
k=\alpha
, we get

\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\bigl[\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\bigr]\leq C_{t_{0}}\|q_{0}\|_{(k,\infty)^{\prime}}+C_{t_{0}}K+Ce^{-\lambda t_{0}}\sup_{s\geq t_{0}}\bigl[\|q(s)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\bigr].

For
t_{0}
large enough, we obtain a bound for the left-hand side.
In
(
4.26
),
we replace

r
by
r+r_{2}
.
Recalling that

\|r_{2}(t)\|_{(1,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C\|q(t)\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\exp(-\lambda t)

and using the above bound, we complete the proof.
∎

4.3
Estimates of the tangent processes

We estimate the processes

m^{(1)}
,

m^{(2)}
,

d^{(1)}

and
d^{(2)}

in
Propositions

3.3
,

3.4

and

3.5

respectively. Since the model is one-dimensional, we may remove the indices
i
and
(i,j)

in
d^{(1)}
and
d^{(2)}
respectively.

Proposition 4.10
.

For any

\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\beta\in[1,2)

and

\eta\in(0,1)
,
there exist two positive constants
\lambda
and
C
, only depending on
\kappa
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\eta
, such that, for any
\mu
in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
,

\nu
,
\nu_{1}

and
\nu_{2}
in

{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})

and

z
,
z_{1}
and

z_{2}
in
{\mathbb{T}}
,
the following bound holds:

(4.29)

\bigl\|m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu)\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}+\bigl\|m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C,\qquad t\geq 0.

Moreover,
we can find two real numbers

q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z)
and
q_{\infty}^{(2)}(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})
such that

(4.30)

\begin{split}&\min(1,t^{\alpha/2})\bigl\|d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)\bigr\|_{(1-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}+\min(1,t^{\beta/2})\bigl\|d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\bigr\|_{(2-\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C,\\
&\min(1,t^{\alpha/2})\bigl\|\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)\bigr\|_{(1-\alpha,\infty)^{\prime}}+\min(1,t^{\beta/2})\bigl\|\tilde{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\bigr\|_{(2-\beta,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq Ce^{-\lambda t},\end{split}

for
t>0
, where

(4.31)

\begin{split}&\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)=d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)-q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z)p^{\prime}(t\,;\mu),\\
&\tilde{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})=d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})-q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z_{2})\partial_{x}d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})-q_{\infty}^{(2)}(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})p^{\prime}(t\,;\mu).\end{split}

The reader may observe that the estimate for
d^{(2)}
in
(
4.30
) is not formulated as in the statement of Proposition

3.8
(basically, it is worse). In fact, we chose to give it in this form in order to make it consistent with the estimate of
\tilde{d}^{(2)}
in the second line of (
4.30
).

First Step.

We start with the proof of
(
4.30
).
Throughout the proof, we fix
\eta\in(0,1)
.
By Proposition
3.4
,
we know that, for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
and
z\in\mathbb{T}
,

d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)
solves the linearised equation

(4.32)

\partial_{t}d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)-L_{m(t;\mu)}d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)=0,

with
d^{(1)}(0\,;\mu,z)=D_{z}^{\prime}=-\partial_{x}(\delta_{z})
and
m(0\,;\mu)=\mu
. Then,
by
item (2) in Proposition
4.9
, with
k=1
,
we get the two bounds for

d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)
and

\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)
, with
q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z)
being given by
Proposition
4.9
.

As for
d^{(2)}
, things are more complicated.
By Proposition
3.5
, we indeed know that, for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}

and any
z_{1},z_{2}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})
solves the equation

(4.33)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})-L_{m(t;\mu)}d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\\
&\hskip 5.0pt-\partial_{x}\Bigl(d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigl(J\star d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\bigr)+d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\bigl(J\star d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigr)\Bigr)=0,\end{split}

with
d^{(2)}(0\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})=0
.
The two bounds in finite time follow from
(
Local
)

(noticing that the estimate for
d^{(1)}
gives a bound
for
\partial_{x}d^{(1)}
in
(W^{2-\alpha,\infty}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))^{\prime}
, which is needed to estimate
\tilde{d}^{(2)}
, and that a similar estimate holds for
p^{\prime}(\cdot;\mu)
).
In order to apply
(2) in Proposition

4.9
and get bounds in long time, we let

r(t)=\partial_{x}\Bigl(d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigl(J\star d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\bigr)+d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\bigl(J\star d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigr)\Bigr).

However, we cannot prove that

\|r(t)\|_{(1,\infty)^{\prime}}
decays exponentially
fast, since
(
4.30
)
just provides an exponential bound for

\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{i})
, and not for
d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{i})
(with
i=1,2
).
Instead, we focus on

(4.34)

\bar{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2}):=d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})-q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z_{2})\partial_{x}d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),

for
q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z)
as in (
4.31
).
We easily see that

(\partial_{x}d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z))_{t\geq 0}
solves (in a weak sense) the equation

(4.35)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\partial_{x}d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)-L_{m(t\,;\cdot)}\partial_{x}d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\partial_{x}\Bigl(d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)\bigl(J\star m^{\prime}(t\,;\mu)\bigr)+m^{\prime}(t\,;\mu)\bigl(J\star d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z)\bigr)\Bigr)=0,\end{split}

in the space

\cap_{T>0}L^{\infty}([0,T],(W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})\cap_{T>1}L^{\infty}([1/T,T],(W^{2-2\alpha,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))^{\prime})
,
with
-\partial^{2}_{x}(\delta_{z})
as initial condition.
Choosing
z=z_{1}

in
(
4.35
),
multiplying
by

q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z_{2})

and then subtracting to
(
4.33
), we obtain

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\bar{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})-L_{m(t;\mu)}\bar{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})-r(t)=0,\end{split}

with

(4.36)

r(t)=\partial_{x}\Bigl(d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigl(J\star\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\bigr)+\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\bigl(J\star d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigr)\Bigr).

We first evaluate the
(W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{\prime}
norm of
r(t)
.
Using the fact that the convolution kernel
J
is odd, we get, for any
\xi\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
,

\begin{split}\langle r(t),\xi\rangle&=-\Bigl\langle d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigl(J\star\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\bigr)+\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\bigl(J\star d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigr),\xi^{\prime}\Bigr\rangle\\
&=\Bigl\langle\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2}),J\star\bigl(d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\xi^{\prime}\bigr)\Bigr\rangle-\Bigl\langle\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2}),\bigl(J\star d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1})\bigr)\xi^{\prime}\Bigr\rangle.\end{split}

Invoking (
4.30
) with
\alpha=1
(using in addition the smoothness of
J
), we then have

\|r(t)\|_{(1,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq Ce^{-\lambda t},\qquad t\geq 1,

where the values of
C
and
\lambda
are allowed to vary as long as they only depend on
\kappa
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\eta
.
By item (2) in Proposition

4.9
,
but initiated from
time
1/2
(recalling that we have a bound for

\|\bar{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\|_{(1,\infty)^{\prime}}
at
t=1/2
)
and with
k=1
,
we deduce
that there exists a constant
q_{\infty}^{(2)}(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})
such that

(4.37)

\bigl\|\tilde{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\bigr\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq Ce^{-\lambda t},\qquad t\geq 1,

with

\tilde{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})=\bar{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})-q_{\infty}^{(2)}(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})p^{\prime}(t\,;\mu)
.
This completes the proof of
(
4.30
).

Second Step.

We now turn to the proof
of
(
4.29
), which is quite similar to the first step.
By Proposition

3.3
, we know that, for any

\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
and
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})
,

(m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu))_{t\geq 0}
solves the same equation
(
4.32
)
but with
\nu-\mu
as initial condition.
Therefore,
we have the bound for

m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu)

in (
4.29
), using item (2) in
Proposition

4.9
, with
k=0
.
We have the same for

\tilde{m}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu)
, with an obvious definition for the latter.

We now treat
(m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}))_{t\geq 0}
. For
t\in[0,1]
, the bound follows from
(
Local
)
.
To address the case
t>1
, we write the analogue of
(
4.35
)
but for
(\partial_{x}m^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,\nu))_{t\geq 0}
and the analogue of
r
in
(
4.36
). We have
\|r(t)\|_{(1,\infty)^{\prime}}\leq C\exp(-\lambda t)
.
By (2) in Proposition
4.9
, with
k=1
,
we recover
(
4.37
) but
for

\tilde{m}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
(with an obvious definition for it).
We get a bound for

\|m^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\|_{(0,\infty)^{\prime}}
for
t\geq 1
.∎

In the rest of the subsection,
{\mathcal{U}}
denotes the same functional as in
(
2.4
). We start with:

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\Phi:{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be
a rotation-invariant function that satisfies
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
(see Definition
\ref {def:rotation:invariant:function})
. Then, for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})
and any

q\in(W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}))^{\prime}
with
\langle q,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0:

(4.38)

\begin{split}&\frac{\delta\Phi}{\delta m}(\mu)\bigl(\mu^{\prime}\bigr)=0,\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\frac{\delta^{2}\Phi}{\delta m^{2}}(\mu)\bigl(\mu^{\prime},q\bigr)+\frac{\delta\Phi}{\delta m}(\mu)\bigl(q^{\prime}\bigr)=0,\end{split}

where
\mu^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
are the derivatives, in the sense of distributions, of
\mu
and
q

(so that the above expressions are well-defined thanks to the regularity of
\Phi
).

We start from the very definition of rotation-invariant function. It says that, for any

\psi\in\mathbb{T}
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\psi}_{|\psi=0}\Phi\bigl(\mu\circ\tau_{\psi}^{-1}\bigr)=0.

The left-hand side writes in the form

-\langle[\delta\Phi/\delta m](\mu),\mu^{\prime}\rangle
. We deduce the first identity in the statement.

Assume now that
\mu
has a (strictly) positive continuous density
\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}x
and consider an element
q\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
such that

\langle q,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
. Then, for
\varepsilon
small enough,

\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}x+\varepsilon q
may be regarded as a density on
\mathbb{T}
. With a slight abuse of notation, we
write
\mu+\varepsilon q\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})
. Replacing
\mu
by
\mu+\varepsilon q
in the first identity in the statement and then taking the derivative with respect to
\varepsilon
at
\varepsilon=0
, we deduce that
the second identity in the statement holds at any pair
(\mu,q)
satisfying the prescribed conditions. Using the
density of
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
in
(W^{1,\infty})^{\prime}(\mathbb{T})

together with the fact that
\Phi
satisfies
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
, the second identity also holds at any pair
(\mu,q)
, with
\mu
as before and

q\in(W^{1,\infty})^{\prime}(\mathbb{T})
such that
\langle q,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0
.
Approximating (for the
1
-Wasserstein topology) any
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})
by probability measures with
a positive density and then invoking again the regularity properties of

\Phi
, we finally obtain the second identity in full generality.
∎

We deduce the following important proposition.

Proposition 4.12
.

Assume that

\Phi
satisfies

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
.
Then, for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\eta\in(0,1)
, there exist two positive constants
\lambda
and
C
, with
\lambda
only depending on
\kappa
,
\alpha
and
\eta
, and
C
only depending on
\kappa
,
\alpha
,
\eta
and
the bounds in

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\alpha,2}
))
, such that,
for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
,
any
t>0
and
any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{T}
,

(4.39)

\displaystyle\Bigl|\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})\Big|\leq C,\quad t\geq 0,

(4.40)

\displaystyle\min(1,t^{1-\alpha/4})\Bigl|\partial_{z_{2}}\partial_{z_{1}}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})\Big|\leq Ce^{-\lambda t},\quad t>0.

First Step.

We start with the proof of
(
4.39
).
It is a mere consequence of
the representation formula in
Proposition
3.3

and the bound
(
4.29
)
in the statement of Proposition

4.10
.

Second Step.

We turn to the proof of
(
4.40
).
We recall the following formula from Proposition
3.6
:

\begin{split}\partial_{z_{2}}\partial_{z_{1}}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})&=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big)+\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\Big),\end{split}

for
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})
,
t\geq 0
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{T}
.
Using the same notation as in
Proposition
4.10
, we have

\begin{split}&\partial_{z_{2}}\partial_{z_{1}}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big)\\
&\hskip 5.0pt+q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z_{2})\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(p^{\prime}(t\,;\mu),d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big)+\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\Big).\end{split}

With the same notation as in (
4.34
) and
(
4.35
) and thanks to the second identity in
(
4.38
), we get

\begin{split}\partial_{z_{2}}\partial_{z_{1}}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})&=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-q_{\infty}^{(1)}(\mu,z_{2})\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(\partial_{x}d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big)+\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(d^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\Big)\\
&=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big)+\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(\bar{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\Bigr).\end{split}

By the first identity in
(
4.38
), we can remove for free
q_{\infty}^{(2)}(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})p^{\prime}(t\,;\mu)
in the second term on the last line.
Using again the notation from the statement of Proposition

4.10
, we obtain

\begin{split}\partial_{z_{2}}\partial_{z_{1}}\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}{\delta m^{2}}(t,\mu)(z_{1},z_{2})&=\frac{\delta^{2}{\Phi}}{\delta m^{2}}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(\tilde{d}^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1}),d^{(1)}(t\,;\mu,z_{2})\Big)+\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta m}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu)\bigr)\Big(\tilde{d}^{(2)}(t\,;\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\Bigr).\end{split}

The end of the proof follows from
(
4.30
)
and

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\alpha,2}
))
(see
in particular, (
2.11
)).
∎

4.4
Semi-group generated by the empirical distribution

We now address the weak error, as in the statement of Theorem

4.3
. By the same regularisation argument as in
the conclusion of the proof of Proposition
3.10
,
we can assume that
\Phi
satisfies
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{4,3}
))
.
Following
the statement of Proposition

4.12
, we must then prove that the constant
C
that we obtain in the main inequality of
Theorem
4.3
only depends on
\Phi
through
the
bounds
in
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
, for a fixed value of
\gamma
as in the statement of
Theorem
4.3
.

Throughout the proof, we make use of the
notation introduced in Subsection
3.5
, letting:

\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\Phi\bigl(\mu_{t}^{N}\bigr)\,\big|\,\mu^{N}_{0}=\mu\bigr],\quad\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T}).

By the Markov property of

(\mu^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
we have

(4.41)

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\Phi\bigl({\mu}_{t}^{N}\bigr)|{\mathcal{F}}_{s}\bigr]=\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}\bigl(t-s,\mu^{N}_{s}\bigr).

We then proceed step by step.
The first step is dictated by the analysis of the strong error in
[
30
]

and consists of a preliminary form of the result up until time
\exp(N^{1/2})
, for

\mu
close enough to
{\mathcal{I}}=\{p_{\psi},\psi\in{\mathbb{T}}\}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

There exist
\delta>0
, only depending on
\kappa
,
and a constant
C\geq 0
, only depending on
\delta
,
\kappa

and the bounds for
\Phi
in

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
,
such that,
for any
N\geq 1

and
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})

with
\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta
,

\begin{split}\forall t\in\bigl[0,\exp(N^{1/2})\bigr],\quad\Bigl|\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)-{\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu)\Bigr|\leq\frac{C}{N},\end{split}

where
\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu,{\mathcal{I}})=\inf_{\psi\in\mathbb{T}}\|\mu-p_{\psi}\|_{-1,2}
.
Here, we may choose

\delta
such that

\{\mu:\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta\}\subset{\mathcal{Q}}_{1/2}
.

The proof relies on some auxiliary results obtained in
[
30
]
.

First Step.

We apply
[
30
, (98-99)]
. It says that there exist a time
T>0
, a real
\varepsilon>0
and a constant
C
, all independent of
N
and the initial conditions, such that, for any integer
n\geq 1
,

(4.42)

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq nT}\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu^{N}_{t},{\mathcal{I}})\leq\varepsilon\,\big|\,\bigl\|\mu^{N}_{0}-p_{\textrm{proj}(\mu^{N}_{0})}\bigr\|_{-1,2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\biggr)\geq\Bigl[1-\exp\bigl(-CN)\Bigr]^{n},\end{split}

where
\textrm{\rm proj}:{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
is the projection mapping defined in

[
56
, Lemma 2.8]

and

[
30
, Lemma 3.5]
(with the small difference
2
2

2

There is another difference. In

[
30
,
56
]
, the dual space is not
W^{-1,2}(\mathbb{T})
, but
H^{-1}_{0}=\{f\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{T}):\langle f,{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=0\}^{\prime}
. Of course, any element of
W^{-1,2}(\mathbb{T})
may be projected onto an element of
H^{-1}_{0}
by discarding its constant Fourier mode.
that the function
\mathbb{T}
is defined here as
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, whilst it is defined as
{\mathbb{R}}/(2\pi{\mathbb{Z}})
in
[
30
,
56
]
).
Notice that the conditioning in the left-hand side
is implicitly required
in
[
30
]
(see (84)). More importantly, there is no need to assume that
\mu_{0}^{N}

is the
N
-sample of a common distribution
and the result holds for an arbitrary initial condition
\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})
.

On
{\mathcal{I}}
, the projection
proj
reduces to the trivial mapping
\textrm{\rm proj}(p_{\psi})=\psi
,
\psi\in\mathbb{T}
.
Therefore, if some probability measure
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})
is close
to
{\mathcal{I}}
(for
\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}
), then it is close to some
p_{\psi}\in{\mathcal{I}}
and, by continuity of

proj
with respect to
W^{-1,2}(\mathbb{T})
,
\textrm{\rm proj}(\nu)
is close to
\psi
. In the end,

\nu
is close to
p_{\textrm{\rm proj}(\nu)}
.
This continuity result, combined with a standard compactness argument, may be formulated as follows. For the same
\varepsilon
as above, we can find some
\delta_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\nu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta_{\varepsilon}
implies

\|\nu-p_{\textrm{\rm proj}(\nu)}\|_{-1,2}\leq\varepsilon/2
.
Hence,
taking
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})
with

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta_{\varepsilon}
, we get

{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq nT}\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu^{N}_{t},{\mathcal{I}})\leq\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\biggr)\geq\Bigl[1-\exp\bigl(-CN)\Bigr]^{n}.

Following

[
30
, (100)]
, we deduce that, for
n=\exp(N^{1/2})
and for a new value of
C
,

{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq nT}\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu^{N}_{t},{\mathcal{I}})\leq\varepsilon\,\Big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\biggr)\geq 1-\frac{C}{N}.

With
\tau_{N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu^{N}_{t},{\mathcal{I}})\geq\varepsilon\},

this may be rewritten in the form

(4.43)

{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\tau_{N}\leq\exp(N^{1/2})\,\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr)\leq\frac{C}{N}.

Second Step.
Following
(
2.6
), we
have the following expansion,
which holds for any
t\geq 0
,

(4.44)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\Phi\bigl(\mu^{N}_{t}\bigr)|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\bigr]-{\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\Phi\bigl(\mu^{N}_{t}\bigr)|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\bigr]-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[{\mathcal{U}}\bigl(t-t\wedge\tau_{N},\mu^{N}_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}\bigr)|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\bigr]+{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}\bigl(t-t\wedge\tau_{N},\mu^{N}_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}\bigr)-{\mathcal{U}}\bigl(t,\mu^{N}_{0}\bigr)|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr].

If we restrict ourselves to
t\in[0,\exp(N^{1/2})]
, then
(
4.43
)
says that the first difference in the right-hand side is bounded by
C/N
, by recalling that
\Phi
and thus
{\mathcal{U}}
are bounded
and allowing
C
to depend on
\|\Phi\|_{\infty}
.

As for the second term in the right-hand side of (
4.44
), we may follow
(
2.7
),
by expanding the
term

({\mathcal{U}}(t-s,\mu^{N}_{s}))_{s\geq 0}
up until
time
t\wedge\tau_{N}
.
Using the fact that, up until time
\tau_{N}
,
the
\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}
-distance between

\mu^{N}
and
{\mathcal{I}}

remains less than
\varepsilon
and
assuming w.l.o.g. that

\varepsilon
is
small enough so that

\{\mu:\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\varepsilon\}\subset{\mathcal{Q}}_{1/2}
, we get from
Proposition
4.12
that
this term is also bounded by

C/N
, with
C
now depending on
\kappa
and
the bounds for
\Phi

in
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
.
∎

The result is extended to initial conditions

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})\cap{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
, for any
\eta>0
, on a smaller time scale.

Proposition 4.14
.

For any
\eta\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C
, only depending on
\eta
,
\kappa
and
the bounds for
\Phi

in
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
, such that, for any
N\geq 1
and

\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})
,

(4.45)

\forall t\in[0,\exp(N^{1/4})],\quad\bigl|\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)-{\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu)\bigr|\leq\frac{C}{N}.

First step.

Lemma

4.13
says that, for
\delta
as therein, the result holds up until
time
\exp(N^{1/2})
if

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta
.
Then, for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})
,
Proposition
4.1
says that there exists a (fixed hence independent of
N
) time
T
, only depending on
\delta
,
\eta
and
\kappa
, such that

(4.46)

\forall t\geq T,\quad\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}\bigl(m(t\,;\mu),{\mathcal{I}}\bigr)\leq\frac{\delta}{2}.

Next, we claim that (the proof is given in the third step below)

(4.47)

{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\bigl\|m(T\,;\mu)-\mu^{N}_{T}\bigr\|_{-1,2}>\frac{\delta}{2}\,\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr)\leq\frac{C}{N},

where
C

only depends on
\eta
,
\kappa
and
the bounds for
\Phi

in
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
, from which we deduce that

(4.48)

{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}\bigl(\mu^{N}_{T},{\mathcal{I}}\bigr)>\delta\,\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr)\leq\frac{C}{N}.

By
(
4.41
), we have, for
t\geq T
,

\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}\bigl(t-T,\mu^{N}_{T}\bigr)\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\bigr],

when
\mu^{N}_{0}=\mu
.
Hence,
by
(
4.45
)
(for

\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta
)
and
(
4.48
),
we get,
for

t\in[T,\exp(N^{1/2})/2]
,

\begin{split}&\Bigl|\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)-{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{U}}\bigl(t-T,\mu^{N}_{T}\bigr){\mathbf{1}}_{\{\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu^{N}_{T},{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta\}}\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr]\Bigr|\\
&\leq C{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1}}\bigl(\mu^{N}_{T},{\mathcal{I}}\bigr)>\delta\,\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr)+{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\Bigl|{\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}}\bigl(t-T,\mu^{N}_{T}\bigr)-{{\mathcal{U}}}\bigl(t-T,\mu^{N}_{T}\bigr)\Bigr|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\mu^{N}_{T},{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta\}}\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr]\\
&\leq\frac{C}{N},\end{split}

where we used the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}
is bounded by
\|\Phi\|_{\infty}
.
On the first line,
we can replace the indicator function
(inside the expectation)
by
\Xi(\mu_{T}^{N})
for some function
\Xi:{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})\rightarrow[0,1]
that is equal to
1
on the ball

\{\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}):\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\nu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta\}
.
By
(
4.48
) again, we deduce that, for

\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})
,

(4.49)

\Bigl|\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[{\mathcal{U}}\bigl(t-T,\mu^{N}_{T}\bigr)\Xi(\mu^{N}_{T})|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\bigr]\Bigr|\leq\frac{C}{N}.

Second Step.

We complete the proof of
(
4.45
), recalling that the latter is already known to hold
on
[0,T]
(since there is no need for any ergodic estimates in finite time). In order to proceed, assume for a while that we are given a function
\Xi:{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}})\rightarrow[0,1]
such that

1.

\Xi
matches 1 on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1/2}
(the latter containing

\{\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}):\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\nu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta\}
) and
0
outside

{\mathcal{Q}}_{3/4}
;

2.

\Xi
is a smooth functional of
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T})
.

Then, since
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1/2}
contains

\{\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}):\textrm{\rm dist}_{\|\cdot\|_{-1,2}}(\nu,{\mathcal{I}})\leq\delta\}
, we
can apply
(
4.49
).
Since
\Xi
is zero outside

{\mathcal{Q}}_{3/4}
,
we know from Proposition

4.12
that
{\mathcal{U}}(t-T,\cdot)\Xi(\cdot)

satisfies

(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
with explicit bounds that
are uniform
with respect to
t\geq T
.
We then apply
the finite-horizon version of
(
4.45
)
to
\Phi(\cdot)={\mathcal{U}}(t-T,\cdot)\Xi(\cdot)
.
By
(
4.49
), we get, for any
t\geq T

and

\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})
,

\Bigl|\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)-{\mathcal{U}}\bigl(t-T,m(T\,;\mu)\bigr)\Xi\bigl(m(T\,;\mu)\bigr)\Bigr|\leq\frac{C}{N},

for
C
only depending
on

\eta
,
\kappa
and
the bounds for
\Phi

in
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
.
Noticing that

{\mathcal{U}}(t-T,m(T\,;\mu))={\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu)

and that
\Xi(m(T\,;\mu))=1
for our choice of
T
(see
(
4.46
)), we
get the announced result.

The function
\Xi
is constructed as in Proposition

4.4
. We consider a smooth non-decreasing cut-off function
\varphi:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
that is equal to
1
on

[0,1/2]
and to
0
on
[3/4,1]
.
We then let
\Xi(\mu)=\varphi(|\mu^{1}|)
.

Third Step.

We now prove
(
4.47
).
It is again a consequence of the finite time horizon of
(
4.45
),
but with a difference of choosing (temporarily)
the functional
\Phi
in the definition of
{\mathcal{U}}
as in
Proposition

2.3
with

(d+\alpha)/2=1
and
\nu_{0}=\mu_{T}
therein.
The result then follows from Markov’s inequality.
∎

Our last step is to extend the previous result to times greater than
\exp(N^{1/4})
. The key idea is that, in long time, the empirical measure necessarily visits the
set
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}
quite often, for any
\eta\in(0,1)
.

Proposition 4.15
.

For any
\eta\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C
, only depending on
\eta
,
\kappa
and the bounds for
\Phi

in
(
Reg
-
\Phi
-(
{\gamma,2}
))
, such that, for any
N\geq 1
and

\mu\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\eta}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathbb{T})
,

(4.50)

\forall t\geq 0,\quad\bigl|\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)-{\mathcal{U}}(t,\mu)\bigr|\leq\frac{C}{N}.

By Proposition
4.14
, it suffices to prove (
4.50
) for
t\geq\exp(N^{1/4})
.
For
T_{N}=\exp(N^{1/4})/2
, we call

\tau_{N}:=\inf\biggl\{s>0:\biggl|\int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{-i2\pi\theta}\mu^{N}_{s+t-T_{N}}(\mathrm{d}\theta)\biggr|\geq\eta\biggr\}.

We prove in Lemma
4.16
that

{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\tau_{N}\geq N^{1/4}\bigr)\leq C/{N}
,
for
C
as in the statement and for
\eta
small enough.
By the strong version of the Markov property
(
4.41
) (noticing that
N^{1/4}\leq\exp(N^{1/4})/4
for
N
large enough),

\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)={\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}\Bigl(T_{N}-T_{N}\wedge\tau_{N},\mu^{N}_{t-T_{N}+T_{N}\wedge\tau_{N}}\Bigr)\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr],

and then, assuming without any loss of generality that
\eta
is small enough,

\begin{split}&\Bigl|\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}(t,\mu)-{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\overline{\mathcal{U}}^{N}\Bigl(T_{N}-T_{N}\wedge\tau_{N},\mu^{N}_{t-T_{N}+T_{N}\wedge\tau_{N}}\Bigr){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{\tau_{N}<T_{N}/2\}}\big|\,\mu_{0}^{N}=\mu\Bigr]\Bigr|\leq\frac{C}{N}.\end{split}
